This report submitted by Mrs. Lawrence Davis, Chm., Mr. After some depth study, the committee re- commended the following changes to the Executive Committee which were approved.
The changes read: (1) If because of health or other factors it seems to the Association wise to terminate the relationship, 90 days shall be given for the superintendent of missions to find other work. (2) He shall not accept any interim pastorate. Limited supply preaching may be done only by invitation of a church without a pastor for not more than two (2) consecutive Sundays or two (2) services in a month, and in real emergencies as approved by the chairman of the Missions -Advisory Committee. (3) The worker agrees to limit revival engagements outside the association to two (2) weeks per year and to four(4)weeks total per year. (4) NEW ITEMS: (1) In cooperation with the Missions -Advisory Committee, tiie Superintendent of Missions will seek out places of mission needs and seek to enlist an appropriate church to meet these needs. (2) In the event no church or churches can be enlisted to meet these needs, upon the instruction of the Missions -Advisory Committee, the Superintendent of Missions may lead in the program to meet the need in the name of the association.
(3) The association or the Missions -Advisory Committee may add other mission duties and responsibilities.

In the July meeting, since the secretary, Doug Roebuck, had left the Association it was necessary to elect another secretary and Clarence Bishop was elected to fill the unexpired term.

During the pastyears some of the churches in Flat River Association and some from other Associations have participated in a limited ministry at Butner, under the leader- ship of our Superintendent of Missions. This has proved to be a very rewarding ministry the to patients as well as to the participants. Now our Superintendent of Missions along with some fourteen other as sociational Superintendents of Missions are planning a two-day tour visit in our Burner area, ^ -^ ' Respectfully submitted, " Mrs. Rowena Frazier, Chm.

REPORT OF WOMAN'S MISSIONARY UNION Report 4 Woman's Missionary Union work has been carried on in the Flat River Baptist Association for many years, seeking to involve, inspire, and inform people of their missions obligations. We are Blessed in our association to have dedicated women who are willing to serve and proinote mission work. This has been a good year in WMU and I shall try to tell of some of the activities.

Mrs. Ray Knott, associational Baptist Women Director, assisted in re -organizing a Baptist Women organization at the Creedmoor First Baptist Church during the past year.

A Missions Study Institute was held at the Tabbs Creek Baptist Church in February, but due to inclement weather, attendance was small. Misses Kathryn Bullard and Bemice Popham from the State Baptist Convention were the teachers for the Institute.

In observance of WMU Focus Week this past year. Morning Devotions over radio station WOXF were led by some of the associational WMU officers.

The associational Baptist Young Women held a banquet during WMU Focus Week at the Hester Baptist Church \A/ith approximately seventy in attendance. This was under the direction of Mrs. Willis Hoyle, associational Baptist Young Women Director.

Approximately three hundred Acteen members and leaders attended the asso ciational Acteens Rally in May at the Mt, Zion Baptist Church. This was directed by Mrs. Peggy Yancey, associational Acteens Director.

The associational Woman's Missionary Union Executive Council meets quarterly to plan, coordinate, and evaluate the work of our churches. Several of our asso- ciational WMU Directors attended the state workshop at Mundo Vista in August to enable us to better serve in our respective roles.

We had approximately 125 in attendance at the two -night Leadership Conference in September at the Delrayno Baptist Church, The Acteens and Girls In Action work at the Oxford Orphanage has been tem- porarily discontinued for the coming year. This is due to existing schedule changes at the Orphanage.

itie highlight of our past WMU year was the Annual Spring Meeting in March at the Hester Baptist Church. We were most fortunate to have Miss Alma Hunt, Ex- ecutive Secretary of Woman's Missionary Union, SBC, and the Rev. Ray Frye, mis- sionary to Malaysia, as our guest speakers. Each of these speakers brought a most challenging message. It was a pleasure to have one of Mr. Frye's Malaysian friends visit us for this meeting.

We learned new techniques of promotion and helping churches to start Baptist Men and Royal Ambassador work.

This past year in the field of leadership services rendered to our churches, the Brotherhood Department has worked with several churches in looking at and studying ftie possibilities, of beginning Royal Ambassador work in their churches. George Gresham REPCRT OF STEWARDSHIP AND COOPERATIVE PROGRAM 1971 Report 6 In a day when many major denomination are cutting back their missions programs we are pleased to report that Southern Baptists continue to make large advances. Co- operative Program receipts at the Southern Baptist Convention level are well ahead of a year ago and appears now to be a record year. God has blessed us and "To God Be The Glory." We are indeed grateful for the fine leadership and wonderful commitment of so many people in the churches of Flat River Baptist Association.

During the year 1970-1971 $49,033 were given by 32 churches of the Flat River Baptist Association through the Cooperative Program to all mission causes we hold dear.

••COMMITTED TO WORLD MISSIONS" is the Cooperative Program theme for 1971-1972 and to help the churches of our association grow in support of Cooperative Program giving and work we recommend: I. That our churches keep in mind the inflationary trends of our economy and plan to increase their contributions to missions. Thateach church establish some definite dollar or percentage, preferrably percentage goals tona/ard which to work during the next several years. To implement a program of percentage increase we suggest: 1 - For churches giving less than 20% advance 2% each year up to 20% 2 - For churches giving 20% but under 30%level, advance 1% each year to 30% 3 - For churches reaching 30% level, advance 1/2% each year until the objective established by the church is reached.
II. That every church observe Cooperative Program Month - October.
ll{ That Every Church Observe Cooperative Program Day on the third Sunday in April.
IV. That every church use a stewardship program tohelp develop and subscribe a worthy budget for your own church. Such stewardship programs materials are available through your associational chairman of stewardship and cooperative program. Contact this agent well in advance of your budget promotion for any assistance you may desire.
V. That every Church plan to have elected personnel attend the associational Stewardship Workshop on May 9, 1972. This workshop has proved to be a most helpful instrument in promoting local church finances and Cooperative Program giving.

Respectfully submitted.
Earl W. Howard, Chairman STATE MISSIONS REPORT Report 7 State Missions in North Carolina can be divided into two major categories: (1) ministries that Strengtfien and (2) ministries that LENGTHEN the witness of the local church.

STRENGTHENING ministries involve the various church program organizations such as Sunday School, Church Training, WMU, Brotherhood, and others. These pro- grams enable the local church to realize its mission potential. State Missions provide resources and personnel that assist in the development of these ministries.
Thus, State Missions, help the local church become a "mission station." The second type of State Missions are those ministries that LENGTHEN the witness of the local church. These efforts represent local concern and require the support of the local congregation. These ministries include Urban Missions, Interracial Co- operation, Resort Ministries, Language Ministries, Chaplaincy, Seminary Extension, Ministry to the Deaf, Literacy Work, Migrant Missions, Mountain Missions, Week-day .
Ministries, and Youth and Family Service.

Many of these ministries are co -sponsored with the Southern Baptist Home Mission Board as well as other resources. All total. State Missions involves over fifty different ministries across North Carolina. These programs serve as a vital link in the total World Mission responsibility of us as North Carolina Baptists.

Respectfully submitted, M. Wayne Oakes REPORT OF THE HOME MISSION BOARD Report 8 The Home Mission Board has tried to strengthen and expand their efforts to assist churches and evangelism.

At the close of 1970, there were 2,222 home missionaries which is 13 less than 1969. The reduction was caused by the rising cost of living and a slow down of giving. This means a lot of the mission opportunities could not be taken, especially in the newer areas of the convention. A training plan was put into operating for lay evangelism, including materials, spiritual preparation and training which prepares laymen in sharing their faith. The division also has projected plans for use of television in the presentation of the Christian faith and the addition of a staff member for youth evangelism in high schools. There are 942 Southern Baptist ministers serving as chaplains in the military, hospitals and institutions. There are 617 on active duty with the military and 325 in other positions. These ministers reported 11,086 professions offaith and 850 units of work started such as new worship services, Bible classes or Sunday School.

Our summer ministry has expanded. Summer students mission personnel reached a new high of 940, sponsored by the Home Mission Board.

Missionaries reported people saved and led to use the Word of God to witness because of the distribution of scriptures. Hundreds of scriptures were sent free through the plan with the American Bible Society and volunteer donations.

This past year we have had working in the Flat River Association from the Home Mission Board, Mr. Roy Owens of the Division of Associational Service of Relationship service, and Mr. Lowell Sodeman of Industry Chaplaincy Department.

We had the first Industrial and Ministers dinner conference in North Carolina here in the association.

We also had Mr. Richard Mckay of Hospital Chaplaincy Department visit the institutions in the Butner area. , ■; ; , . . . .' ; . Respectfully submitted, . , . , Mrs, Archie Denny \'..l FOREIGN MISSIONS REPORT Report 9 MISSIONARY SUPPORT The main purpose of the Foreign Mission Board is the sending out and maintaining of missionaries in Southern Baptist overseas missions.
The Board had an overseas missionary staff of 2,501 at the close of 1970. This in- cluded 165 missionary associates, 4 special project medical personnel, and 121 missionary journeyman. Appointments (during the year totaled 182 (92 career, 18 associates, 64 journeymen, 1 special project medical couple, and 6 career reappoint- ments). Between January and May, 1971, 35 more were added.

But the need for reinforcement personnel is evidenced by the fact that hundreds of calls for Missions (Organizations of Southern Baptist missionaries) and National Conventions in dozens of countries have come to our Foreign Mission Board.

On May 1, 1971, Southern Baptist missionaries were under appointment to 76 geographical or political entities. New fields added in 1970 were Laos, Mozambique, Surinam, and Upper Volta. A couple was assigned to Barbados on an exploratory basis.

EVANGELISM AND CHURCH DEVELOPMENT The ultimate purpose of all our missionary endeavors is to lead people to new life in Jesus Christ and the cultivation of New Testament churches. We now have 5,853 organized churches on mission fields with which Southern Baptist missionaries are related. Seventy -five per cent of these are self-supporting. Work and accomplishments reported are largely those of national churches, leaders, and conventions, with the assistance of missionary co-workers and resources from Southern Baptist.

Many types of ministry are utilized in the programs of evangelism and church development. These include radio and television, English -language churches, lay- leadership training, stewardship promotion, camps and conferences, work with women, men and youth? music and religious education.

During the year, large-scale evangelistic efforts centered in Asia (in relation to the meetings of the Baptist World Congress in Tokyo) and in Central and East Africa.
Other areas carried on less extensive projects.

Tile number of Baptist radio and television programs increased, and special communications workshops were held in several areas during the year.

SCHOOLS AND STUDENT WORK The training of national leaders is an absolute necessity if strong, self supporting churches are to be established. Realizing this to be true the result has been the establishment of Educational institutions in various lands including all levels from kindergarten through university. Of special importance is the development of pastors and other church leaders through theological schools and in programs below the full seminary level.

PUBLICATION WORK Publication ministries are a vital part of Southern Baptist overseas missions, as all kinds of Christian literature are provided for purposes of evangelism and the development of vigorous church life. Our missionaries are re- lated to publishing centers in 24 lands and to informal literature production programs in a number of other countires. They assisted in producing over 27 million pieces of literature: tracts, books, periodicals, Bibles, hymnals, and other items.

Tlie most remarkable expansionof publishing efforts took place in the development and outreach of correspondence courses. Enrolment in the Bible Way Correspondence School in Zambia reached 15,000 by the end of 1970. Correspondence courses also enrolled 2,000 persons in India and 3,500 in Pakistan.

HOSPITAL AND MEDICAL CARE The value of this ministry in opening doors for the Christian gospel to be shared is unlimited. During 1970, missionaries and national workers engaged in ths ministry treated more than 51,000 inpatients and saw nearly 930,000 outpatients.

In spite of the fact that in this area of our work we have critical need for additional workers, the work is progressing and expanding.

As an example of what is being done clinics in India were seeing 3,000 patients a month, and those in the Dominican Republic were ministering to 10,000 people per month.

BENEVOLENT MINISTRIES During 1970 the Foreign Mission Board provided funds for emergency and recurring relief in several contries. Assistance was continued for Nigeria, to relieve suffering and the severe malnutrition problems caused by the recent civil war there. Vietnam was another area with continuing pressing needs for direct relief.

Relief was given in areas suffering from natural disaster - East Pakistan, Peru, the Philippines, and Brazil - where drought, typhoon, earthquake, or flood resulted in great human distress. In Brazil, following the floods, a small furniture factory was opened to provide replacement furniture and also employment opportunity.

Agricultural work was expanded through the development of a Baptist Rural Life Center in the Philippines, the securing of property for a demonstration farm in Pakistan, and local agricultural projects on several mission stations in Liberia.

Good will and community centers in 17 countries reached people in various ways: literacy classes, homemaking courses, Bible study clubs, recreational programs and evangelistic services. Our children's homes on mission lands cared for nearly 1,200 boys and girls last year.

CONTRIBUTIONS Approximately 50 per cent of Southern Baptist Convention Co- operative Program funds are made available annually for foreign missions. This is the basis upon which sustained advance can be built. Still needed urgently on over- seas fields are land, buildings, and equipment, amounting to several million dollars' worth.

HELPS OUR ASSOCIATION We are grateful to the foreign Mission Board for their assistance in securing missionary speakers for our camp for Royal Ambassadors, Girls in Action, and Acteens. This camp is held each summer at Chowan College for boys and girls from our association.

FOREIGN MISSION REPRESENTATIVES IN OUR MIDST We aie happy to have the Ralph Harrell's make our association their headquarters during their furlough year.
Their work is with the Baptist Mission of East Africa, located in Nairobi, Kenya.
Itiey will be staying in the home of Mr, Sam Knott, father of Mrs. Harrell, We are also happy to report that Dr. and Mrs. Hugh Mckinley will arrive on furlough in January from Rhodesia, being associated with the African Baptist Seminary in Gwelo. They will be staying in Wake Forest. Mrs. Mckinley is also tfie daughter of Mr. Sam Knott and the twin sister of Mrs. Harrell.


In this time of historic observance, when Southern Baptists are reviewing their beginnings and the foundations of their work, the American Bible Society, which was organized in 1816, appreciates the privilege of serving all forms of Southern Baptist Work around the world. All the service of the Bible Society is founded on these basic missionary principles: 1. People must have the Bible because it presents Jesus Christ, the one hope of men and nations.

2. Each person must have the Bible in his own language because it speaks to his heart as no other language can speak.

3. The Bible is the essential tool of missions and evangelism. The missionary uses it in all his work. The Bible itself is a missionary. It can go where the human missionary cannot go. It can stay on after he must leave and keep on speaking to a person's heart.

4. But, there is a worldwide shortage of Bibles - because, in most countries people cannot afford even the cost of printing the Scriptures.

The American Bible Society is a missionary, non-profit organization, serving churches of many denominations. It helps missionaries translate the Bible. It pub- lishes the Scriptures without note or comment. It distributes without profit, usually below cost, and without any charge in cases of special need. For the blind, it supplies Scriptures in Braille and other raised -letter systems. Talking Bible records, and tape recordings.

A program of missionary advance, with the theme, '*God's Word for a New Age,'* is being carried on by the American Bible Society, in cooperation with forty -eight other national Bible Societies in a world fellowship called the United Bible Societies.
The aim is to provide greatly increased quantities of Scriptures urgently needed for worldwide mission work.

Through our support of the American Bible Society we make available for dis- tribution: Bibles, testaments, Portions for Personal Bible Readings, Bible Reading Programs, Person to Person Evangelism, Hand -marked New Testament Projects, and Readings for Quiet Meditations and Bible Study.

We can give these to the sick, people in airports, bus stations, railroad stations, to **New Americans." International Visitors (as it is written in their own language), tourist courts, hotels, migrant workers camps, jails, prisons, juvenile detention homes, waiting rooms of doctors, dentists, hospitals, and the people you meet every- day.

There are over seventy million people in the United States who have never met Jesus Christ in the Holy Scriptures, nor know what He asks of them and what His love can do for each one of them. "Let us all be a bearer of the Book" to reach a neighbor, a friend, or someone who does not know Him as He really is, and thus enrich that person's life.

Respectfully submitted, Hesley Clark REPORT OF NOMINATION COMMITTEE Report 11 (Officers listed in Associational Directory) This report submitted by Mrs. Oliver Davis, Chm.? Mr. Buck Mann; Rev. Maylon Kearney; Mr. Mclendon Averett, Mr. Ralph Green.

SUPERINTENDENT OF MISSIONS REPORT Report 12 This has been a good year in our association. There have been activities in the association that reflects the leadership of our churches and pastors. It is extremely important for us to realize the ministry of our association could not move without the churches and pastors. You may feel that this is said in jests however, this is not the case at all.

We have seen the churches become more concerned for the second phase of missions. It is exciting to realize this year every church in the association con- tributed to the associational mission cause.

Within the year we have seen several of our ministers resign. These men being, Ronald King, Brassfields William Robertson, Corinth? Steve Williams, Delraynol Rudolph Pulley, Felloxwship; Clyde Pearson, Island Creek; Dana Scrivner, Mr. Olivet?
Walter Coleman, Oxford Baptist? Talmage Williams, Ffeaces Chapel? Richard Englert, Fbplar Creek? and Doug Roebuck, Tabbs Creek. As you can readily see, we have had a lot of turn over this year.

However, 1 am grateful for the lay leadership of our churches. We have excellent ministers in our association. There are men who have served us for a number of years. Brother E.W. Greene has served over 27 years and Brother Clarence Bishop is serving in 13 years in the work, and Brother Lemar Wheeler is given himself to 10 years of service in the area. Tlie list could continue down to the present day. We have 16 student ministers, 4 working ministers, and 13 ministers. We are truly appreciative of these men and their places in our association.

The on -going programs of the association have much to offer us in the future.
There are definite needs. These needs have difficulty expressing themselves in our association. 1 must be honest in appealing for your help. We have tried to determine these needs in relationship of the local churches. This year, we have faced difficulty in attaining information back from the churches.

We have employed the use of questionnaires, direct appeal to select groups.
These methods have not proved successful in attaining this information. Several of our organizations are poorly staffed not by head directors but with resource personnel.
We have very capable individuals leading the organizations. They are willing and able to assist you in your church. As you know, no organization in our structure can go into your church without an invitation. We wait for this invitation at this time.

The mission programs of ministries have proven to be one of our major thrust in the association this year. It is amazing to see the speed of some of the concepts taking form in the area. 1 will only give a brief outline of these endeavors in relation- ship of excellent cooperation with the State Baptist Convention, Home Mission Board, Foreign Mission Board, and Sunday School Board in Nashville.

HOME MISSION BOARD - Dr. Roy Owens of the Rural Urban Department of Associational Services visited our association to assist our Research and Study Committee. However, our committee was farther along than the suggestions from Dr.
Owens.

This summer I was privileged to travel with the Home Mission Board to Glorieta and Ridgecrest for a conference on Rural Urban Ministry. 1 served as a faculty member both weeks.

FOREIGN MISSION BOARD - The Foreign Mission Board has assisted us by keeping in touch with missionaries. They worked with concerning, however, last minute we had to secure our own missionary team.

NORTH CAROLINA STATE BAPTIST CONVENTION - The Church Division Pro- grams have aided us in many efforts of our association. Each one has given them- selves unselfishly to our ministry here.

The Division of State Missions has contributed heavily to our new concept of ministering in our association.

The Chaplaincy Department assisted us in the first Industrial and Ministers Con- ference in North Carolina.

Town and County has helped us in the pastoral aid of the First Baptist Church of Tungsten. The department stood ready to aid us with our Resort Ministry of $500.00.

' , 34 City and Metropolitan Department is continuing to assist us in the Butner ministries. We are hopeful that we will be able to inaugurate a ministry soon to this need.

Now, 1 would like to share with you the activities that I have been in this year. 1 must say that this summation is not given for brag purposes. It is given in order that you may have this information.


There is tremendous interest in North Carolina and the entire Southern Baptist Convention in the new WIN materials on lay wimessing and the one week Lay Evangelism Schools. Over twenty -five associations have already started specific planning toward a one week Lay Evangelism School in their area. The first Lay Evan- gelism School sponsored by the Division of Evangelism will be November 1-7 in the Mecklenburg Association. Other one week dates have already been confirmed in* several associations. Jesus reminded all Christian laymen of our task in Luke 24:48 - **And ye are witnesses of these things," Thousands of laymen and youth will respond if they are properly challenged and trained.

The Division of Evangelism in cooperation wiih our Brotherhood Department and the N.C, United Methodi>st Conference will sponsor four Regional Evangelism Con- ferences for Baptist and Methodist men during October 18-19 and 21-22, 1971 across the state. Lay evangelism will be the emphasis of these evening meetings. Dr. Ben Johnson of Atianta. Georgia who has assisted Dr. Billy Graham and Dr. Ken Chafin in several conferences, and Dr. Ralph Neighbor of Houston, Texas will be the two main speakers. Rev. Bob Gibbons will be the guest soloist. Laymen will preside, share personal testimonies and have the Scripture and Prayer.


The next Statewide Youth Evangelism Night will be on Friday evening, August 18, 1972 at Greensboro Coliseum.

On the local level we have had an associational-wide meeting with Mr. Corbin Cooper on how to set up an Area -Wide Revival. Your evangelism committee has met several times concerning this matter. Other meetings have been held to discuss the proposed crusade. At this time, we are in a holding pattern concerning this matter. We request your prayers as we continue to work in this area.

During the past church year, our 32 churches reported 240 additions by baptism and 228 other additions. Let us sn-ess the need for every Christian to be about the daily task of witnessing to the love of Jesus Christ in every area of his life so that "some might be saved." Respectfully submitted.
Rev. Don Price REPORT OF BAPTIST CHILDREN'S HOMES OF NORTH CAROLINA, INC.

Report 14 The child care and family service ministry of North Carolina Baptists has under- gone many new and thrilling experiences during the past year. Since the associations last met, our program of services has been strengthened in various ways.

The desire for a home to serve Western North Carolina has become a reality.
The first two cottages on the Broyhill Home campus near Waynesville have been completed, providing for twenty -five additional children in group care. Also during the past year, a maternity home has been opened in Asheville.

The Ten Star Builders Program, designed to inform Baptists and other friends of the Children's Home in Eastern North Carolina of the services we are rendering to children and their families, was launched last year. The initial response has been encouraging. Funds are being raised in Lenoir County for the W,C. Reed Memorial Gymnasium and plans are to have the dedication of this building on Sunday, November 7, 1971.

Foster homes in Eastern North Carolina now number sixteen with spaces for twenty- five children. Five years ago we had only one foster home in the Eastern area. This is encouraging growth. Additional foster homes are needed. This affords opportunity for a meaningful and rewarding ministry for children and foster parents alike.

The purpose of the Children's Homes is to provide children loving care in the spirit of Christ. Tfie pursuit of this basic goal has led the Homes to develop a variety of services, all family centered. Some children need resident care. For these we have group homes and foster homes. This addition was made possible by the generous challenge gift of $80,000.00 from Judge and Mrs. William York, of Greensboro. The trustees accepted the challenge and raised an additional $80,000.00.

I hope you will pray for our Baptist Homes for the aging and remember that any ministry to an older person in the name of Christ is a direct ministry to Him.

Respectfully submitted, Garland Catlette BAPTIST HOSPITAL REPORT Report 16 The Holy Scriptures tell us that Jesus went about doing good. His whole ministry was to the total man, mind, body, and soul. He has left this ministry to us. Jesus did this out of a heart filled with love, concern, and compassion for all mankind.

This is what our Baptist Hospital is trying to do now, minister to the whole man in the Spirit of Christ. They cannot do it without the help of all concerned Baptist.
This is our ministry as well as theirs.

We are glad to report that the financial picture on the operations of the hospital is more encouraging. There will continue to be difficult problems and the hospital must operate on a sound financial basis if it is to continue to serve. But we have been operating in the black all this year and have reduced significantly the large deficit which had accumulated over the past several years. The hospital, from its conception, and beginning has provided good medical care for our people, including those who could not pay the cost. In these days of great medical advances and spiriling cost, this policy has not been changed. This Christian ministry is the heart of the work at the Baptist Hospital.

Our Hospital has had to expand because of the demands upon it. A new patient tower will be opened for partial occupancy in 1972. A 14 bed coronary unit is now open. All of these additions are expensive, and if our hospital is to keep up its ministry, we must help. If it is to fulfiu its ministry, it must have our support. It is doing in North Carolina what hospitals operated by the Foreign Mission Board is doing around the world. It should be supported with the same enthusiasm, and for the same reasons we support our mission program. This is missions too. May God fill our people with the same love and concern for all people that our Lord has, I submit the following figures for your information: 32 churches in Flat River Association contributed through the Mother's Day Offering.

$3,701.16 >was contributed.

17,557 persons \A/ere admitted to the hospital last year.

150,174 days of care to patients.

51,563 out patients. ' : 17,241 emergency room patients.

Respectfully submitted, Maylon Kearney REPORT OF THE GENERAL BOARD Report 17 The General Board has met as required by the Constitution and the Executive Committee has met at least once a month during the past year.

The question of the problems of the Paramedical Schools at Baptist Hospital has consuraraed much time. At the \A;riting of this report the full Board has to consider a number of recommendations regarding this. The Board will made a recommendation to the Baptist State Convention in the November meeting concerning the Paramedical School plus a loan of $14,000,000 to the Hospital. The recommendation v/ill not affect the recommendation approved by the General Board in June concerning the appointment of a committee of 15 to consider future relationships \A/ith the Hospital.

As you kno\A' the Fruitland Justice Building was destroyed by fire. The Executive Committee met and gave the Director of Fmitland authority to accept the lowest bid to demolish and clear away the debris of this building. The Board has considered recommendations concerning rebuilding and financing this building.

The General Board will recommend to the Convention a budget of $7,750,000.
through the Cooperative Program. The overall goal will be $8,000,000. Of the base budget 33.34% will be allocated to the Southern Convention, 25.3% to Christian Higher Education, 11.14% to Social Service Institutions (Home for Aging, Hospital, Children's Homes) and 30.22% to Convention -wide items and State Missions (General Board Programs ).

The Board recommends that the "Overage" beyond $7,750,000. be allocated as follows: Southern Convention, 33.34% Christian Higher Education, 35% Social Service Institutions, 16.66% and State Missions, 15%.


Respectfully submitted, Warren T. Bush ' ^ REPORT OF THE ANNUITY BOARD ' Report 18 As prices of goods increased and the value of the dollar decreased in 1970, a large number of Baptist pastors, church staff members and denominational employees moved to provide greater protection in the years ahead for themselves and their families. Most of these persons did so by increasing their Southern Baptist Pro- tection Program participation to at least ten per cent of total salaiy.

While members upgraded, the Annuity Board \was introducing Good Experience Credit. This gives Protection Program members benefits over and above the amounts set forth in their certificates.

Increased benefits are made possible by interest earnings in excess 6f the Protection Program's initial actuarial assumptions. Since January 1, 1970, active members received Good Experience Credit in the form of increases in retirement credits accrued. From 1 Thessalonians 4:13-14 we read: ''But 1 would not have you to be ignorant, bretheren, concerning them which are asleep, that ye sorrow not, even as others which have no hope. For if we believe that Jesus died and rose again, even so them also which sleep in Jesus will God bring with him." Isn't theword*'hope" a wonderful sounding expression of joy, gladness and expect- ancy." It becomes an even more joyous word when we know of the hope which is ours through the death and resurrection of our Lord Jesus Christ. Because He rose, we who are in Christ can believe that we shall one day be reunited with our beloved dead in Christ.

The words of the following poem by James Mcgrahanaham express our Christian hope: Blessed hope that m Jesus is given.

In our sorrow to cheer and sustain, ' That soon in the Mans ion of Heaven, We shall meet with our loved ones again.

Blessed hope in the word God has spoken, All our peace by that word we obtain; And as sure as God's word was ne'er broken, We shall meet with our loved ones again.

Blessed hope! how it shines in our sorrow.

Like the star over Bethlehem's plain.

That it may be with Him, ere the morrow We shall meet with our loved ones again.

Blessed hope! the bright star of the morning.

That shall Herald his coming to reign, Oh, the glory that waits its fair da\A/ning, When we meet our lov'd ones again.

1 shall read the names of our beloved dead from the churches of our association afterwhich we shall stand for a moment of silent memorial on their behalf. Rev. Don Price will close our service with prayer.

On November 1-4 there will be anassociational-wide Bible Conference help in the Hester Baptist Church. This is to be led by competent workers. This Bible Study is for all members of this association who desire to study and gain more Bible knowledge Several of our pastors and Sunday School directors attended the Sunday School Con- ference at Camp Caraway on October 8-9.


1 challenge each of you to become more involved in the Sunday School program of your church during the coming year as we work together under the theme •' PEOPLE TO PEOPLE". This is for fellowship, and then any other planned activity we may see fit to add later. Why not make this tonight our regular weekly Sunday School Workers Study and Training Meeting and allow our monthly Wednesday night to serve as a general meeting?" **What would we do each Sundav night in such a meeting as this tonight?" asked a teacher.

The pastor responded with the possibility of gathering in the four new age grouping divisions and discuss, explain, familiarize ail with the scope, plan and know how of the new study materials. He told the group that really this was an idea of preparing for next years' workers and Sunday School year, but that we must start somewhere.

One of the deacon adult class teachers caught up the idea and stated that we would be in so much better shape now if only this idea, this sort of concept could have caught on before now.

During the discussion it was pointed out that Youth in Training was meeting simultaneously in another building, Girls in Action grades 1-6 were meeting at 6:00 p.m. on Sundays. The pastor teasingly told all present that training for better Sunday school work on each Sunday night would also guarantee more in attendance at the evening preaching or worship hour, 'too. The spirit of the entire meeting manifested this; a warm enthusiasm shared by all present, a feeling of togetherness, and a sense now we are really getting down to the business of taking Bible teaching and training seriously pervaded over the group.

Isn't the recognization that church training must goon an integral part of a church whose Sunday A.M. and P.M. worship services are growing, a church which has added forty new members since January 1, 1971, a church which has 40-50 in midweek prayer services, which has 12-18 adult gather one week night a month in homes to study the Bible, a church which recently made 125 or more visits in one week?
Bring up a child in the way he should go . . .* should be followed by *train up a church in the way it should grow/ Respectfully Submitted, Ray B. W. Morgon MUSIC REPORT 1970-71 Report 27 A In the area of Church Music the year 1970 -71 held many highlights. Under the lea- dership of Mrs. Frank Daniel, the following activities were carried out: A Musicians fellowship was held at the Oxford Baptist Church. In the absence of Mrs. Daniel, Mrs.

The month of March was highlighted by a visit from the Baptist Student Union Choir from Appalachian State University. Their concert was held at the Delrayno Baptist Church in connection with Associational Youth Night.

The Sing, which has become so popular in this association, was held at the Grassy Creek Baptist Church on March 21.


The summer was highlighted by the associational R. A. and G. A. Camp in which classes were offered in Music. The classes and choirs were directed by Mrs. Barbara Humble assisted by Miss Linda Compton and Miss Beverly Royster.

In the plans for the future is a regularly scheduled meeting of the Musicians Con- ference, the first of which will be held on October 26 at the Oxford Baptist Church.

We are anticipating a productive year through the ministry of Church Music in the Flat River Baptist Association.

Respectively submitted Barbara C. Humble CHRISTIAN LITERATURE REPORT Report 28 This past year ushered in an exciting new decade concerning Christian literature.
We live in constantly changing times and our literature has become more contemporary with an emphasis for meeting the challenges that we face.

Church literature is offered in five series which are: Convention Uniform for Adults and Youth Life and Works for Adults and Youth Forefront for Adults Foundation for Children and Preschoolers Support for Leaders, members, and families.
Some of our periodicals are: (1) HOME LIFE is pattered to meet the spiritual needs of the family and is con- sidered by many to be the most interesting publication set forth by the Department.


(4) CHURCH MUSICIAN is one of many publications of the music department with other publications, CHORAL TONES, CHORAL OVERTONES: Opusl: Opusii: YOUN MUSICIAN: MUSIC MAKERS: AND CHILDREN'S MUSIC LEADER, Publications not published by the Church Literature Departments: (1) HOME MISSIONS - this is one of the most controversial publications in the Baptist life this year. It is published by the Home Mission Board and seeks to deal with the problems of our day with real answers.

(2) COMMISSION is the Foreign Missions Board's magazine and gives news and stories that come to us from the foreign fields we serve as Baptists.

(3) Woman's Missionary Union has many magazines for its various groups. DI- MENSION is for the WMU Council; ROYAL SERVICE for Baptist Women? CONTEMPO for Baptist Young Women; ACCENT for Acteens; DISCOVERY and AWARE for Girls in Actions and START is for leaders and teachers of the Mission Friends.

94) BIBLICAL RECORDER is the North Carolina Baptist newspaper. It gives infor- mation from the state officers, news from the churches and articles dealing with cur- rent issues which affect us.

(5) CHARITY AND CHILDREN is the state paper which comes from the Children's Homes of North Carolina and gives news of the work there.


The Christian Life Commission's emphasis on applied Christianity is finding good acceptance among Southern Baptists.

During the past year, numerous articles and stories on applied Christianity were channeled by the Commission through practically all of the state Baptist papers.
Southern Baptist Convention agency publications and other mass media outlets. The Commission's literature program has been substantially strengthened by ten new •'Issues and Answers" pamphlets covering the subjects of aging, capital punishment, consumer credit, generation gap, open housing, pollution, population explosion, re- volution, sex education, and taxation. The commission now has some 70 tracts in print and available for helping churches emphasize the practical dimensions of the Christian faith. A complete and illustrated order form is available upon request.

The Christian Life Commission's conference program has developed especially well during the past year. The largest national seminar ever sponsored by the Com- is missions \A?as held in New York in March on "National Priorities and Christian Respon- jsibility" with more than400 in attendance. Three of the addresses from the conference Iwere carried nationally on the National Broadcasting Company's "Faith in Action'* j program. All the addresses have been printed in booklet form and are available from I the Commission for $2.50 as a study resource. Four regional conferences scheduled I for 1971 have proven to be a new and helpful way of getting the Commisstion's em- jphasis on applied Christianity out to a wider audience than ever before.
j A new approach to providing pastors and other church leaders with resource raate- I rials on the pressing moral issues of our times has been utilized this year. Three .Icarefully researched resource papers on pollution, urban crisis, and dmgs have been brinted and inserted in Home Missions magazine, and others are projected for the future.

The Christian Life Commission has also focused on drug education by developing for the Seminary Extension Department an Independent Reading Pmarara for pastors lentitled PROBLEMS PASTORS FACE: HELPING PEOPLE WITH DRUG TOBLEMS. This program is designed to give pastors basic factual information about drugs plus a jchristian critique of drug abuse. It also suggests resources for helping people who 'have drug problems.

j The Christian Life Commission's objective is to help develop the kins of Christian lnorality, both private and public, which undergirds the total Southern Baptist witness for Jesus Christ. God is calling Southern Baptists to act responsibly in all phases of :life, permeating the community as the salt of the earth and the light of the world. In ksponse to this calling from God, the Christian Life Commission is dedicated to pmphasizing the application of the Christian faith in the world.

Warren Bush Frazier Rev, Clarence Godwin Frazier Rev, Lemar Wheeler Frazier Rev, Talmage Williams Frazier Rev, David Blakely HISTORY OF CONCORD BAPTIST CHURCH Concord Baptist Church is located in the southern part of Granville County, three and one half miles from Creedmoor, North Carolina, The original church was build of logs in the year 1782. It was named Locks Church. In 1805 the church was constituted by Elders George Rebbards, J. Ruke and James Weathers and the name was changed to Concord Baptist Church. From 1805 to 1855 a period of fifty years there is no record.
It was in its early years that Concord reached its peak.

There are many interesting facts about Concord Church, A special gallery was in use for Negroes from the time of the organization in 1805 until 1867, when they went into a separate building.

In 1859 the church was presented the Bible by the ladies of the church.

In 1888 chairs were purchased for the church. Some of these chairs are used today in the Sunday School rooms. Many of the chairs were sold to members in 1965 when new pews were purchased.

During the earlv years the church ordained two ministers, Elijah Hester and L.L.
Hudson.

Hie first Baptist Young People's Union was organized at Concord Church in 1925 xwith Ralph Daniel, first President. Another great leader was Mrs. Tlielma Coley.

Today Concord has a resident membership of 175 with total membership of 265, The present building is air-conditioned and in 1968 a new piano was purchased.

During the history of Concord Church noted pastors have served the pastorate.
Among these was Dr. W.R. Cullom, while serving here (1891-1893) he was a student at Wake Forest College. He was also a professor of the Bible there. On October 11, 1959, Dr. Cullom was guest speaker at the Homecoming Service.

Another well-known minister, Rev, E,G. Usry, served the pastorate of Concord for twenty -seven years.

One of our present deacons, Walter B. Clark, has served on the deacon board since 1929. He has served this office with untiring efforts. A man of generous spirit, richly endowed with those noble traits of character which prompts him under all circumstances to act fully upon the principles which should govern all men in their dealings with others. His dear wife, Mrs. Maggie Veasey Clark, has taught in our Sunday School for over fifty years and today teaches the Adult Ladies Class. Too much cannot be said for these dear christians. We feel in years to come we will still see the results coming from the work of these two genuine Christians.

We have had many dedicated men and women to serve this church as deacons and clerks, Sunday School Superintendents, and other leaders. We feel that under the leadership of these people and many other dedicated members, we can attribute the continual growth of our church.

Chisenhall 1966 - --Complied by Mrs, Mary Grace Cash, 1971 THE HISTORY OF THE FIRST BAPTIST CHURCH OF TUNGSTEN On July 3, 1949, Bullock Baptist Church met in called conference to discuss plans for establishing a Mission Sunday School at the Tungsten Mine. The church agreed to the recommended plan of the pastor and the body of deacons to employ the services of Miss Ztella Dew Woody to lead in the organizing of the proposed mission. The Bidlock Baptist Church agreed to pay Miss Woody $25.00 a \A;eek for a period of 6 to 7 weeks.

On October 1, 1950 letters were granted to all members of Tungsten Mine to become charter members of the newly organized church. And on November 5, 1950, a special meeting was held to organize the new church. A motion was made and approved to call the newly formed church "The First Baptist Church of Tungsten." It was then agreed that all persons joining the church within six weeks would be charter members also. It was also voted to join the Flat River Baptist Association, When the Tungsten Mine was closed in 1958, the Tungsten Church was very much affected in that many of its members moved elsewhere. The church struggled on, and except for a handful of faithful and dedicated members the church doors were never closed.

After being without a pastor for six months, the church called the Rev. James S.
Talley as pastor on January 1971.

The Lord has blessed this little church in a mighty way and it began to prosper and history began to be made in this church. The church began to serve full time having two services on Sunday and Wednesday night prayer meeting. The church began to grow in membership and a choir was organized with Mrs. Lucille Forbes as director. For the first time in this churches history a church budget was adopted.

Many other beautification projects were initiated by the members. A few of these are a pastors study and extra Sunday School rooms were added, the church was carpeted and new lighting was installed. With the Lord leading and the people working towards that goal The First Baptist Church of Tungsten will go far for our Lord, Pastors who have served at the •* First Baptist Church of Tungsten" 1. Rev. John W. Phillips 1950 - 1951 2. Rev. Ned Hughes 1951 - 1954 3. Rev. Fred Ellis 1954-1955 4. Rev. Ernest Canipe 1955 - 1957 5. Rev. Joe Bennett 1957 - 1958 6. Rev. Lee Pittman 1958 - 1970 7. Barbara >was bom November 2, 1910, Died March 9, 1971 from a massive heart attack following surgery. He had been in extremely bad health for two years.

Education: Graduated from Kenly High School; A.B. degree from Atlanta Christian College, Wilson, N.C.; B.D, degree from Vanderbilt University, Nashville, Tenn.; one and one -half years graduate work in Boston University, Boston, Mass., and one and one -half years graduate work in the University of N.C. in Chapel Hill towards his Ph. D in Sociology.

Satterwhite, chairman of Time and Place Committee reported that the annual spring meeting for 1972 will be held at Providence Baptist Church, and the leadership conference for 1972 will be at Delrayno Baptist Church.

Miss Cindy Howard, of Mt. Zion presented a vocal rendition of "Open the Gates of the Temple." Rev. Ray Frye missionary to Malaysia was the missionary speaker for the after- noon, speaking on his mission work in Malaysia, The evening session opened with the congregation singing "Hark the Voice of Jesus Calling.'* The devotion was presented by Mrs. Alma Rogers, and Mrs. Evelyn Mangum of Creedinoor Baptist Church. Hester Quartet rendered special music for the evening entitled "He Touched Me" and For God So Loved." Rev. Dan Page, superintendent of inissions introduced the speaker for the evening.
Miss Alma Hunt, executive secretary of Woman's Missionary Union of Southern Baptist Convention, Birmingham, Alabama. Miss Hunt, continued the throughts on •'Living the Spirit of Christ in Openess and Freedom." Challenging each WMU member to be a raolder of circumstances rather than a victim of circumstances, by living a relevant faith in Jesus Christ.

Rev. Ray Frye, brought a challenge to those present that we unite in concerted prayer that someone may feel the call to go from this association to serve on the mission field.

Meeting adjourned at 8:30 P.M. Miss Sarah Ann Hobbs, executive secretary of Woman's Missionary Union of North Carolina pronouncing the benediction.

Carol Sing 4 Pastor's Conference , ' 8 Christmas Fellowship for Pastors and Wives 10 Foreign Missions Day in Sunday School 31 Murdoch Visitation Sunday NON -STATISTICAL TABLE ♦This page is included as a solemn reminder that the weightier matters for which we are responsible and in which we are engaged often defy analysis. Much that we do is measurable, but many of our efforts toward justice, mercy and faith are not.
To those whose anonymous deeds to little known persons have found Divine approval, this page is dedicated.

"HIS MINISTRY>OUR MINISTRY" ANNUAL SERMON, 1971 Text: Luke 4:16-21 In this text Jesus announced his ministry. It \was religious, social, and political: to preach good news to the poor - religious, and recovery of the sight of the blind - social, to set at liberty the oppressed - political. That affirmation made him a total threat to the established order. That's \A/hy they killed him. The concept of a weak, mild, soft-spoken Jesus cannot explain what happened in Galilee. He was exciting, stormy, and uncompromising. The world doesn't kill the weak, but in any era people react to those who attack their folkways and their hallowed traditions. Jesus was such a person; no one took him for granted.

Now, the New Testament states that the church is the "body of Christ." The imagery is vivid. Whateverelse the terminology seeks to convey, obviously, the church is to be an extension of the ministry of Jesus. This isn't optional; it isn't decided by a majority vote; it is a command! His ministry is to be our ministry.

With this in mind there are several questions to be pondered in our day. For instance, what has happened to this dynamic, earth shaking message and ministry of Jesus in the hands of the 20th century church? Unfortunately, that which once turned the world upside down, in its present institutional expression, packs the power of a creampuff. Why does the church today have more members and less influence than at any other time in its history? Why are young people, according to a recent report, leaving the church twice as fast as any other age group? Just two months ago in one of our larger cities young people participating in a parade reportedly were shouting, •* Jesus - yes, church - no!" Lets look at tiie problem squarely. You may not agree with my suggestions or conclusions, but 1 am more concerned about stimulating some dialogue with your thinking processes. For if the church is to claim its rightful plane as both catalyst and servant to this generation, laity and clergy must ask some honest questions about the direction and priorities of the church in our day.

The problem is three -fold - all feeding on each other. First, church is dull. That's right - d-u-1-1. Dorothy Sayers wrote some years ago, "The church of our day has succeeded in doing what the apostles and even the enemies of Christ never did: it has made Christ appear dull!'* In the early sixties in another state 1 attended the Pastor's Conference just prior to the annual Baptist State Convention. The main speaker was a layman, who had been assigned the subject, "The Church and Its Problems in Contemporary America." He spoke very articulately from a layman's point of view. His words were scathing yet filled with love for the church; his voice was angry but tempered with compassion. At the conclusion there was a hushed stillness. Finally, one pastor spoke and asked the speaker to state in one sentence the basic problem of the church. The reply was, "I fear, sir, the church is too damn dull!
That stings. It stung them.

Second, the church is dull because we have become what has been called "barracks Christians." You remember that Paul lived in an occupied nation. Con- tinually encountering the military and observing their discipline, he referred to Christians as "soldiers of Christ." Today we sing by rote, "Onward Christian soldiers, going as to war, with the cross of Jesus, going on before..." Where are we going?
To the world for that is what God so loved. With what are we going? With the ministry of Jesus: religiously, socially, and politically - to the poor, to the blind, to the oppressed.

Suppose for a moment the commander \A/ere to ask us, ''How is the war going on in the world?" What would we reply? **Oh, it's going just greats we had five hundred of the soldiers together today. That's more than we've had in a long time. The spirit was wonderful; you've never heard such good singing." But the commander would probably reply, "Good, but how about the war?" "Well, we just added on an addition to the barracks; you ought to see the new kitchen. We've just installed in- door restrooms." With exasperation the commander would exclaim, *'How about the war!" Why does a paratrooper diligently train? Because he knows that in the immediate future, he will need that training when he jumps. Today we are bored with our train- ing sessions in the church, because we never jump. We linger in the safety of the barracks in perpetual training sessions, but too seldom do we jump when the action is with the ministry of Jesus.

Third, we have insulated this earth shaking message from the nitty-gritty of today's world. Innovative involvement is lacking. We have molded a safe religion by embaracing the status quo. We have hugged non -controversial causes. Anyone can join the church today without changing his basic attitudes and concepts. In fact, in recent years strong evidence has been presented to verify that much of the joining of the church in the so-called "Bible Belt'* is directly correlated to the belief that the church fortifies the southern philosophy or its way of life, to wit: SOUTHERN CHURCHES IN CRISIS by Samuel S. Hill, Jr., THE SURGE OF PIETY IN AMERICA by A.
Roy Eckardt, THE BURDEN OF SOUTHERN HISTORY by C. Vann Woodard, and SOUTH- ERN WHITE PROTESTANTISM IN THE TWENTIETH CENTURY by Kenneth K. Bailey.

I believe that the 1970's shall prove to us, if they already haven't, that the swollen church rolls of the 1950' s have been an empty victory, because cheap grace has been sounded through our mortgaged belfrey bells. Strangely enough, at a juncture in history, when the gospel as it relates to the now is needed wilih clarity, the church has played it safe and speaks in a vacuum! Evidence abounds. In an a joining state the ''great'* issue to be debated at the annual convention is to be speaking in tongues!
And in this state we are already hearing ominus noises that this year we may hassle over the mode of baptism! How sad, In a period of history when even everything nailed down is coming loose, the church sits on the sidelines bickering with itself over irrelevant matters.

Dr. Gerald Kennedy, that articulate bishop of the Methodist Church, reminds us that when the Russian Revolution was raging in the streets of Petrograd, the leaders of the church were insulated inside and cathedral locked in 'great theological debate'* over the cut and color of clerical robes! In the Eighth Century B.C. Isaiah lamented not that the, world was blind, but because the people of God refused to see. Ex- claiming that God was bored with liturgical exactness and hollow religious observ- ances, he insisted that God desired that they learn to do good, to seek justice, and to correct oppression. They never learned. Seven hundred years later Jesus carried on a running battle with their ancestors of the same persuasion, and in obvious exasperation he pronounced the final verdict on that narrow, internalized religious system, when he said, "Behold your house is foresaken and desolate!" Dr. Elton Trublood in his recent book, THE HUMOR OF CHRIST reminds us that Jesus gathered about him people who were sick of wrangling over religion that was narrow, hair splitting, rigid, and inhuman, and he taught them how to live a life before God, while seeking to bring about change in the world. It is my personal belief that the church of our day is repeating the tragic story of the people of the Old Testament. The question is no longer can the church convert the world, but can God convert the church to the ministry of Jesus?

Yet, there's one thing we have going for us. We are the master devisers of slogans to which we rarely pay attention thereafter. Notice the theme of the Southem Baptist Convention for 1971, which is also the theme for this Associational meeting: "Living the Spirit of Christ in Expectancy and Creativity." It sounds good, but it begs to be applied. Perhaps the theme could at least act as a catalyst to prod us to the real- ization that we must recapture for our own day the bravery and tenacity of our Baptist forefathers. These men went to the forefront of the social needs of their day with colleges, hospitals, homes for the orphaned, the aged, and other ministries. Today we ride on their coattails as we haphazardly support what they began. We still paddle in the backwater of the forethought of those people. Innovation to the sit- uations in our own day that captures the imagination of our people has not yet arrived.
It may be a "no -no" for me to say so, but it is my honest observation that as the federal and state governments more and more invade our previously "sacred cow** areas of education and hospitalization, the rank and file of Baptist people shall give less and less to support these institutions. Furthermore, just as mtmy of the programs spawned in the 1930' s are now passing off the scene, that previously magical caption "Cooperati-ve Program" no longer grasps the imagination and dedication of our people.

It is a new day and a new challenge The time for the abandonment of safety and the ploy for public applause by our denominational leaders, pastors, and layman has arrived. New challenges must be brought to this era with the ministry of Christ: denominationally, associationally, and through the local church.

Consider these suggestions. First, we must become biblically evangelistical as the message of Jesus stands in opposition to the isms of this generation. Today evangelism has all but dried up in our local churches. The annual revival is, as one has said, "a warmed over memorial service to something that happened a hundred and fifty years ago." What did the great revival spirits of the past have that is missing in our hybrid evangelism of our own day? 1 speak of men such as John Wesley, George Whitfield, Charles Finney, and others. Paulstaggin his book, THE CONVERTED CHURCH elucidated that these men lit revival fires by calling people to Christ, but at the sametime calling them to correct certain social conditions. He asserts that Charles Finney's revivalism was identified directlywith the abolition of slavery. Stagg writes that two-thirds of all antislavery societies were in Baptist and Methodist churches - revival churches. As the early church had proclaimed, ''Jesus - yes, Caesar - no!" the churches under the tutelage of these great evangelists were called upon to proclaim, "Jesus - yes, slavery - no!" That social task is unfinished. Today, people must be called to Christ by pro- claiming, "Jesus - yes, racism - no!" Bigotry and Christianity will not mix.
Southern Baptist can have one but not both. We lost our power at that subtle point when we began to soft-pedal the great issues that gnawed at the fabric of this nation and became concerned with the "numbers racket.'* The effort of our Christian Life Cora- mission and our BIBLICAL RECORDER is commendable, but the truth of the matter is that the sporting world and the government has done more to correct racial oppression than this culturally captured denomination. As in the days of Isaiah, it is the people of God who will not see!

Second, 1 suggest a moratorium on church building. Certainly, this would not be feasible in all instances, but the point is valid. We are spending entirely too much on ourselves. In the local churches we enter elaborate stewardship compaigns to build elegant temples to put vanity that too often take upon themselves an atmosphere of country clubism, giving to the poor and the dispossessed the feeling that we are interested only in ourselves. The church of Christ is not brick and mortar; it is flesh and blood: And the more we invest in bricks the safer we are forced to play it with the gut issues of the now in order to perserve it, get it paid for, and keep it going.
A young layman 1 know, looking at a newly completed church building in which he held membership, exclaimed, "That thing costs us eight hundred dollars a week just to keep it sitting there! And in the process we fail tiie spirit of Jesus's ministry in at least two ways: we can't teach the radical nature of the gospel for fear that much of our membership will quit, and we know there are children almost within sight of the steeple who haven't had a nutritious meal in days." We need to recall that the countrymen of Jesus had the right building, the right carpet, the right curtains, even the right Book. Yet, Jesus indicated that all the money they had spent was to no avail.
Tlie reason? They had prostituted the message of God, and that big holy building was left to them desolate.

Third, 1 advocate the firing of the Superintendent of Missions - not because he hasn't tried to do his job, but because he can't. He is a good, capable man. Indeed, he has the marks of a genuine leader. But in the final analysis he is cornered into pushing the denominational program in the local churches - a program entirely too introverted having been fashioned to help people play it safe in the barracks. How- ever, why not rehire him and give him a job worthy of his abilities. Until a better title comes along, call him "Director of Associational Church Action." Perhaps he could lead the churches to service in the following areas: First, to establish a family counseling service for all people regardless of race, creed, or color. Competition with the mental health clinic should not be our goal, but simply the counseling of troubled people by competent people with the unapologetic orientation that Jesus Christ is the way to psychic wholeness.

Second, to establish a Human Relations Program between black and white churches in this general associational area. Why not the implementation of some of the verbalization from our Sunday school classrooms that* 'You ought to love everybody.'* In race relations a large vacuum has been created because God's people have been afraid, inhibited, and the pawn of social mores. In this Southland, where Baptist are a bragging majority, slavery, the ensuing pattern of segregation, and many of the racial problems of our day must be laid at our feet. When reconciliation was needed, we huddled in the barracks and said nothing!

Third, the establishment of an Associational Budget thatwill provide scholarships for the young in the day of increasing educational cost, supplements for the genuinely poor to whom welfare, etc. keeps them just above the destitute level. Also, the establishment of accredited, non -segregated educational facilities - preferably in our too seldom used church buildings - to teach people of all ages how to read and write, sew and cook, and other needed skills in this technological age. Last year one of the young ladies of our church was one of the census takers in the county. She was appalled at the poverty and ignorance of both races. This association has a direct responsibility to these people.

Fourth, the establishment of a Drugs Anonymous (including Alcoholics Anonymous) to train pastors and laity to minister to those addicted to all forms of drugs in this country as well as providing a place of acceptance for those wishing to conquor the drug habit. My experience in this county leads me to the conclusion that we have alcoholics by the bushel loads, and the users of hard drugs continues to increase.

No doubt, you are thinking this will cost a lot of money. Of course it ^A/ill.

Constitution PREAMBLE For the declaration of its fundamental purposes and the pemianent guidance and control of its activities, this body does adopt the following as its constitution, to wit: ARTICLE I - NAME The name of this body is the Flat River Baptist Association of North Carolina.

ARTICLE 11 - OBJECTIVES Section 1. The objectives of the Association shall be to foster evangelism, missions and benevolences, Christian Education and such other objects as maybe promoted by the North Carolina Baptist State and Southern Baptist Convention, and the Baptist World Alliance; to preserve correct statistics of the Churches, to promote harmony and give Christian counsel in case of difficulty in and among the Churches upon their invitation; and to devise and recommend measures for programs of common interest to all the churches.

ARTICLE III - MEMBERSHIP Section 1. Tlie Associati.on shall be composed of all the pastors of the churches of the Association and messengers elected by the churches. Each church is entitled to three messengers for the first one hundred members or fraction thereof and one additional messenger for each additional fifty members or fraction thereof: providing no church shall have more than ten messengers.

Section 2, Any Missionary Baptist Church, which has been a member in good standing for a period of twelve months or more with another Missionary Baptist Association, and any new Missionary Baptist Church seeking admission to this body must present its application to the Executive Committee not less than six months prior to the annual meeting, and shall be received into the Association upon a majority vote of the members present.

ARTICLE IV - ORGANIZATION Section 1. The officers of the Association shall be a Moderator, Vice -Moderator, Clerk, Treasurer, Assistant Treasurer, Auditor, Church Training Director, Sunday School Director, Woman's Missionary Union Ixrector, Brotherhood Director, Director of Church Music, Director of Visual Aids, Historian (or Historians), Three Trustees, and Superintendent of Missions, and Parliamentarian. These officers shall be elected at each annual session and shall hold office for one year, or until their successors in office shall be filled to respective offices, except the Superintendent of Missions whose term of office is indefinite, and the Trustees who will serve three -year terms on a rotation basis with one being elected each year for a three -year term. The Moderator cannot succeed himself more than one term. All terms of office shall begin immediately before adjournment of the annual meeting of the Association.

Section '2. The Moderator and Vice -Moderator shall be elected from the floor at the beginning of the second session of the first day. The other officers (except the Superintendent of Missions) as named in Article 4, Section 1, shall be elected by the Association after receiving nominations from the Nominating Committee, and after giving opportunity for nominations from the floor of the Association. The committee shall have the right to electitsown officers, other than the chairman, who is the moderator. ■ Section 3, It shall be the duty of the committee to carry on the work of the Asso- ciation between sessions, \A/orl< out the budget, and present recommendations and rejx^irts to the Association.

Section 4. Meetings of the executive committee may be called by the moderator.
All members shall be ncnified in writing prior to the meetings. It shall be necessary to have a quorum of 20 to transact business.

ARTICLE Vil - Omies OF COMMITTBES Section 1. A committee of committees, composed offive from different churches, shall nominate five members for the Nominating Committee, three members for the Time, I^ace and Iveaclier Committee, three from the Resolutions Committee, three for Obituary Committee, nine for Missions -Advisory Committee, seven for the Evan- gelism Committee, five for Budget Committee, six for Program Committee, eight for Calendar Committee, ten for Ordination Committee, four for Christian Life Committee, four for Camp Committee, and three for Constitution Committee and submit them for election by the Association at the opening session. These committees shall be cora- ]X3sed of pastors and lay persons from the different churches.

Section 2. Tlie Nominating Committee named at the Annual Associational Meeting shall nominate all officers during the year, including the nominees to be elected at the following annual meeting, except the Moderator and Vice -Moderator whose election is described in Article 4, Section 2. Tlie Sunday School Tdirector, Church Training Director, Woman's Missionary Union Director and the Brotherhood Director shall be nominated for election by the Executive Committee at its July meeting. Other officers to be elected at the annual association are Clerk, Treasurer, Assistant Treasurer, Auditor, Director of Church Music, Director of Audio -Visual Aids, Historians, three Tmstees, any other officer the Association may decide it needs, and the following representativeswhowill report to the annual Association: State Missions, Home Missions, Foreign Missions, Baptist Colleges, Relief and Annuity Board, North Carolina Baptist Hospital, North Carolina Baptist Homes, and other objects or agencies of denominational interest.

Section 3. Duties of Time, Hace and Preacher Committee shall be to recommend the time and place of the next annual meeting of the Association and any other meet- ings of the Association and shall select the preacher for the annual sermon and reprt to the Association.


vsccri(in 5, rxitics of the Obituary Committee shall be to form all expressions of rnchioi'ialso Section 6. The duties of the Missions- Advisory Committee shall be to study the missionary needs in the Association, counsel with the Superintendent of Missions in promoting the adopted program of work, recommend projects with which the Churches of the Association may cooperate. The Superintendent of Missions shall be responsible to this Committee. This committee should be representative of all phases of the As- sociational program. The Sunday School Director, Church Training Director, Woman's Missionary Union Director, and the Brotherhood Director shall serve ex-officio as non- voting members.

Reports REPORT OF COMMITTEE ON COMMITTEE Report 1 This report listed in Associational Directory under No. V. This report submitted by Rev. Thomas Lx)liey, Chm.; Mr. Robert Walker; Mrs. William H. Dean; Mrs. Ned Jones; and Mr. Gerald Rash.

REPORT OF THE EXECUTIVE COMMITTEE Report 2 - At the very beginning of this report I would like to lift some sentences from the constitution of the Flat River Baptist Association that pertain to the composition and duties of the Executive Committee. This information, I believe, will be helpful to all of us.

"The Executive Committee is composed of all active pastors, and officers, all chairmen of all standing committees of the Association along with one elected representative from each of the churches in the Association and such persons who will report to the annual associational meeting on the various objects and agencies of denominational interest. Our first order of business was election of officers for the new year in this committee. George Gresham was elected Chairman and Mrs. Flora Mann was elected as Secretary. The agenda included dis- cussion of the Butner Christian Social Ministries; a report on the work of the Resort Ministries for last year and ways to improve this ministry this year.


In July, Mr. Ernest Upchurch, Secretary of the City and Metropolitan Department of Baptist State Convention of N.C., met with our committee with regards to the Butner Christian Social Ministry. After lengthy discussion, the committee recommend- ed to the Executive Committee that the Association proceed with a lesser program of ministering with theparticipationofthe Baptist State Convention of N.C. at our earliest possible time. The Missions-Advisory Committee having the responsibility of drawing up the final agreement between the two parties and the Missions Advisory Committee will have the authority to negotiate with the Baptist State Convention for the employ- ment of an individual at the earliest possible time.

Also, at the July meeting, Brother Dan Page submitted his resignation which was accepted with much regret.

In a special called meeting on July 24, 1972, the Missions Advisory Committee proceeded to work out a schedule for the as sociational office secretary during the interim period being without a Superintendent of Missions. The schedule will include two days in office and one-half day at home for the first three weeks in each month and three days during the fourth week of each month. Additional work days were ap- proved depending need of. The Rev. James T alley was asked to moderate the Flat River Baptist Hour each Sunday until a new Superintendent of Missions is employed.
Rev. Earl Howard, Rev. Clarence Godwin, and Mr. Henry Currin were named to serve as Selections Committee to find a Superintendent of Missions.



3) Approved a request for $1500. pastoral aid from the Baptist State Convention of N.C. for Tungsten First Baptist Church.

4) Approved the Rev. Earl Howard as worker for the Butner Christian Social Minis- try starting October 1, 1972.

Respectfully submitted, George Gresham, Chm.
Missions Advisory Committee REPORT OF CONSTITUTION COMMITTEE Report 4 CHANGE IN CONSTITUTION Article VII, Section 6: NOW READS - The duties of the Missions- Advisory Committee shall be to study the missionary needs in the Association, counsel with the Superintendent of Missions in promoting the adopted program of work, recommend projects with which the Churches of the Association may cooperate. The Superintendent of Missions shall be responsible to this Committee. This committee should be representative of all phases of the Associational program.

REVISION - The duties of the Missions-Advisory Committee shall be to study the missionary needs in the Association, counsel with the Superintendent of Missions in promoting the adopted program of work, recommend projects with which the Churches of the Association may cooperate. The Superintendent of Missions shall be responsible to this Committee. This committee should be representative of all phases of the Associational program. The Sunday School Director, Church Training Director, Woman's Missionary Union Director, and the Brotherhood Di- rector shall serve ex- officio as non- voting members.
