Veasey WOMAN'S MISSIONARY UNION REPORT Report 5 As time has come again for Woman's Missionary Union to evaluate our work for the year, I feel there has been many accomplishments, but there are many more that need to be made. It is with deep regret that some of our churches in Flat River Asso- ciation do not have any type of WMU work; there are some that have one or two or- ganizations; then others that have all the organizations. What is wrong? Have we lost our desire to involve, inspire, and inform people of their missions obligations? I am afraid that we as Christians and WMU members cannot wholeheartedly say that we fulfilled our WMU Watchword forthe past year - "All will openly proclaim that Jesus Christ is the Lx)rd. To the Glory of God the Father". (Phil. 2:11) As we begin a new year, may we all come to realize that out of the belief that member involvement in Woman's Missionary Union will strengthen what the church does in missions: in representative missions, as weleam, give, pray, and heed God's call, as we serve Him.

I shall try to tell of some of the activities that have taken place during this year.

The WMU Spring Meeting which was held in March at the Providence Baptist Church was a most inspiring and challenging meeting. We were fortunate to have with us our ' ' 24 state WMU President, Mrs. Gilmer Cross; and Mrs. Hugh Mckinley, missionary to Rhodesia.

The Baptist Young Women held an Hawaiian Aloha at the Mountain Creek Com- munity Building in April. This organization has seven churches with Baptist Young Women and all were represented. This was under the direction of Mrs. Willis Hoyle, associational BYW Director.

The Acteens Rally was held in May at the Oxford Baptist Church. The theme was "Things Get A Little Easier Once You Understand". Testimonies were given by three missionary kids. This Rally was directed by Mrs. Peggy Yancey, associational Ac- teens Director.

Under the direction of Mrs. Thomas J. Currin, Girls In Action Director, a most successful Day Camp was held in May at the Delrayno Baptist Church.

A Reception was held in February for Mission Friends Directors and Leaders. This was directed by the associational Mission Friends director, Mrs. Howard Frazier.

Several of our associational WMU Directors attended the State Workshop at Mundo Vista in September.

The two-night Leadership Conference was held in September at the West Oxford Baptist. The first was for inexperienced leaders of two years or less of service and the second night for three years or more. The attendance was approximately 140 for the two conferences.

Each of the associational WMU officers took part in observance of WMU Focus Week by participating in Morning Devotions over radio station WOXF.

The WMU Watchword for the 1972-73 year, "Ask of me, and I will make the na- tions your heritage, and the ends of the earth your possession." Psalm 2:8. May we each ask ourselves. What is my attitude and action towards missions? Missions ad- vance tomorrow depends on decisions we and our churches make today.

Pray that God shall do His work through us as Woman's Missionary Union mem- bers in the year ahead.

Respectfully submitted, Mrs. Lawrence Davis Assn. WMU Director REPORT OF BROTHERHOOD Report 6 The Brotherhood Department of our Association has been busy this year trying to enlist new members in both Baptist Men and Royal Ambassadors. Last year we re- ported 15 churches with either Baptist Men or RA's or both. Your Associational offi- cers have visited in three churches to organize new chapters with about 25 men and boys participating. We had one church to start Baptist Men work and 2 churches to begin RA. We hope more of our churches will begin this important work this year.

During the year, we had two most enjoyable and important events. The first being our Baptist Men's Banquet in January with 120 men and their wives present. This was the second such banquet for the Flat River Assoc, and we hope to plan more in thq future. Then our RA Conclave in May was twice as large as the previous year. We had over 100 Ras and leaders in attendance with 8 churches represented. Both men and boys are looking forward to next year.

Some of our churches took men and boys to the Men and Boys Convention in Ra- leigh which is always a highlight of the year. This Convention has grown so large that it is divided into three locations instead of the usual one.


We miss very much the leadership and guidance of our former Supt. of Missions, Brother Dan Page, but we hope with your help and prayers and God's guidance, we can continue to grow.

If you do not have Brotherhood organizations in your church, please consider the importance of training your men and boys in missions for Christ.

Please call your associational officers, Mr. Wilbur Owens or myself if we can assist you in any way.

Respectfully submitted, George Gresham Assn. Brotherhood Director COOPERATIVE PROGRAM REPORT Report 7 The Cooperative Program is a part of the tithes and your offerings given through your church. Thus, your dedicated life with a part of your day-to-day labor goes toward witnessing to a lost world.

The Cooperative Program is sharing your love of God through 6,000 state, home, and foreign missionaries. Last year they were instrumental in bringing more than 66,000 people to Christ who in turn followed Him into the waters of baptism.

The Cooperative Program is busy training capable leaders by helping students with scholarships in more than 800 Baptist schools with more than 100,000 pupils around the world.

The Cooperative Program is ministering to the needs of the sick through 21 Bap- tist hospitals and 105 clinics in many nations. You helped more than 1,000,000 pa- tients last year who could not pay the cost of their care. The Cooperative Program aids in bringing shelter, food, clothing, and other necessities of life to 8,000 home- less children plus more than 2,000 elderly people through Children* s Homes and Homes for the Aging throughout the world.

Last year, 1,020 Baptist churches in North Carolina increased Cooperative Pro- gram giving by 10 percent or more over what they contributed the previous year. Did the church where you serve increase this much?

During the past year the churches of the Flat River Association gave $58,941.00 through the Cooperative Program which was a gain of $9,908.00 over the previous year.

Will you do two things?

1. Will you lead the church where you serve to make a worthy increase in Co- operative Program giving?

2. Will you pray for your missionaries and other Cooperative Program causes each day?

Respectfully submitted, Leon Perkinson STATE MISSIONS REPORT Report 8 State Missions is a cooperative ministry by the churches to people who need God and other churches that need help. The amount of help and the success of the State Missions program is determined in a large measure by the financial support it re- ceives from each church and each association.

Missionary work at home and across North Carolina is the objective of State Mis- sions. Resort Missions are a part of this program and North Carolina ranks third na- tionally in the number of people visiting its resort and recreation areas. The Baptist Child Care Center on the Indian Reservation near Cherokee, N.C. receives support from State Missions. Our Chaplaincy program reaches institutions such as Mccain Sanatorium, Odom Correctional Institution, Cameron Morrison School, Jackson Train- ing School and others. A number of military bases in North Carolina also receive help.

Another area where State Missions plays an important role is in Church Lot Aid, Pastoral Aid, and Aid for Associations. In 1971 over $80,000 was provided for pastoral aid. That same year twenty- eight churches in North Carolina received a total of more than $30,000 dollars in financial aid. Aid for associational missionaries' sal- aries totaled $73,696 dollars. Part of our own Flat River associational missionaries' salary is paid through this program as well as a number of churches in this associa- tion have received financial help.

The Fruitland Baptist Bible Institute where almost one thousand ministers have received training in Bible, Preaching, Christian Education, etc. is directly supported by State Missions.

This is just a partial list of the many missionary activities supported by State Mission dollars. State Missions should be very important to us all because it seeks to minister to those around about us at home who need to know that Christians in their own neighborhood really care.

State Missions goal for 1972 is $300,000 dollars. The figures for this year are not complete, but in 1971- the thirty- two churches of the Flat River Association gave less than thirty-six cents per resident member per year in support of this program. Surely if we are concerned about the evils of our world and those around about us we will rededicate ourselves to greater support for this cause in the years that lie ahead of us.

Respectfully submitted, laicy A. Finch, Jr.

HOME MISSIONS REPORT Report 9 Missionaries for the Home Mission Board number 2,229. The rising cost of living and a slowdown in giving affected the mission force, which numbers close to the same figure as one year ago. However, more than 400 missionaries were appointed in all categories. Of those under appointment, approximately 900 are career missionaries, 712 associate missionaries, 64 US-2, 73 state convention staff members supported in part, and 480 pastors who receive church pastoral aid. Two-thirds of all mission- aries are jointly supported by state conventions, and only one- third are entirely sup- ported by the Home Mission Board. This mission force serves in all fifty states, Puerto Rico, and Panama.

In cooperation with the Radio and Television Commission, the Division of Evan- gelism will premier in October a weekly television presentation featuring Kenneth Chafin, the Spring Street Singers, and special guests in an unapologetic presentation of the faith leading to personal commitment. The Division of Evangelism also has added staff members for specialized work - in renewal evangelism, Reid Hardin, and in bus evangelism, William Powell.

Southern Baptist ministers served as Chaplains in the military, in hospitals, institutions, and industry. There were 573 in the military (reflecting slightly the re- duction in the armed forces - last year the total was 617); 61 in Veteran's Adminis- tration; 172 in hospital chaplaincy; 84 in institutional; and 19 in industrial chap- laincy.

In the wake of Hurricane Fern which hit the Texas coast in the fall of 1971, the Board contributed $1,000 in disaster relief. Earlier in the year, $50,000 was given to assist the California Baptist General Convention and the churches in recovering from the earthquake which shook the San Fernando Valley area in February.

A total of 960 student summer mission personnel were under appointment in 1971, 693 sponsored by the Home Mission Board and 267 by BSU. Eighty- seven of the 693 sponsored by the Board received no compensation and 21 paid their own travel ex- penses. More than 900 are expected to be appointed in 1972.

Church extension continues to receive one of the top priorities in home mission work, apd a major emphasis is planned using the theme, "Extend Now.'' Leaders continue to push for those methods which make full use of laity in leading home Bible fellowships, especially in the early growth period of a church. Also, the "Ex- tend Now" emphasis stresses the use of organized work, such as Sunday Schools, Vacation Bible Schools, and other organizations as firstunits, plus community events which help to start work within an area.

The administration of associations has been strengthened through planning, con- ducting, and evaluating the work in associations, the sharing of findings through con- ferences and publications, and through the development of associational strategy awareness conferences. The ASSOCIATIONAL ADMINISTRATION bulletin serves as an effective link in providing these services.

Respectfully submitted, Bill Quarles SOUTHERN BAPTIST FOREIGN MISSIONS Report 10 The sending out and maintaining of missionaries is primary in Southern Baptist overseas missions through the channel of the Foreign Mission Board. At the close of 1971 the Board had an overseas missionary staff of 2526. This included 169 mis- sionary associates, 5 special project medical personnel and 125 missionary journey- men.

Southern Baptist Missions and National Baptist groups in dozens of countries con- tinue to call for reinforcements in missionary personnel. Some requests are still not filled, even though they have been repeated for several years.

On January 1, 1972, Southern Baptist missionaries were under appointment to 75 geographical areas or countries.

Underlying all our missionary endeavors is the ultimate purpose of leading peo- ple to new life in Jesus Christ and the cultivation of New Testament churches. Or- ganized churches with Southern Baptist missionaries are related on mission fields now total 6045, of which more than 70 per cent are self-supporting. Work and ac- complishments reported are largely those of national churches, leaders and conven- tions, with the assistance of missionary co-workers and resources from Southern Baptists.

The program of evangelism and church development includes many types of min- istry; radio and television, English language churches, lay- leadership training, stew- ardship promotion, camps and conferences; work with women, men and youth; music, and religious education.

New recording or radio- TV studios are developing an outreach in France, Italy, Indonesia, Brazil and Ecuador. Progress has been made in camp programs in Guyana, and a new retreat and training center has been opened in the Philippines.

Publication ministries are an integral part of Southern Baptist overseas missions, as all kinds of Christian literature are provided for purposes of evangelism and the developing of vigorous church life. Our missionaries are related to publishing centers in 23 lands. Correspondence courses continue to help reach thousands with the gospel.

The training of national leaders is a prerequisite to strong indigenous churches.
Educational institutions under Baptist auspices in various lands may include all levels from kindergarten through university. Of special importance is the develop- ment of pastors and other church leaders through theological schools. New Bible schools for the training of church workers have been opened in Togo, Uganda and Austri'a. Work among students in university circles has been strengthened as several new projects were launched last year: a Baptist student center in Israel, a coffeehouse in Mexico, and an outreach library - youth center in India.

The program of hospitals and medical care serves the physical needs of thousands of people who would otherwise be neglected and often opens doors for strategic evan- gelistic witness. Construction of the Baptist hospital in Bangalore, India progressed during 1971. Day clinics in local churches and mission points are helping to meet health needs in several countries.

Benevolent ministries include agricultural programs, good will and community centers, children's homes, and direct relief work. During 1971, Southern Baptists, through the Foreign Mission Board, provided funds for victims of disease, war and natural disasters in several countries, particularly in East Pakistan, now Bangladesh.

How can we in the Flat River Baptist Association help in continuing to obey our Lord's command to spread the Cos pel a round the wo rid? We must continue to increase our gifts through the Cooperative Program; we must give sacrifically to the Lottie Moon Christmas Offering; we must pray regularly for our missionaries and for the work; we must be willing to urge our own young people to face up to God's claim upon their lives even to the point of serving Him anywhere in the world.

Respectfully submitted, Mrs. W.A. Mitchiner AMERICAN BIBLE SOCIETY Report 11 The American Bible Society shares the same concern for worldwide missions as.
the Southern Baptists. The goal of the society is to put the Bible in the hands of every person, in his own language.

The American Bible Society has adopted for its theme of 1973 "Key 73, Calling Our Continent to Christ." It is their goal to work closely with the churches of our country in encouraging the distribution of scripture to the people.

Because of the activities of the society in supplying missionaries with scripture for use in their work and its work with the local church bodies. Southern Baptists have long supported the American Bible Society. Each church or individual can make an annual gift to continue this noble work.

Respectfully submitted, James H. Carter, Jr.

EVANGELISM REPORT Report 12 It is exciting what the Holy Spirit is doing through the ministry of evangelism throughout our state and association. We thank God for pastors and lay people who attempt to witness for our Lord.



Let us rejoice in the excellent reports of revival meetings in our association dur- ing the past year. 264 baptisms and 218 other additions to our churches were reported.
It is our hope and prayer that we shall continue to have a strong increase in baptisms this coming year that many more people may experience Christ by genuine faith commitment.

Remember to pray earnestly for the leadership of the Holy Spirit in the total min- istry of evangelism in this Association, our state, our nation and our world.




One way our churches can help to relieve the financial burden of our schools is through participation in the Baptist College Scholarship Offering Day. Every year many of our churches provide student aid funds for needy North Carolina Baptist students by means of this special offering on Father's Day. Other churches prefer to include this as a regularly budgeted item. We urge more of our churches to enter into this in- vestment in our youth.

Dr. John E. Weems became the sixth president of Meredith College. He was form- erly Vice-President for Finance and Administration of Middle Tennessee State Uni- versity.

Gardner-Webb College received accreditation as a four- year college by the South- em Association of Colleges and Schools.

Seventy young men and women fromthe seven Baptist colleges worked in churches across North Carolina through the Baptist Youth Corps program. Their salaries are paid jointly by the colleges and the churches.

The inclusive enrolment of the seven North Carolina Baptist colleges during the past academic year was 16,609.

Several scholarships became available for young men and women, especially those active in their churches, during this year through several of our colleges.

Respectfully submitted, David G. Peach REPORT OF BAPTIST CHILDREN'S HOMES OF NORTH CAROLINA, INC.

Report 15 For the past eighty- seven years, North Carolina Baptists and their Children's Homes have cooperated in a program of Christian child care. The Homes have been sensitive to human need and have provided children with quality care. North Carolina Baptists have given generous and enthusiastic support to their child care ministry.

The agency is vigorously pursuing its basic purposes. Changing conditions which are affecting American life today are determining factors in the number of children needing our care and the types of care that are needed. More and more we are being asked to serve children with special needs beyond neglect and dependency. The ur- gency of the present and the challenge of the future is to find effective patterns of care for these children.

The Children's Homes continues to expand and improve its services to children and their families. Two new cottages are under construction at Broyhill Home. Mills Home is engaged in a year-long study to determine how we can best serve the children in the Piedmont Area. The Ten Star Builders' Program is enlisting the support of Eastern North Carolina for Christian child care in our area. Across the state, renewed* effort is being given to the enlistment of foster families. Expanded services to fami- lies are being initiated.

One of the major tasks confronting our work in Eastern North Carolina is the up- grading of our campus facilities to meet minimum standards for licensing our child care program. Most of our older buildings at Kennedy Home will require extensive and expensive renovation to meet these standards. Some buildings may have to be completely replaced with new structures. To meet this urgent need, we ask our Bap- 3i tist friends in Eastern North Carolina to give serious and prayerful thought to increased support for operating expenses and special bequests and donations for capital im- provements.

We believe this ministry of child care deserves the confidence and support of North Carolina Baptists. Our Baptist friends have demonstrated through their interest and support that they believe in what we are doing. The Cooperative Program con- tinues to be the life-blood of our financial support. The 1971 Thanksgiving Offering channeled $400,000 into our ministry of child care. Special gifts and bequests from individuals continue to provide for capital improvements. We deeply appreciate this generous support, and we pledge ourselves to continue this ministry of child care in the name of Christ and on behalf of North Carolina Baptists.

During the past year, thirty- one churches in the Flat River Baptist Association contributed $5,471. to the Baptist Children's Homes of N.C.

Respectfully submitted, Mrs. Earl Cobb NORTH CAROLINA BAPTIST HOMES FOR THE AGING Report 16 In our Baptist Homes for the Aging, we feel that there is no relationship more important and more blessed than the one that we have with our Baptist people in our Baptist Associations throughout the State. It is a special joy to visit personally the Associations and to have fellowship with the good people who mean so much to us in our ministry and to express to them our special thanksgiving. It is with a deep sense of gratitude to the Lord and to you that we hear this report on our Baptist Homes for 1972.

The most significant fact about this support from our Baptist people is that the major- ity of the residents in the Homes are persons with real financial needs, and these love gifts are used for their care. In fact, these gifts are a lifeline to the residents, and they could not be maintained without them. The greatest challenge to our Baptist people is to continue to increase the Special Offering from year to year, and thus keep pace with the needs of the residents who depend so greatly on it.

Our Baptist Homes now operate five resident Homes, with two located in Winston- Salem, and one each in Albemarle, Hamilton, and Yanceyville. These Homes have a capacity for 162 residents. In addition, there is a Nursing Care Unit, located in Winston- Salem, with a capacity for 63 patients. In these Homes we specialize in tender loving care and do not know of any place this side of Heaven and outside of one's home, where a person can receive more compassionate Christian care.


limited basis only, directly as patients. The majority of the beds in the Nursing Care Unit are used for the residents of the five Homes, when nursing care becomes neces- sary. We are glad that the base of our operation has been broadened to include a min- istry to the aging sick person, and we can only hope that we will move forward and be able to receive more patients in the years ahead.

Ministry to an older person is a direct ministry to Christ and is one of the finest things that North Carolina Baptists are doing. Be glad that you are a Baptist, and be grateful for the part that you are having in making the life of an aging person more abundant and more secure.

Respectfully submitted, Mrs. Lucy G. Lyles REPORT OF NORTH CAROLINA BAPTIST HOSPITAL Report 17 Good patient care continues to be the main objective of Baptist Hospital in Win- ston-Salem. It offers not just medical expertise, but an all important part of patient care is a Christian concern for the total person with all his needs.

Because no one with a real medical need is turned away regardless whether he can pay for his care, the hospital has to rely on outside financial help from concerned and caring Baptists. As Christians, our part comes in giving on Mother's Day through the Mother's Day Fund which makes it possible for those who are ineligible to receive any other kind of financial help and who cannot pay for their medical care, to receive this medical care.

Hospital costs, the latest equipment and medical care costs continue to rise, and in order for the hospital to meet these costs, and still provide care for those who cannot pay, we, as Baptist Christians must realize our obligation in this ministry to the sick.

In the past year, twenty- eight churches in the Flat River Baptist Association con- tributed $2,955 towards the Mother's Day Fund. This amount is good, yet, this year the hospital faces rising costs! So, Baptist Christians, let's realize our duty and obligation and have a very real burden in our hearts concerning this important minis- try and give more.

Respectfully submitted, Rev. James S. Talley REPORT OF THE GENERAL BOARD THE BAPTIST STATE CONVENTION OF N.C.
TO FLAT RIVER BAPTIST ASSOCIATION 1972 Report 18 The purpose and function of the General Board of the Baptist State Convention is to act for the convention in the interim between sessions, and shall have general supervision of all agencies and institutions fostered and supported by the conven- tion.

The General Board has met once a cparter as required by the constitution and the Executive Committee of the General Board has met at least once a month during the past year. It has been a busy year.

The Baptist Hospital in Winston-Salem again claimed prayerful consideration this year. It is encouraging to note that our Baptist Hospital (a medical center) is operat- ing at the present time on a sound financial basis. Dr. Cecil Sherman, chairman of the committee of 15 to study the overall operation of the Baptist Hospital and its relationship to the Baptist State Convention gave us a progress report at our June meeting. He did not bring recommendations at the time, but recommendations will be made at our October meeting and the Baptist State Convention meeting in Novem- ber.


For several Months Co-operative Program gifts have exceeded the previous year by an average of about lo^^o. This is encouraging.

Among the many items labored over by the Board during the year, I share briefly this in closing. We have recommended, and already set in motion a program of em- phasis for the Baptist churches of North Carolina for the next three years with four major goals. We encourage each church to adopt the program TOGETHER WE AD- VANCE and pledge to make every effort over the next three years (1973-1974-1975) to increase EACH year: l-lo'^o in Baptisms 2-10^0 in Sunday School enrollment 3-10% in Cooperative Program gifts 4-10^0 in Support of Associational Missions It is our prayer that North Carolina Baptists will continue to grow in knowledge and commitment to the glorious cause of Christ as we Advance Together.

Mrs. J.R, Williams Mrs. Sterling Canrington REPORT OF OBITUARY COMMITTEE Report 24 Let us pause for a few moments in our busy lives to call to remembrance our beloved dead in Christ.

From Psalm 112:1-6 we read, "Praise ye the Lord. Blessed is the man that feareth the Lord, that delighteth greatly in his commandments. His seed shall be mighty upon earth: the generation of the upright shall be blessed. Wealth and riches shall be in his house: and his righteousness endureth forever. Unto the upright there ariseth light in the darkness: He is gracious, and full of compassion, and righteous. A good man sheweth favour, and lendeth: he will guide his affairs with discretion. Surely he shall not be moved forever: the righteous shall be in everlasting remembrance.'* How wonderful to be 'in everlasting remembrance'. We can all call to mind wonder- ful remembrances of our departed loved ones. Memories are not always pleasant; for we can recall our loneliness at their leaving us and how much we miss their presence.

For the Christian, death is only a stepping stone to greater heights of joy.

As we read in the fourth verse, "Unto the upright there ariseth light in the dark- ness"; this is the promise of eternal life given to all who have accepted Christ.

I would like for you to listen to this poem entitled "EVIDENCE" by William W.
Ellis.

"While meandering one morning on the sand along the shore.
Where the breakers splashed retreatment from the fury of their roar, I was halted in my musing by some footprints on the beach - Mute but circumstantial witness, with a lesson they would teach.

Someone earlier had been there; and the surf had not effaced Tracks theii feet had left in strolling, marking so clearly where they paced; And 'twas futile to make issue with a fact so plainly shown. . .
That I was not first to be there. By the footprints that was known.

All creation is a witness: man is not the product of His inventiveness and genius. Earth about, and stars above Testify of someone greater who preceded us and planned All phenomena of Nature. These attest - and faith demand.

Here is truth for all who reason - an analogy for those Who are open to conviction and who do not faith oppose: Some One trod the way before us, leaving footprints doubt and time Have been futile at obscuring, giving evidence sublime.' ' After I have read the names of our beloved dead, will you please stand for a mo- ment of silent memorial to their remembrance and remain standing while Rev. Charles Middleton of the Stovall Baptist Church leads us in prayer.

Respectfully submitted, Mrs. Reynolds Frazier NOTE: Our departed friends and loved ones are listed on a special page in their honor and memory.

There is and always will be a great need for more and better Bible teaching in our Association.


Each of the thirty- two churches in our Association reported having Vacation Bible School this year. There was an enrolment of 2,792 with an average attendance of 2,434. One church received recognition for having a Distinguished school. Nineteen churches received Merit recognition and the remaining twelve did not report their achievement. Respectfully submitted, David Blackwell CHURCH TRAINING REPORT 1971 -1972 Report 27 "The purpose of a report", says Robert L. Bowers in Church Administration July, 1967, "is to communicate, to inform, to present facts. It should not attempt to effect action." Then he follows with six very obvious and helpful "guideline suggestions: (1) organize; (2) be brief; (3) be accurate; (4) be clear; (5) be objective; and (6) use common sense." By point of privilege your Director of Church Training wishes to take exception to Mr. Bowers' statement "It should not attempt to effect action" and to guideline (5) be objective in its explanation and application. He says "Keep opinions out of the report. Do not use slanted words, or words intended to influence a judgement." The purpose, thrust, and concern of church training, it seems, is to provide ade- quate and capable church leadership, especially in voluntary stewardship enlistment.
Interest has waned in association effort at "M" Night and at Preparation Week which used to offer considerable help. Vacation Bible School Clinics and Women of the Church Missions workshops and group planning meetings remain as a force in over- all church training collectively in Flat River Association. Some gains in church train- ing have been observed in individual church acceptance of a challenge to train. One church started weekly meetings of its workers by divisions, and by churchwide ses- sions. Curriculum study, age characteristics, teaching improvement and techniques, and regular adult Sunday School classes at night for adults were carried out. Those were both alternately and simultaneous for nine months before Summer disbandmenr.
There were enrolled 32 adults, 16 young people and 9 Sunday School pupils, all meet- ing on Sunday night. One church is preparing a questionaire type assessment of felt needs in preparing for more effective Christian growth and work. This is encouraging and well worth trying in any local church.

Respectfully submitted, ' Ray B.W. Morgan MUSIC MINISTRY REPORT Report 28 The purpose of a music program in a local church is two-fold: 1) to educate; 2) to lead in worship. With this in mind I would like for you to consider the first point, education.

We try our best to get good piano, instrumental, and voice teachers for our chil- dren and we make sure they take dancing lessons for co-ordination. Yet when it comes to our church music we decide on Wednesday night what we will sing for Sunday, we pick out songs upon our arrival at Sunday School and Church Training and hope the pianist can play them, rather than planning that which co-ordinates with the lesson and giving our pianists advance notice in order that he/she may practice them.

We do not stop to realize that the music program in our churches can be A most and I would go so far as to say THE most educative institution. For it is here that we have complete freedom as to methods and materials that we can use to help in- dividuals and choirs learn.

Reflecting over the past year and the music for the Flat River Baptist Association, the following activities were held: On December 5, 1971, A Christmas Carol Service was held at the Delrayno Bap- tist Church under the direction of the Associational Minister of Music. There were Adult and Youth Choirs participating from Delrayno, Butner, Peace's Chapel, Stovall, Hester, and Oxford Baptist Churches.


In May an associational choir festival was held at Oxford Baptist Church. Youth and Children's Choirs participated from Stovall, West Oxford and Oxford Baptist Churches.

The music program of the Flat River Baptist Association will give you pastors, music directors, pianists, and organists an opportunity to learn ways of organizing music programs, methods of working with choirs of all ages and means by which we can enhance the worship experiences of our churches through a music ministry.

Plans for the coming year are based upon observation of needs and requests made by some pastors who wish to develop their music programs in their respective church- es.

Attendant upon interest indicated by the musicians andpastors of this association, a six weeks course will be offered consisting of classes in conducting and choir methods.


Your associational music director has had the privilege of accompanying state- wide music festivals and youth music workshops for several years the latest being a choral clinic held at Hayes Barton Baptist Church in Raleigh two weeks ago. It is our dream that Directors of Music in the various churches will participate in the fes- tivals on the associational level in order that preparations may be made for your choirs and music personel to attend the music functions that our Baptist State Con- vention Music Dept. offers.

As associational Minister of Music for Flat River, I would like to challenge all church members to NOT SETTLE for the ordinary but to strive for the ultimate.

Respectfully submitted, Barbara C. E, Frazier Rev, Lewis Mcmannen Bullock Godwin HISTORY OF BUTNER BAPTIST CHURCH During 1947, after Mr. John Umstead was successful in getting the State to pur- chase the property then owned by the War Assets Administration and known as Camp Butner, a group of Residents of the area started having organized religious services in early 1948, meeting in a room in Piedmont Village.

From these first meetings, the Community Methodist Church was started, at first more or less non-denominational. Baptists in the group made pleas to the Flat River Association and also the Yates Association of Durham, but were offered very little encouragement in establishing a Baptist Church.

During these early days, a couple moved to Butner from Laurinburg, N.C. by the name of W. Edwin Bullock and Lillian Bullock. Both were originally from Creedmoor.
Mrs. Bullock had been active in the Baptist Training Union in Laurinburg and had become close friends with Mrs. Winston Pearce, whose husband. Rev. Pearce, was pastor of the First Baptist Church of Durham. Rev. Pearce was very active in the Baptist Council of Durham and Vicinity. This council was an organization composed of several churches in Durham, with their main objective being to establish missions. All of the churches involved were members of the Yates Baptist Association. Rev. B.E.
Morris, pastor of Grey Stone Baptist Church, was president of the council. Rev. Oren Bradley was the Supt. of Missions. Through the 'never die* efforts of Mrs. Lillian Bullock, a community canvas was made and the results were given to the council, after which various ministers of the council came out and held services. A week of Revival was scheduled with services beginning on Sunday afternoon, Sept. 5, 1948 at 3:00 P.M.

Services were held at the Baptist Chapel, which was Post Chapel 1, located on D Street, and one of three chapels left by the Army. A fruitful Revival was held and two weeks later, on Sunday, September 19, 1948, Butner Baptist Church was organized by The Baptist Council of Durham and Vicinity, and accepted into the Yates Association of Durham. The Baptist Council of Churches purchased the Post Chapel from the War Assets Administration to be used for a Baptist Church in Butner and gave it to the Butner Baptist Church, at no cost, at the time of organization. An old dining hall was later purchased and moved to the site to serve as Sunday School Departments. The land was leased from the State and later purchased. A home across from the present Fire Station was purchased for a parsonage which was in turn traded for the old parson- age building next to the church. Edwin Bullock was the first Chairman of Deacons.
Rev. Oren Bradley was called as interim pastor until the first pastor. Rev. Edwin R.
Andrews, was called on January 1, 1949. He stayed until August, 1949. Rev. Clyde Melton was called in December, 1949, but an auto accident kept him from the pulpit until March. 1950. He was with the church until December, 1950. Rev. Hal M. Helms followed him in August, 1951 and remained until July 1, 1954. During Rev. Helms pastorate, the church petitioned Flat River Association for membership, at which time the church came under watchcare until a letter of recommendation was received from the Yates Association.

Rev. Harold Lester was pastor in 1955. On November 1, 1956 Rev. Warren Bush was called as pastor and remained until June 30, 1970. During Rev. Bush's pastorate, three buildings were built. In 1960 Unit I, w'hich is the Children's Building was built; in May, 1965 a new Parsonage was built; and in November, 1967 Unit II of the Educa- tional Plant was built. This building included the Youth Department, Fellowship Hall and Kitchen. On January 3, 1971 Rev. David G. Peach was called as pastor. He is the present pastor. On September 24, 1972 ground was broken for the new Sanctuary and additional educational space.

--Information gathered from Church Records, Members and Former Members, Former Pastors, Former Leaders of the Baptist Council of Churches and Residents of Butner. N.C.



Mrs. A.J. Blackwell of Mountain Creek Baptist Church gave the devotion for the afternoon and Oxford Baptist Church presented a special feature on the superintendent of missions. Miss Ann Johnson read the minutes and gave a treasurer's report which were accepted as read.

Mrs. Hesley Clark, chairman of Time and Place Committee reported that the annual spring meeting for 1973 will be held at Amis Chapel Baptist Church and the leadership conference for 1973 will be held at Hester Baptist Church.

Miss Claudette Hilton, of Hester Baptist Church presented a most inspiring vocal rendition of "He" and "I Will Tell the World I Am A Christian".

Mrs. Hugh Mckinley missionary to Africa was the missionary speaker for the af- ternoon.

The evening session opened with congregation singing. The devotion was pre- sented by Mrs. William Finch of the West Oxford Baptist Church. The Enon Trio pre- sented special music for the evening.

Mrs. Lawrence Davis, introduced the speaker for the evening, Mrs. Gilmer Cross, President of WMU of the Baptist State Convention of North Carolina. Mrs. Cross con- tinued the thoughts of "Living the Spirit of Christ In Expectancy and Creativity".

The meeting adjourned at 8:00 P.M. with Rev. Dan Page superintendent pronounc- ing the benediction.O.P. Home Missions 5 Pastors Conf., Fellowship 6 Chatter Club 11-18 Youth Week 11 Home Missions Day in S.S.

Currin, Josephine Currin, West End Baptist Church Constitution FLAT RIVER BAPTIST ASSOCIATION PREAMBLE For the declaration of its fundamental purposes and the permanent guidance and control of its activities, this body does adopt the following as its constitution, to wit: ARTICLE 1 — NAME The name of this body is the Flat River Baptist Association of North Carolina.

ARTICLE 2 — OBJECTIVES Section 1. The Objectives of the Association shall be to foster evangelism, missions and benevolences. Christian Education and such other objects as may be promoted by the North Carolina Baptist State and Southern Baptist Convention, and the Baptist World Alliance; to preserve correct statistics of the Churches, to promote harmony and give Christian counsel in case of difficulty in and among the Churches upon their invitation; and to devise and recommend measures for programs of common interest to all the churches.

ARTICLE 3 — MEMBERSHIP Section 1 . The Association shall be composed of all the pastors of the churches of the Association and messengers elected by the churches. Each church is entitled to three messengers for the first one hundred members or fraction thereof and one additional messenger for each additional fifty members or fraction thereof; providing no church shall have more than ten messengers.

Section 2. Any Missionary Baptist Church, which has been a member in good standing for a period of twelve months or more with another Missionary Baptist Association, and any new i Missionary Baptist Church seeking admission to this body must present its application to the Executive Committee not less than six months prior to the annual meeting, and shall be received into the Association upon a majority vote of the members present.

ARTICLE 4 — ORGANIZATION Section 1. The officers of the Association shall be a Moderator, Vice-Moderator, Clerk, Treasurer, Assistant Treasurer, Auditor, Church Training Director, Sunday School Director, Woman's Missionary Union Director, Brotherhood Director, Director of Church Music, Direc- tor of Visual Aids, Historian (or Historians), Three Trustees, and Superintendent of Missions, and Parliamentarian. These officers shall be elected at each annual session and shall hold office for one year, or until their successors in office shall be filled to respective offices, except the Superintendent of Missions whose term of office is indefinite, and the Trustees who will serve three-year terms on a rotation basis with one being elected each year for a three-year term. The Moderator and Vice-Moderator cannot succeed themselves more than one term. All terms of office shall begin immediately before adjournment of the annual meeting of the Association.

Section 2. The Moderator and Vice-Moderator shall be elected from the floor at the beginning of the second session of the first day. The other offices (except the Superintendent of Missions) as named in Article 4, Section 1, shall be elected by the Association after receiving nominations from the Nominating Committee, and after giving opportunity for nominations from the floor of the Association.
I In the event of the resignation or death of the Moderator or Vice-Moderator, the Clerk shall call the Executive Committee into session to elect someone to fill the vacancy for the unexpired term.


ARTICLE 7 — DUTIES OF COMMITTEES Section 1. A committee of committees, composed of five from different churches, shall nominate five members for the Nominating Committee, three members for the Time, Place and Preacher Committee, three for the Resolutions Committee, three for Obituary Committee, thirteen for Missions- Advisory Committee, seven for the Evangelism Committee, five for Budget Committee, six for Program Committee, eight for Calendar Committee, ten for Ordina- tion Committee, four for Christian Life Committee, four for Camp Committee, and three for Constitution Committee and submit them for election by the Association at the opening session.
These committees shall be composed of pastors and lay persons from the different churches.

Section 2. The Nominating Committee named at the Annual Associational Meeting shall nominate all officers during the year, including the nominees to be elected at the following annual meeting, except the Moderator and Vice-Moderator whose election is described in Article 4, Section 2. The Sunday School Director, Church Training Director, Woman's Missionary Union Director and the Brotherhood Director shall be nominated for election by the Executive Commit- tee at its July meeting. Other officers to be elected at the annual association are Clerk, Treasurer, Assistant Treasurer, Auditor, Director of Church Music, Director of Audio- Visual Aids, Historians, three Trustees, any other officer the Association may decide it needs, and the following representatives who will report to the annual Association: State Missions, Home Missions, Foreign Missions, Baptist Colleges, Relief and Annuity Board, N.C. Baptist Hospital, N.C. Baptist Homes, and other objects or agencies of denominational interest.

Section 3. Duties of Time, Place and Preacher Committee shall be to announce the time and place of the next annual meeting of the Association and to recommend the time and place of the annual meeting of the Association five years in advance and other meetings of the Association.
They shall recommend the preacher and the alternate for the Annual Sermon for the next year and report to the Association.

Section 4. Duties of Resolutions Committee shall be to study all resolutions presented to the Association on the first day and report to the Association on the second day, and form all expressions of courtesy.

Section 5. Duties of the Obituary Committee shall be to form all expressions of memorials.

Section 6. The duties of the Missions Advisory Committee shall be to study the missionary needs in the Association, counsel with the Superintendent of Missions in promoting the adopted program of work, recommend projects with which the Churches of the Association may cooper- ate. The Superintendent of Missions shall be responsible to this Committee. This committee should be representative of all phases of the associational program. The Sunday School Director, Church Training Director, Woman's Missionary Union Director, and the Brotherhood Director shall serve as members of this committee.

Section 7. Duties of the Evangelism Committee shall be to work with the pastors. Superin- tendent of Missions, Executive Committee and churches of the Association to promote simul- taneous revivals, warehouse revivals, institutional evangelism, and evangelistic conferences and seek to stimulate a consciousness of evangelism needs within the Association.

Section 8. The Program Committee shall be responsible for planning the meetings of the Association. This committee shall be composed of the Moderator, Vice-Moderator, Music Director, immediate past Moderator, and pastors of the host churches.

REPORT OF THE EXECUTIVE COMMITTEE Report 2 As a matter of helpful information for all of us, I want to begin this report by quoting some sentences from the constitution of the Flat River Baptist Association that pertain to the composi- tion of the Executive Committee: "The Executive Committee is composed of all active persons, and officers, all chairmen of all standing committees of the Association along with one elected representative from each of the churches in the Association and such persons who will report to the annual associational meeting on the various objects and agencies of denominational interest. It is required by the constitution to meet at least once each quarter to 'carry on the work of the Association between sessions, work out the budget, and present recommendations and reports to the Association.' The mod- erator serves as chairman. I happened to be sitting at the end of the table and there being no one else to pass it to, I became chairman. Mrs. Lawrence Davis was elected secretary.

Mr. Earl Howard reported that in January, 1973 his salary as Director of the Butner Christian Social Ministries would be paid equally by the Association and the Bapfist State Convention of North Carolina.

The committee voted that the then functioning Selections Committee be paid ten cents per mile plus meals and any other expenses incurred in securing a Superintendent of Missions.

At our second meeting on March 22, 1973 upon recommendation of Mr. Ernest Upchurch of the City and Metropolitan Missions Department, Baptist State Convention, our committee voted to recommend to the Execufive Committee that Mr. Howard's salary be increased to $1,800.00 per year becoming effective in 1974. It was also agreed to extend our assistance to this ministry through the calendar year 1973 as our associafional calendar year ends in October.

There were reports from each committee and ex-officio member. It was especially rewarding to hear of the New WMU work organized at the Brassfield Baptist Church.

Now, to speak of the extra meeting of this committee. Our Selections Committee announced that the Lord had lead them to the man they believed to be the one who would be our Superintend- ent of Missions. After a question and answer session by our committee, Mrs. Robert Wainwright was accepted and a motion made to recommend him to the Executive Committee.

Our deep appreciation was expressed to the Selections Committee for a job well done in finding Mr. Wainwright for us with God's guidance.

The remaining moments of the meeting were spent in discussing our various problems in our own churches and organizations. One of our members expressed concern that we might frighten Mr.
Wainwright away even before he finished unpacking.


Mr. Wainwright told us of his need for a filing cabinet and the desire that the possibility of purchasing an electric mimeograph machine and stencil burner. We recommended that the trustees purchase this cabinet and look into the purchase of the other equipment.

A discussion concerning the voting power of the ex-officio members of this committee followed. The voting members recommend to the Constitution Committee that an amendment be made enabling them to vote at the Missions Advisory Committee meetings. Carrying out the mission tasks is of primary importance in helping the church move toward its objective.

Now, may we reflect over the past year with the following WMU activities: On March 27, 1973 the Annual WMU Spring Meeting was held at the Amis Chapel Baptist Church. Mrs. George Braswell, missionary from Iran and Mrs. Oren Bradley, an approved state worker were our speakers. Each bringing a most imformative and challenging message.

Under the direction of Mrs. Frances Morton, Baptist Women's Director, a prayer retreat was held on May 7, 1973 at the Tabbs Creek Baptist Church. This proved to be a very inspiring service.

The Baptist Young Women under the direction of Mrs. Wayne Young, Director, attended a banquet at Henderson Motel on April 12, 1973.

The associational Acteens Rally was held on April 30, 1973 using the theme, "You've Got A Lot To Live".

On May 5, 1973 at the Dexter Baptist Church under the direction of Mrs. Lucy Lyles, Girls In Action Director, a most successful Day Camp was held with approximately 160 in attendance.
Directors and leaders of Mission Friends organizations in our churches enjoyed two delight- ful evenings of fellowship in November and February under the direction of Mrs. Howard Frazier.

In observance of WMU Focus Week, morning devotions were given over radio station WOXF by members of the WMU Council.

Our association was represented at the State WMU Workshop at Mundo Vista in September by several directors of the organizations.

At the state WMU meeting on March 13 and 14 of this year in Raleigh, our Association was recognized as having achieved the Distinguished Recognition for 1971-72. Several of our churches within our Association received recognition also.

The Leadership Conference held in September at the Hester Baptist Church was well attended.

In February, 1973, WMU work was begun again at the Brassfield Baptist Church. This gives all of the churches in the Association but two with WMU organizations.

The WMU associational goal is concerned with more organizations. We all know that organizations themselves are useless. But count WMU organizations in our churches in terms of lives influenced for missions, of the prayer potential of members, of the millions and millions of dollars given for missions because of the way God has blessed Woman's Missionary Union. Then we see that we must be done with a half-hearted effort. Surely God expects that we match our efforts with the magnitude of opportunities today.

If we want to do God's will, we may have to change our way of thinking and begin to see things as God would have us to see them. Serving the Lord in WMU is one of the greatest joys imaginable. Pray for us as we serve.

Respectfully submitted, ( Mrs. Lawrence Davis, Assn. WMU Director BROTHERHOOD REPORT Report 6 What is Brotherhood? If someone should ask you this question, could you answer them? The objective of Brotherhood in a Baptist church is to support the church in it's tasks of leading them to a deeper commitment to missions, to a more meaningful prayer life for missions, to a larger stewardship on behalf of missions, and to a personal involvement in missions.

In Matthew 28:19-20, Jesus told the disciples, "Go ye therefore, and teach all nations, baptizing them in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Ghost: Teaching them to observe all things whatsoever I have commanded you; and lo, I am with you always even unto the end of the world." The Brotherhood starts with the men and boys in the local church, teaching them missions and then working out into the community, and on to people who are not Christians.

To carry out this objective the Brotherhood fulfills four tasks for a church. They are: (1) teach missions; (2) engage in mission action; (3) support world missions through praying and giving; and (4) provide and interpret information regarding the work of the church and denomi- To help accomplish these tasks this year we were engaged in the following activities: for the boys we had a Royal Ambassador Conclave with about 100 people involved. We had an associational Baseball League with 126 boys in seven teams; also there were training sessions for RA counselors and leaders and Baptist Men officers. We had a banquet for Baptist Men and their wives with 160 attending and having to turn away 80 for lack of room. At our next banquet we hope to have enough room to seat everyone who wishes to come.

Your associational officers are ready to help you in anyway possible and at any time. We look forward to working with each of you and our new Superintendent of Missions, Mr. Mr. Greene, a product of this Association, has worked fervently to promote the great needs of our convention in the churches of this state. We are especially thankful for his dedicated service.

Respectfully submitted, Rev. David D. Blakely STATE MISSIONS REPORT Report 8 What does Acts 1 :8 say to you? To many it reads, "You shall be witnesses to me both in your community, and in all of your state, and in adjoining states, and unto the uttermost part of the earth." In North Carolina, two-thirds of each home missions ministry is actually financed by state missions. In other words, when you come across a ministry in our state which may be labeled, "Home Missions," two-thirds of the money comes from state missions.

Associational missions is also related to state missions. In 1973 for example, $75,000 state missions money will go toward associational missions projects. All total, fifty-six of the eighty North Carolina associations receive some financial support from state missions.

State, home and associational missions, therefore are NOT programs of duplication but rather cooperative ministries which reflect the best stewardship of Baptist money.

Respectfully submitted, Johnny Brummitt HOME MISSIONS REPORT Report 9 The number of missionaries for the Home Mission Board was 2,255 at the end of March, 1973. The Board had to choose between increasing support of present home missionaries or enlarging the missionary force.


The Board and its missionaries keep in their minds that their purpose is leading people to follow Jesus Christ as Lord and Saviour no matter what the condition or circumstances.

In the past year the plans were completed for the production and distribution nationwide color evangelistic television program. It was entitled, "Spring Street USA". Twenty stations started broadcasting the program. They hope to increase to one hundred stations. As time goes on the program goes on, the program will meet such a need that will become self supporting.

There are 882 Military and civilian chaplains. Southern Baptist ministers served as chaplains in military, hospital, institutional, and industrial capacities. There were 57 veteran administra- tion, 183 hospital, 88 institutional, and 15 industrial chaplains.

The Annie Armstrong Easter Offering increased from $714,152. to $6,137,612. This was the first time special offering has exceeded the total Cooperative Program contributions to the Board.

The past year proved to be a great year for evangelism because there were 450,000 baptisms reported. It was the largest number of lay people involved in home witnessing.

Promotional plans were completed and materials prepared for the "Witness Now" and "Share the Word Now" project for 73-74 denominational emphasis.

We are grateful for the excellent support provided by Southern Bapfists. The Board is seeking to use available funds in the most productive way possible in crossing barriers of many kinds to reach people for Christ in the homeland.

Respectftiuy submitted, Mrs. Curtis Wilson FOREIGN MISSIONS Report 10 Missionary Support The sending out and maintaining of missionaries is primary in Southern Baptist overseas missions through the channel of the Foreign Mission Board. At the close of 1972 the Board had an overseas missionary staff of 2,507. This included 174 missionary associates, 2 special project medical personnel, and 128 missionary journeymen. Appointments during the year totaled 171 (83 career, 1 1 associates, 66 journeymen, 2 special project medical workers, and 9 career reappointments).

Southern Baptist Missions (organizations of missionaries) and national Baptist groups in dozens of countries continue to call for reinforcements in missionary personnel. Some requests are still not filled, even though they have been repeated for several years and are still urgent.

Evangelism and Church Development Underlying all our missionary endeavors is the ultimate purpose of leading people to new life in Jesus Christ and the cultivation of New Testament churches. Organized churches with which Southern Baptist missionaries are related on mission fields now total 6,554, of which more than 75 percent are self-supporting. Work and accomplishments reported are largely those of national churches, leaders, and conventions, with the assistance of missionary co-workers and resources from Southern Baptist.

The program of evangelism and church development includes many types of ministry: radio and television, English-language churches, lay-leadership training, stewardship promotion, camps and conferences; work with women, men, and youth; music, and religious education.

Hospitals and Medical Care The program of hospitals and medical care serves the physical needs of thousands of people who would otherwise be neglected and often opens strategic doors for evangelistic witness.
During 1972, missionaries and national workers engaged in this ministry treated nearly 55,000 inpatients and saw more than 890,000 outpatients.

Contributions At the end of 1972, Southern Baptists' contributions for foreign missions totaled $35,439,5 14. The 1972 Lottie Moon Christmas Offering goal was $ 18 million - an increase over the $17,833,810 contributed (as of May 1, 1972) through the 1971 offering.

As we learn more of the needs of our Foreign Missions, we must realize, all of us that have the gospel, it is our responsibility to share it with the world.

Our Lottie Moon Christmas Offering will be soon, and as we set our goals for our church j please remember this is the centennial of Lottie Moon sailing to China to begin her long missionary stay and hard work and sacrifice she made. She gave $ 1 ,000 of her own money to help I; the Foreign Mission Board send most needed workers on the field. Later she gave all her savings I and in the end her life for the cause of Christ. Our goal for Southern Baptist Christmas Offering is j $20,000,000. We must pray and increase our offering. Then our missionaries will say again, ! "Thank you Southern Baptists". Aulick Keeton Mr. F. As of June 1, 1973, in Flat River Association, 25 churches and the Association were participating in the retirement program. For assistance and information concerning your churches involvement in the Annuity Program you should contact; Southern Baptist Annuity I Board, 511 North Akard Building, Dallas Texas 75201.

BAPTIST COLLEGES Report 16 I Churches and Colleges Working Together I In North Carolina, Baptist churches and Baptist colleges enjoy a supportive partnership. It is still true that most of our pastors, missionaries, church staff members and denominational ; leaders are educated in Baptist colleges. Another outstanding example of church-college cooper- jation is the Baptist Youth Corps, which provided dedicated student leadership for more than i; eighty North Carolina Baptist churches during the summer of 1973. On campus, the college presidents interpret to the faculty and staff that our churches and colleges play important, J complementary roles in the Christian missionary enterprise.

Cooperative Program Your North Carolina Baptist colleges continue to be grateful for the support which comes through the Cooperative Program. During 1973, more than two million dollars will be given to support Christian higher education. Your colleges share this support as follows: Campbell College - $ 340,721 Chowan College 212,951 Gardner- Webb College 249,153 Mars Hill College 296,002 Meredith College 296,002 Wake Forest University .- 517,471 Wifigate College 217,210 $2,129,510 The Importance of Baptist Students The colleges make a special effort to recruit North Carolina Baptist students. In fact, no qualified student is turned down in any of our colleges because of inability to pay. The churches help to make this possible through the Cooperative Program and the Baptist College Scholarship Offering on Father's Day. Baptist colleges stress the importance of the individual student, and are anxious to enroll the youth from our churches who plan to attend college. Churches can help their students and their colleges by encouraging interested young people to apply for enrollment in one of the seven North Carolina Baptist colleges.

Council on Christian Higher Education Since 1945, the cooperative efforts and relationships of North Carolina Baptist colleges have been coordinated by the Council on Christian Higher Education of the Baptist State Convention.
The Council office is eager to assist both the churches and the colleges as together they help to create persons and a world as Christ would have them.

Respectfully submitted, David Peach REPORT OF N. C. BAPTIST CHILDREN'S HOMES INC.

Report 17 Christian Child Care is a creative opportunity. The Baptist Children's Homes of North Carolina is committed to quality care which nurtures the whole person, physically, mentally, emotionally, socially, and spiritually. It is our hope that each child in our care will have the opportunity to develop the full potential which is his by God's creation.

Hundreds of dependent children are served from three primary centers of operation, namely: Kennedy Home in the East; Mills Home in the Piedmont; and Broyhill Home in the West. From these centers North Carolina Baptists provide group care, foster home care, family services, preschool education, higher education, and maternity care.

In Eastern North Carolina the Ten Star Builders Program continues to offer exciting opportunities for a great host of friends to share in developing modern facilities and the most up-to-date services for needy children and families. Although a new cottage for children is being constructed on the Kennedy Home campus, the major emphasis of the Ten Star Builders Program for 1973-74 will be in the direction of the expansion of Odum Home at Pembroke, North Carolina. A challenge gift has already been promised for the construction of a Recreation Building. A campaign is under way this fall to raise funds for two family-style cottages for children and a residence for the Director.

In Eastern North Carolina we have a strong and growing group of families who have opened their hearts and homes to foster children. However, from time to time we have a need for additional foster homes for some children who have special needs which cannot be met ade- quately in group care. If you are interested, write or call our nearest office.

The Baptist Children's Homes of North Carolina has always operated on a very conserva- tive budget. For this reason we have very little margin from which to cope with today's high cost of living. Therefore we make a strong appeal to our Baptist people and other friends for increased financial support to the Cooperative Program, the Tranksgiving Offering, and special gifts. A fifteen percent increase is desperately needed in order to enable us to continue an effective ministry at the present level. It shall be our purpose in the future, as in the past, to keep faith with our Baptist people in providing a Christian Home and way of life for the boys and girls we serve.
Thank you for the numerous ways you help to make this ministry possible.

Respectfully submitted, Mrs. Billy Tilley !

REPORT OF N. C. BAPTIST HOMES FOR THE AGING Report 18 As Baptists we are sharing in one of the most meaningful ministries in North Carolina when we have a part in contributing to our North Carolina Homes for the Aging. There have been distinctive blessings in these twenty-two years of this ministry, particularly last year.

Our realization that working together in this special ministry to older people is a great venture of faith in God. Through our cooperation God has increased one Home to five resident Homes. There are two in Winston-Salem; — (Resthaven and the Hayes Homes); one in Al- bemarle; one in Hamilton; and one in Yancey ville. One hundred sixty-two residents can be taken care of with a Nursing Care Unit in Winston-Salem that can accommodate 63 patients and serve all of the residents of the five Homes when nursing care is needed. The income of four out of every five residents is very limited.

Let us not forget that our love gifts furnish a supplement to their care. We want to look to the Lord confinually for His great love in this venture of faith.

Through our gifts we have contributed $185. per month for the majority of these residents in our five Baptist Homes. We have contributed 20 per cent of the total income of the Homes through this Special Offering. We gave ten per cent of the total budget through the Cooperative Program. This ministry is a distinctive binding tie that keeps us close together.

Remember there can be no finer care given on this earth than this ministry in our Baptist Homes for the Aging. This is done in the name of Christ and everything should be done to give our best to our Lord in this way.

Pray for this work, let's go and visit them, and continue liberally and loyally to share in this ministry to the Aging in the future.

On February 22, 1973 our association engaged in a one night Sunday School Revival. There were over three hundred fifty in attendance at the Oxford Baptist Church with twenty-six churches participating. A message on commitment was delivered by Dr. John Eddins and numerous commitment cards were signed in relationship to the challenges presented.

Each of the thirty-two churches in our association reported having Vacation Bible School this year. Oxford Baptist Church also had a Mission Bible School. There were 2,757 enrolled in Vacation Bible School in our association with a total mission offering of $838.37. There were nineteen merit schools and four advanced schools reported. The report of Vacation Bible Schools were much better than earlier years and we hope there will continue to be much more advance- ment in the coming years.


It is disappointing to the Flat River Association, in my opinion, that only 140 people are involved in a Sunday Night Church Training Program. It must not be said that Sunday Night is the only place to train leaders and encourage growth, rather the church should be involved in every activity, in training and leadership.


:, Respectftilly submitted, Rev. Danny Collins Church Training Director MUSIC REPORT Report 22 Music is a language of religion. The relationship of music to worship dates back to the time of the Old Testament. In II Chronicles 5:13, 14 we read "It came even to pass as the trumpeters and singers were as one, to make one sound to be heard in praising and thanking the Lord; and when they lifted up their voice with the trumpets and cymbals and instruments of music, and praised the Lord, saying. For he is good; for his mercy endureth forever: that then the house was filled with a cloud, even the house of the Lord." That the early Christians used music for the purposes of Christian education is evident in Paul's letter to the Colossians, Chapter 16.

It is said that Bach had to walk 100 miles to hear the great organist Buxtehude play. We are living in a time of pure convenience. Opportunities for training in music are all around us and I encourage you Pastors and congregations to encourage and make possible further training for your choir directors and organists.

In December we will have a carol sing that we hope all churches of the association will participate in. The date is December 2nd at three o'clock at Butner First Baptist Church. Next May there will be a music festival for all choirs. It is our hope that all churches will again be represented. Plans are being formulated for some conducting classes for the choir directors training classes in children's choir leadership, and a church music associational school. We also encourage congregations to send their music directors and organists and/or pianists to music weeks at Caswell and Ridgecrest. It is our hope that by working together in the association we can raise the standards of music in all churches and further the development of children's choirs.

Respectfully submitted, Mrs. Barbara C. This is a tender and sacred hour; however, it should not be one of mourning, but of praise and thanksgiving for the victories won!

We will read a portion of Revelation 7:9-17. Here John gives us a beautiful picture of Heaven and the joy and happiness there for those who are faithful until the end. By faith in our Lord Jesus Christ we believe that we will be gathered together with our dear loved ones around the throne of God, where we can sing praises and worship Him throughout eternity.

The names of the deceased members will be read at this time, after which the congregation will join in singing the first verse of "When The Roll Is Called Up Yonder I'll Be There", followed with the first verse of ' 'When We All Get To Heaven, What a Day of Rejoicing that will be", and then, we will sing the last verse of "Amazing Grace"; "When we've been there ten thousand years bright shining as the sun, we've no less days to sing God's praise than when we first begun." The Memorial Service will be concluded with prayer beginning with silent prayer, as a tribute to the memory of our beloved servants of God, Rev. W. I., Hester Baptist Church 3. Larry Mccracken, Knotts Grove Baptist Church Upon the recommendation of this committee, the above men were ordained.
