Blakely, Chairman Committee on Ordination ADDRESS BY SUPERINTENDENT OF MISSIONS Report 28 ' I am happy to be here, serving as Superintendent of Missions of the Flat River Baptist Association. I want to take this opportunity to thank you for the support you give to the association, both financial and in the work of the association.

I want to call your attention to the 1973 Digest of Letters from the churches. You will find that we have all pluses, that is increases except in these columns — they are Sunday School enrollment; Brotherhood enrollment and bapfisms. That indicates that we are giving more money but we are winning less. This should be a real concern to us. It is a real indication that we need to Share the Word Now.

I Corinthians 6:20 — "We belong to God, we are His, He bought us with a price." When we walked down the aisle and made our profession of faith and joined the church, we were saying publicly, "Lord, Come into my heart and rule my life, I am turning it over to you. I have made a mess of things. I want you to take charge of my life, from this point onward I want to live for you." He receives us as we are with our filty rags about us or as little children which many of us literally were. We had no idea what our commitment meant, but we knew His love and the forgiveness of our sins.

He doesn't expect us to stay in our filthy rags or our childlike faith. To belong to God, to know His love through Christ means to become like him, one who knows love and shows love.

As you walk through the pages of the New Testament you see on every side of you the way that Jesus showed love. The way He shared the Word.

He healed the leper; He ate with sinners and outcasts of society. He gave sight to the blind; He healed the demon possessed, He called the little children to come to Him; He forgave the woman caught in adultery, He fed the hungry; He comforted the lonely; He gave His life on a cross.

In Jeremiah 37: 17 the king sent for Jeremiah fi'om prison and asked, 'Ts there any word from the Lord?" The Living Bible reads, "The king asked him if there were any recent message from the Lord." All of us need to ask if there is any recent message from the Lord. Each church should ask if there is any recent message from the Lord. Are there some things that God wants me to do today?
Are there some things that God wants our church to do this week, this year?

Too often we haven't asked if there is a word from the Lord. We have asked "What did we do last year?' ' What does brother Jones think we should do? What is the church over there doing? Or even more sadly, we haven't asked anything at all, we have just done it the same old way that it has been done before.

Have we asked if we should enlarge our Sunday School by adding some new classes and reaching some new people?

Have we asked if we should increase our gifts to the Cooperative Program and other mission causes?

Have we asked if God wants us to launch out on some new area of ministry to his children to share the Word?

Such as to prisoners; to campers; to alcoholics; to people who cannot read; to the lonely people of the community; to our troubled youth; to couples whose marriage is breaking up?


Ministering to the needs of people as Jesus did means more than serving the needs of those who are members of our church. It means more than visiting the sick members of your church: more than visiting the shutin members of your church more than comforting the bereaved members of your church more than helping a needy family which are members of your church more than having proper worship services each Sunday.

As important as all of these things are, the ministry of Jesus indicates much more. Jesus went about doing good where very often we just go about. We wander from one meaningless activity to another filling up time when Jesus has called us to fill our lives full of meaning by serving people.

Could it be that we are so absorbed in balancing the budget, finding teachers for the Sunday School and planning meetings that we have come to think that a church is being the body of Christ if it is operating efficiently? The truth is that it is being the body of Christ only if it accomplishes what a church is meant to accomplish.

When John sent his disciples to ask Jesus if he were ". . . he who is to come . . ."(Matthew 1 1 :3), Jesus didn't answer, ' Tell John I have selected 1 2 disciples and they are all assigned to one of the church programs. We have surveyed the neighborhood the lame walk, lepers are cleansed and the deaf hear, and the dead are raised up, and the poor have good news preached to them." He was doing what the Messiah was supposed to do and what He called His church to do. He didn't have to give John a direct answer to His question because what He was doing told who He was.

Does what we are doing plainly say that we are the church, the body of Christ, or is it a little hard for those who are looking at us to tell who we are?

(Enemy in the Pew, p. 20) "Referring to "the glory of the Church in the early age of the Apostles", Samuel H. Miller says: "It is true that they had no great buildings. They met in synagogues. They met in Roman basilicas. They met in all kinds of hand-me-down places. They met in houses and homes and even in jails; but that was a great Church! . . . They did not care whether they were dispossessed or not. They did not care whether they were respectable or not. They had something that the world had never given them. They did not care whether they had churches or not. They did not care, in a sense, whether they lived or not. They were arrested, thrown into jails, martyred; they were persecuted, hounded, shamed, and disgraced. All the while they sang their hymns and testified: "This thing that we have is more valuable than anything we ever knew. We would not exchange it for wealth or respectability or prestige. We will die first." That's true Christian Commitment. That's the kind of commitment we all want and we all know that Crhist has called us to. It's the kind of commitment we must have if we are going to minister to the needs of people as Jesus did. It's the kind of commitment we cannot have unless we are willing to minister to the needs of people as Jesus did.

There is no way that we can plead ignorance to the needs of people or that we can say we don't have the resources to meet the needs of people. But too often we don't act in the face of need. We are as irresponsible as the man who was reported as playing golf on the Bel- Air Golf Course when homes near the course were on fire. The people from the burning houses began to gather near the green where he was playing while their homes went up in smoke. The golfer screamed at one of the women, "Stop sobbing while I'm putting." Do we scream at the world to stop sobbing its message of lostness and hurt because it bothers us and disturbs our little games?

Jesus tells us that when you have done it unto one of the least of these my brethren you have done it unto me.

Paul in writing to the Galatians, tells them about the change that came about in his life when he met Christ that day on the road to Damascus. He said the Christians in Judea didn't even know what he looked like. All they knew was what people were saying, that ' 'our former enemy is now preaching the very faith he tried to wreck . ' ' Then he says , "and they gave glory to God becau se of me." (Galatians 1:23-24) This to me would be the greatest compliment I could have, to be able to say "they gave glory to God because of me. " This will be said of all of us if we are doing what Christ has called us to do.
We will be able to say with Jesus, "Go tell John what you see and hear." We belong to God, we are his. He bought us with a price. I Cor. 6:20.

Robert H. Wainwright REPORT OF THE BUTNER CHRISTIAN SOCIAL MINISTRY in the Flat River Baptist Association Report 29 The Butner Christian Social Ministry, sponsored jointly by the Flat River Baptist Associa- tion and the City and Metropolitan Division of the Baptist State Convention of North Carolina, was begun October 1, 1972 under the direction of Earl W. Howard, Pastor of the Mount Zion Baptist Church. The work has made notable progress in bringing the churches of the Flat River Baptist Association and the Butner institutions; (i.e. John Umstead Hospital for the mentally ill; Umstead Youth Center for 'first offender' male youth; North Carolina Center for the blind; C. A. Dillon Scool for youth rehabilitation; and Murdoch Center for multi/Mental Retardees), together for a meaningful service relationship.

Our fifth Sunday visitation program at Murdoch has shown excellent growth with several of our churches serving on a regular basis and others coming into the program as opportunity prevails. Special programs at Murdoch in the summer months met with excellent support by volunteers and money and other needed materials. It is exciting to report that of the 300 residents who needed outside assistance to attend camp for a week, more than 275 were provided for through your generous, caring contributions.

We continue to have excellent response for the Sunday School and preaching station at the Dillon School. We have recently met with administration personnel in an effort to provide in-service training for volunteers who wish to work with these juvenile offenders.

A recent program started at Alcoholic Rehabilitation Center on Monday of each week has several pastors of local churches learning something about alcoholism, alcoholics and related problems and treatment, and resources for the communities to use in assisting people with this problem.

Through the generous concern of many individuals and groups of our association we have provided several hundred copies of the New Testament, books, magazines, personal items, clothing, and numerous other useful articles to the Butner institutions residents. For these many services we are most grateful and we will continue to pray and work and inform you of the many wonderful ministry opportunities that are yours through BCSM.

Butner Christian Social Ministry is NOT a program, NOT institutions, but PEOPLE who need and respond to your Christian concern.

R. R. Pulley Walter Mitchell Oren Elms Warren Bush Clarence Godwin Lemar Wheeler Talmage Williams David Blakely Lewis Mcmannen Tom Lolley HISTORY OF BRASSFIELD BAPTIST CHURCH I Brassfield Church was organized on the 30th day of August, 1823, one hundred fifty years J ago by William Worrell, James Weathers, and Zachariah Allen. The church was named for Caleb Brassfield, who donated one acre of land to build the church on, and afterwards moved to Weakley County, Tennessee. This acre donated by Caleb Brassfield is at the northern end of the ! church property, now used as a church cemetery. The first church was a log building situated on this acre of land and called "Brassfield Meeting House." In regard to William Worrell, we know very little as we find no mention of his name in any of the church proceedings after its organization and we have never heard of any one of that name ever living in this community. The descendants of James Weathers were very active members of the church in its early history, but all have been away from the community for a number of years and at this time there are none of his descendants members of this church. Zachariah Allen, who was its pastor for the first twenty years after its organization, lived in the community, raised a large family and his descendants have always been prominent in the church services and today we have descendants of his on the church roll, representing the seventh generation.

I think it will be appropriate at this time to call your attention to the covenant these three brethren entered into with their God and one another when they organized this church: "For as much as Almighty God, by His grace has been pleased to call us, whose names are hereunto subscribed, out of darkness into His marvelous light and all of us having been regularly baptized upon a profession of our faith in Christ Jesus, and having given up ourselves to the Lord and to one another in a gospel church ever to be governed and guided by a proper discipline, agreeable to the Word of God, we do therefore in the name of our Lord Jesus and by His assistance, covenant and agree to keep up the discipline of the church we are members of in the most brotherly affection towards each other while we endeavor punctually to observe the following rules, to wit: First, in a brotherly love to pray for each other, to watch over one another and if needs by in the most tender and affectionate manner to reprove one another; that is if we discover anything amiss in a brother, to go and tell him his faults according to the directions given by our Lord in the I8th Chapter of St. Matthew's gospel, and not to be whispering about and back-bifing one another. We do agree, with God's assistance, to keep up family worship, attend church meetings, observe the Lord's day and keep it holy; not to absent ourselves fi^om the communion of the Lord's Supper without a lawful excuse; to be ready to contribute to defraying the church expenses and for the support of the ministry, and not to irregularly depart from the fellowship of the church nor to remove to distant churches without a letter of dismission; these things we do covenant and agree to observe and keep sacred in the name of and by the assistance of the Holy Trinity, Amen." We don't have a sure date when the present building was built, but we do know it was built before 1846, for we have accurate records from that date. The records kept by Anthony Clement, the first clerk, from 1823 to 1840 were either lost or destroyed. The church bought three acres of land in 1842 from Anthony Clements. On this plot of land the present church was built and now stands. So our best guess is that the present building was built around 1842.

The church was built for both black and white members with two large galleries for the black.
The roll of the church from 1837 to 1866 carried members of both races. After the Civil War the black members wanted to organize a church for their own race, so Brassfield helped them in organizing Rock Spring Baptist Church about five miles southwest of Brassfield. Today this is one of the largest Negro churches in the county.

Brassfield Church, from its organization until 1860, was a member of the Flat River Associafion, during that year it got a letter from that association to join a new association that was being formed out of a part of the Flat River, Tar River, and the Raleigh and was then called the Central Association but since has been united with the Raleigh Association and today is known as The Raleigh Associafion. N. E. Cannady, clerk of Brassfield was elected and served as the first moderator of this new associafion (Central). Brassfield remained a member of the Central Association for 84 years. In 1944 it went back and joined the Flat River Association and is now a member of same.

In October, 1858 Brethren Armstead Blackley, Elijah Winston, and other members with- drew from the church and organized a new Baptist Church near Henry Smith's school house and that is now known as Pleasant Grove Church.

In 1868 Brethren I. S. Bailey, A. L. Davis and 35 other members of the church were organized to form an arm of the church at Green Wood near the Wake County line. The building is still standing and we know it as the Tink Mangum place. They continued to worship there until 1877, when they disbanded and returned to the mother church.

Brassfield Church, during its one hundred fifty years existence has had fifty pastors. The longest pastorate held by any one was twenty years by Rev. Zachariah Allen, one of the founders of the church. The second was ten years by C. L. Gillespie and the third longest was nine years by Rev. G. T. Watkins.

Brassfield, being located not far from Wake Forest College (presently Southeastern Baptist Seminary), has always been able to secure a pastor. We have been blessed with such old pioneer church builders as Zachariah Allen, J. S. Purefoy, and E. W. Scarboro; it has also had the privilege of having some of the D.D.'s of the denomination, such as Drs. Wingate, Brooks, Walters, and Cullom.

Brassfield being located convenient to the college has been somewhat of a training school for our younger preachers, but has been very fortunate in finding among them some of our most brilliant and consecrated preachers such as Mcduflfy, Rickman, Watkins, Seymour, Fergerson, Pool, and others.

Allen was program chairman. Dr. T. C. Allen had the devotion. Short talks were made by Dr. G.
T. Sikes, Claude Garner and Mrs. Mertie Allen Aiken. G. L. Allen read the history of the church after which Dr. W. R. Cullom preached the morning sermon.

In May, 1923 (the same year as our centennial celebrafion), the church decided to elect some women deaconesses. For the records the first five deaconesses elected were Miss Cora Garner, Mrs. Carrie Lawrence, Mrs. Ava Whitfield, Mrs. Neva Walters, and Mrs. Meta Mangum. Only one of these, Miss Cora Garner, still lives.

Up until 1950 deacons and deaconesses elected served for life. On this date the church voted to rotate their active services and since then they are elected for only six years.

The church did not have a cemetery until 1924. On that date the church laid off a plot of land for that purpose. For the records, Macon Victor Estes was the first to be buried there in 1924 and G. L. Allen, the second in 1925.

From all the changes that have taken place since the early years of the church I would consider the greatest has been in the discipline of its members. Any member who was accused of wrong doing was brought before the church to defend himself. If said charges was proven true and he repented and asked for forgiveness, everything was forgiven, but if he didn't he was excom- municated.

Some of the sins that the church could not tolerate were drunkenness, fighting, and profan- ity. For the records, one brother was had up before the church for riding in a horse race and another for going to a ball game.

Any member that did not attend church services regularly or failed to contribute to church expenses was excommunicated.

It is interesting to note that the church has experimented with many different days and ways of having church services: third Saturday afternoon and third Sunday morning; third Sunday morning the third Sunday night; third Sunday morning and first Sunday morning. For several years the church called two pastors, one for the third Sunday and one for the first Sunday. In all the changes the third Sunday was never left out. Since its organization 150 years ago, Brassfield has always had preaching every third Sunday morning. Only since September, 1971 has the church had preaching every Sunday morning, Mr. James Carter being the first pastor for such services.

I can not close this history of Brassfield Church without referring to some of the many very unique recordings found in the minutes. Among some of the interesting items were these: In 1875, the church voted to let women vote for the first time in calling a pastor. Up until that date (52 years) women were not allowed to vote on church matters. In 1865 the church voted to pay Pastor T. W. Walters $74.00 a year in provisions, with com to be valued at $1.00 per bushel and wheat at $1.Z5 per bushel. In 1932 C. L.Gillespie agreed to preach a year with no mention of a fixed salary. The church was just to pay him what they could and wanted to. In all the early minutes, the church is referred to as "she." Today we pause and pay tribute to those who have labored to make Brassfield Church what she is today. We pledge anew our loyalty and faith in her future . May she ever stand as a beacon of light for those who are in darkness.

Elder Robert T. Daniel, an agent of N.C. Baptist Society for Foreign and Domestic Missions, and Samuel Duty were in charge of the service or organization. Members were received from the Grassy Creek, Tabbs Creek, and Tar River churches. A constitution having 9 articles was adopted at their first meeting. By-laws concerning non-attending and non- contributing members were added in January 1835. Throughout the minutes of the business sessions of the church, these articles were strictly followed. Records of excommunication for non-compliance are found as late as 1917.

The name, Hester, was chosen by the membership in honor of Captain Benjamin F. Hester who gave the land for the church. That land with additional purchases constitutes the land on which Hester Church Meeting House now stands. The first meeting house was a log structure, 20 ft. by 20 ft. This served the congregation until 1841 when a frame building was erected.

Elder William B. Worrell served Hester as the first resident pastor. He was bom in Halifax County, N.C. about 1800. At the age of sixteen he came to Granville County and was given a home by Deacon Thomas Williams of the Island Creek Baptist Church. He was ordained by the Grassy Creek Church in 1820. Elder Worrell was called as pastor of Hester at its constitution in 1823.

Hester Baptist Church was a member of the Flat River Association as early as 1829.
Through the years Hester has contributed greatly to the association. Many of the moderators and other officers of the associafion have been from Hester. She also has been an active member of the Baptist State Convention of N.C. from its organization as a convention. As early as 1840 delegates are recorded as attending state convention meetings. Some of Hester's early pastors played significant roles in organizing the state Baptist Convention. Dr. Samuel Wait, one of Hester's pastors, preached the organizational sermon for the Baptist State Convention in Greenville. Another pastor, Dr. Robert H. March, served as president of the Baptist State Convention from 1891 to 1905. He was also elected vice-president of the Southern Baptist Convention on two occasions. As early as 1868 Hester was contributing to both Foreign and Home Missions. By 1873 the church was regularly sending contributions to the state convention in Raleigh. In 1920 the church agreed to send 50% of the church money collected to the general funds of the convention unless they were otherwise designated.

Hester has truly been from its organization a Missionary Baptist Church. Not only has she contributed financially, but also she has shown a missionary spirit in other actions. According to the church minutes, at least two sister churches in the Flat River Association were directly helped by Hester in their efforts to organize. In April, 1960, thirteen members were dismissed by letter to constitute a church near Mountain Creek. In September, 1874, a mission station was started near Sam C. Hobgood's farm. This mission station was organized as Enon Baptist Church in May, 1875.

Another evidence of Hester's missionary spirit is seen in the fact that at least eight men from her congregation have been licensed and ordained to preach the Gospel. These are James B.

Hester has long been interested in missions and in the teaching of the Bible. The church minutes of 1865 record that B. F. Hester was elected superintendent and J. W. Badgett served as assistant superintendent of a Sunday School (then called Sabbath School). The Flat River Association Minutes record an active Sunday School at Hester since 1972.

The Woman's Missionary Society was organized in 1909 through the efforts of Mrs. W. W.
Ragland, the former Rosa Lee Kinton. Mrs. Rayland was baptized at Hester Church in 1883 and remained an active and faithful member until her death in November, 1945. Believing that the women of Hester Church should organize for mission work, Mrs. Ragland called the mission- minded women of the church together. They organized a Sisters' Missionary Society. This was only 21 years after the organization of the Woman's Missionary Union of the Southern Baptist Convention. The first meetings of the Hester missionary society were held on Saturday after- noons at the church while the men were meeting for business conference. This worked quite well since the women at that time were not allowed to speak their opinions or to vote on church business.


Hester Church has also experienced times of great spiritual and evangelistic awakenings. In revival services conducted by Rev. E. D. Poe, from Durham, in July, 1920, 78 professions of faith, 2 restorations, 9 additions by letter, and 2 additions by experience were recorded. The pastor. Rev. George Tunstall, baptized the 78 new converts in a single service. Several of these remain as members of Hester Church today. There are other records of large numbers of additions after the revival meetings. The records also report that the "doors to receive church members were opened." It seems that in the early days of the church this was the only time when an invitation to unite with the church was given.

As the membership ofthe church was increased, so did the size ofthe meeting house. In 1885 the church with a membership of 200 voted to enlarge the frame building by adding twelve feet to the front. This was done at a cost of approximately $300. In 1918 the 400 members ofthe church voted to erect a new brick building. This building serves as the present sanctuary. Its original design was in the form of a cross. Realizing the need for more educational space, the first educational wing was added to the left of the sanctuary in 1951. Again in 1968 additional education space was added with the erection of a wing to the right ofthe sanctuary. This leaf of faith required the church to enter into its largest church debt. An investment in the amount of $165,000. was made in the new building.

Through the years Hester has progressed from a church having quarter-time meetings (1823-1921) to a church having half-time meetings (1921-1949) to a church meeting on a full-time basis in 1949. In February, 1949, the church voted to call a full-time minister. Roger E. Williams, who was serving as Associational Missionary for the Flat River Association, was called as its first full-time pastor. In July of 1949, a lot on Cherry Street in Oxford was purchased for the erection of the present church parsonage.

Other highlights from past minutes include the observance of the 100th Anniversary in September of 1923, the adoption of the budget system in 1917, the purchase of the first piano in 1922, and the adoption of the rotating system for the board of deacons in 1951. A rotating system for the trustees was adopted in 1965. Perhaps one of the most outstanding actions of the church occurred on August 23, 1964. On that date the church adopted a financial plan that literally turned the concept of giving in Hester Church ' 'downside-up" . The church voted to adopt the "Growth In Christian Stewardship Program." Little did the members realize that in only five short years the church budget would grow from $22,221. in 1964 to $52,081. in 1970 — an increase of $29,221.!
The church has confinued to realize that God does expect us to be good stewards of our possessions.

Hester Baptist Church has been served faithfully through these 150 years by these fifteen servants of God: Rev. William B. Worrell, 1823-1832 Rev. James King, 1832-1837 Rev. Thomas King, 1837-1840 Rev. William M. Jones, 1840-1848 Dr. Samuel Wait, 1848-1857 Dr. R. I. Davis, 1857-1859 Rev. Moses Baldwin, 1859-1863 Rev. E. J. Beacham, 1863-1868 Dr. Robert H. Marsh, 1868-1917 Rev. George T. Tunstall, 1917-1926 Rev. W. D. Poe, 1926-1948 Rev. Roger E. Williams, 1948-1953 Rev. Paul C. Mattox, 1953-1961 Rev. Leslie H. Giles, 1961-1970 Rev. Tom E. Lolley, 1970-present In 1971 a church activity bus was purchased by several of the men and given to the church. A church pictorial directory was prepared in 1972. In 1972 the sanctuary was repaired and painted.
The sanctuary has also been air conditioned. In October of 197 1 , the church voted to call Tilden Bridges, Jr. , a student at Southeastern Seminary, as assistant to the pastor. The tremendous love and generous spirit of the church was again demonstrated when the church went without the presence of the pastor in the pulpit from late February 1972 until the first of January, 1973.
Brother Bridges; Mrs. Linda Mckinnish, the youth director of the church; the other staff members; the deacons; and the congregation helped in immeasurable ways during the pastor's prolonged illness.

Hester Baptist Church now has 600 members. It has the following organizations: Baptist Women, with five groups; Bapfist Young Women; Acteens I and II; Girls IN Action I and II; Mission Friends; Baptist Men; Cub Scout Park 620; Boy Scout Troop 620; and a Sunday School with eight departments and an enrollment of 447. Its present budget is $5 1 ,284. 16. Ofthis amount, $11,831. plus special offerings, is committed to missions beyond the local church. The church debt has been reduced to 48,588.89 as of August 31, 1973.

Compiled by Mrs. Tom Lolley HISTORY OF THE WEST OXFORD BAPTIST CHURCH The Sunday School and Church movement in our village goes back as early as 1903, when Mr. Jim Long, Mr. H. J. Council, and Mr. H. H. Eatman conducted Sunday School, moving from house to house. A Mr. Ross, who became interested in this movement allowed a spare room in his home to become the first permanent locationn for their Sunday School. They confinued meefing there unfil 1909, when the meefing place was moved to the Chapel across from the mill.
Here it remained, teaching the word, until 1910.

In that year a revival was held at the Chapel with Reverend Pace bringing the messages. At the close of this revival series, and possibly growing out of this revival spirit, the West Oxford Baptist Church was organized.

Mr. R. W. Lassiter donated a lot, 30 feet by 60 feet, to the Church. It was located on what is now a part of Mr. D. F. Lanier's front yard on Church Street. The Church was organized with eleven charter members, including Mr. D. F. Lanier, Mrs. D. G. Thomerson, and Mrs. W. H.
Long.

Mr. E. G. Usry was a young man in the merchantile business in Oxford, who became intensely interested in this Church movement. He was called as the Church's first pastor, and with his help and guidance a one-room frame structure was built on the lot donated by Mr.
Lassiter. Sunday School was held each Sunday morning and preaching services each Sunday night.

The Church grew steadily, and in 1921 a new lot, adjoining and just north of the old location, was donated by the Oxford Baptist Church under the leadership of General B. D. Royster, Sr.
The frame structure was moved to the new lot in 1922 and eight Sunday School rooms were added.

Shortly after this, Mr. Usry asked for, and was granted, a leave of absence to attend Wake Forest College. Mr. C. A. Upchurch of Raleigh was called to serve as interim pastor. He served for seven months, after which the Church called Mr. L. L. Hudson to serve until Mr. Usry returned ft-om college and resumed his pastorate.

The Church grew in blessings, and weathered the usual storms of Church life quietly and with dignity. In 1948, remodeling plans were begun. The plans included brick veneering the outside, the addition of a Sunday School room in the basement and complete redecoration for the inside. The electric organ was added in 1951.

The Church was saddened in 1950, when after forty years of faithful service, Mr. Usry felt constrained to retire due to failing sight. Mr. W. C. Barham was called, and served the Church from March, 1951, until March, 1952. Upon his resignation, Mr. Homer I. Nichols was called to serve the Church as its first full-time pastor.

The Church constructed a pastorium on Grace Street at a cost of approximately $1 1,000.00 on a lot donated by Mr. D. F. Lanier.

The need of additional classroom space prompted the purchase of the frame building adjacent to the Church in February of 1953. This housed the nursery and some adult Sunday School classes while plans were being formulated for a future education building.

Mr. A. S. Lamm was called to serve as pastor of the Church on August, 15, 1953, and with a continuation of growth, once again the need for additional space was felt.

A piece of property on the corner of Pine Tree Road and Hillsboro Street was acquired at a cost of $9,500.00 and on April 16, 1959, ground was broken for an Educational Building to house the Church until a future date when the anticipated three unit master plan could be constructed.
This building was completed and fijlly equipped at a cost of approximately $60,000.00. Dedica- tion services were held on the last Sunday in November of 1959.

Upon entering this new building the Sunday School was for the first time completely departmentalized. Much prayer, work, and love was built into this structure through many hands and hearts who continue in the attitude of the founders of West Oxford Baptist Church in preparing for the future with plans of helping others and furthering the cause of Jesus.

It was saddened hearts that the Church learned Mr. Lamm would resign effective September 15, 1962, to become superintendent of Missions in the State of North Dakota.

Dr. Truman Smith, Professor at the Southeastern Seminary, served as interim pastor from October, 1962 through December, 1962.

The Reverend E. A. "Bud" Abbott came from Tifton, Georgia, as pastor beginning in January, 1963. God used this man to lead the church in a vital ministry to a growing community.
He served until August, 1966 when he accepted a call from Byne Memorial Baptist Church in Albany, Georgia.

Reverend Thomas Sheppard served as interim pastor from September, 1966 through De- cember, 1966.

In January, 1967, Reverend Donald Price came as pastor. As the community continued to grow, so did the Church and it moved with the ideals and goals set by strong leadership from dedicated laymen and laywomen.

In July of 1969 the Church authorized the Trustees to borrow $20,000.00 from Union National Bank in order to retire the outstanding bonds which had reached the ten year maturity date. This $20,000. accounted for the total amount owed on the Church building. A monthly payment of $340.00 was to be paid on the loan plus any amount that might accumulate in the Building Fund.

At a special called conference on November 30, 1969, the Church adopted a Constitution and By-Laws.

On December 29, 197 1 , at the age of 81 , having watched her church grow over a period of 69 years , " Miss Kate "(Mrs.D.G.) Thomerson was the last of the eleven Charter Members to die .

On August 7, 1972, a PAYMENT IN FULL of $868,22 was made to the Union National Bank on the building fund note, leaving the Church free of all debt on the building. A joyous "Note-Burning" ceremony was held on Homecoming Day, October 1, 1972.

This history would be incomplete without recognition of such men as Walter Hughes, Jr.
who served as Building Fund Treasurer; J. B. Yeargin, and Elvis Gresham whose untiring work and dedication gave leadership and support to the Church as it marked this phase of growth and service.

Again the Church felt a loss of the pastor when Mr. Price resigned in August of 1 972 to accept a call to serve the Benson Baptist Church of Benson, N. C. Dr. Roger Crook, Professor at Meredith College in Raleigh, served as interim pastor for a three month term from September 3, 1972 through December 24, 1972.

On October 8, 1972, the Church voted authorization to have needed repairs made on the parsonage in the amount of $1,800.00.

The Reverend Harvey L. Beener came to serve God and to lead this Church beginning January 1, 1973.

During the period of this history, the Church has served many souls and been a source of spiritual enrichment to all who have passed this way. Its history is exceeded only by the bright prospects which challenge its future. May God grant to it both grace and power to uphold its traditions and to extend His Kingdom.


Bush was ordained in 1948 by the Rosemary Baptist Church, Roanoke Rapids, North Carolina. He pastored the following churches: North Warrenton Baptist Church, North Carolina, 1948-53; Wise Baptist Church, North Carolina, 1949-53; Scottsburg Baptist Church, Virginia, 1953-56; Butner Baptist Church, North Carolina, 1956-70. His last employment was serving as Regional Clinical Chaplain at the N. C. Alcoholic Rehabilitation Center in Butner, North Carolina from 1970 until 1973.


Bush was married in 1949 to Bess Ruppelt of Charlotte, N. C. and to this union was bom three daughters, Nancy, Linda, Phyllis.

His death was on February 1 1 , 1973 and at the time of his death was a member of the Butner Baptist Church.


Rev. Johnson served pastorated in North Carolina for thirty-three years. His last were Enon and Mount Zion Baptist Churches in the Flat River Baptist Association for eight years before retirement due to ill health in 1965.

Mr. and Mrs. Johnson made Granville County their home after his retirement. Rev. Johnson passed away November 1, 1972 and was buried in Enon Baptist Church Cemetery. He was a member of the Enon Baptist Church at the time of his death.

Surviving Mr. Johnson are his widow, Irene Carde Johnson; two sons, A. Wayland of Maryland; W. Finley of Greenville, N. C; two daughters, Euleta of Detroit, Michigan; and Angela J. Frazier of Portsmouth, Virginia; and six grandchildren.

Section 2. Any Missionary Baptist Church, which has been a member in good standing for a period of twelve months or more v/ith another Missionary Baptist Association, and any new Missionary Baptist Church seeking admission to this body must present its application to the Executive Committee not less than six months prior to the annual meeting, and shall be received into the Association upon a majority vote of the members present.

ARTICLE 4-ORGANIZATION Section 1. The officers of the Association shall be a Moderator, Vice-Moderator, Clerk, Treasurer, Assistant Treasurer, Auditor, Church Training Director, Sunday School Director, Woman's Missionary Union Director, Brotherhood Director, Director of Church Music, Director of Visual Aids, Historian (or Historians), Three Trustees, and Superintendent of Missions, and Parliamentarian. These officers shall be elected at each annual session and shall hold office for one year, or until their successors in office shau be filled to respective offices, except the Superintendent of Missions, whose term of office is indefinite, and the Trustees who will serve three-year terms on a rotation basis with one being elected each year for a three-year term. The Moderator and Vice-Moderator cannot succeed themselves more than one term. All terms of office shall begin immediately before adjournment of the annual meeting of the Association.

Section 2. The Moderator and Vice-Moderator shall be elected from the floor at the beginning of the second session of the first day. The office of Moderator shall rotate between a pastor and a layman. The other officers (except the Superintendent of Missions) as named in Article 4, Section 1, shall be elected by the Association after receiving nominations from the Nominating Committee, and after giving opportunity for nominations from the floor of the Association.

In the event of the resignation or death of the Moderator, the Vice-Moderator shall complete the unexpired term of the Moderator. In such an event or in the event of the resignation or death of the Vice-Moderator, the Moderator or Clerk shall call the Executive Committee into session to elect someone to fill the vacancy for the unexpired term of the Vice-Moderator.

At no time shall the office Moderator and Vice-Moderator both be filled by pastors or laymen.


ARTICLE 7-DUTIES OF COMMITTEES Section 1. A committee of committees, composed of five from different churches, shall nominate five members for the Nominating Committee, three members for the Time, Place and Preacher Committee, three for the Resolutions Committee, three for Obituary Committee, thirteen for Missions-Advisory Committee, seven for the Evangelism Committee, five for Budget Committee, six for Program Committee, eight for Calendar Committee, ten for Ordination Committee, four for Christian Life Committee, four for Camp Committee, and three for Constitution Committee and submit them for election by the Association at the opening session. These committees shall be composed of pastors and lay persons from the different churches.

Section 2. The Nominating Committee named at the Annual Associational Meeting shall nominate all officers during the year, including the nominees to be elected at the following annual meeting, except the Moderator and Vice-Moderator whose election is described in Article 4, Section 2. The Sunday School Director, Church Training Director, Woman's Missionary Union Director and the Brotherhood Director shall be nominated for election by the Executive Committee at its July meeting. Other officers to be elected at the annual association are Clerk, Treasurer, Assistant Treasurer, Auditor, Director of Church Music, Director of Audio- Visual Aids, Historians, three Trustees, any other officer the Association may decide its needs, and the following representatives who will report to the annual Association may decide its needs, and the following representatives who will report to the annual Association: State Missions, Home Missions, Foreign Missions, Baptist Colleges, Relief and Annuity Board, N. C.
Baptist Hospital, N. C. Baptist Homes, and other objects or agencies of denominational interest.

Section 3. Duties of Time, Place and Preacher Committee shall be to announce the time and place of the next annual meeting of the Association and to recommend the time and place of the annual meeting of the Association five years in advance and other meetings of the Association. They shall recommend the preacher and the alternate for the Annual Sermon for' the next year and report to the Association.

Section 4. Duties of Resolutions Committee shall be to study au resolutions presented to the Association on the first day and report to the Association on the second day, and form all expressions of courtesy.

Section 5. Duties of the Obituary Committee shall be to form all expressions of memorials.

Section 6. The duties of the Missions Advisory Committee shall be to study the missionary needs in the Association, counsel with the Superintendent of Missions in promoting the adopted program of work, recommend projects with which the Churches of the Association may cooperate. The Superintendent of Missions shall be responsible to this Committee. This committee should be representative of all phases of the associational program. The Sunday School Director, Church Training Director, Woman's Missionary Union Director, and the Brotherhood Director shall serve as members of this committee.

Section 7. Duties of the Evangelism Committee shall be to work with the pastors, Superintendent of Missions, Executive Committee and churches of the Association to promote simultaneous revivals, w^arehouse revivals, institutional evangelism, and evangelistic conferences and seek to stimulate a consciousness of evangelism needs within the Association.

Section 8. The Program Committee shall be responsible for planning the meetings of the Association. This committee shall be composed of the Moderator, Vice-Moderator, Music Director, immediate past Moderator, and pastors of the host churches.

ARTICLE IV, SECTION 2 READS NOW-"The Moderator and Vice-Moderator shall be elected from the floor at the beginning of the second session of the first day. The other offices (except the Superintendent of Missions) as named in Article 4, Section 1, shall be elected by the Association after receiving nominations from the Nominating Committee, and after giving opportunity for nominations from the floor of the Association.

In the event of the resignation or death of the Moderator or Vice-Moderator, the Clerk shall call the Executive Committee into session to elect someone to fill the vacancy for the unexpired term." a. PROPOSED CHANGE-INSERT AFTER FIRST SENTENCE-"The office of Moderator shall rotate between a pastor and a layman." b. PROPOSED CHANGE-REPLACE LAST PARAGRAPH-"In the event of the resignation or death of the Moderator, the Vice-Moderator shall complete the unexpired term of the Moderator. In such an event or in the event of the resignation or death of the Vice- Moderator, the Moderator or Clerk shall call the Executive Committee into session to elect someone to fill the vacancy for the unexpired term of the Vice-Moderator," c. PROPOSED CHANGE-ADD NEW PARAGRAPH-"At no time shall the office of Moderator and Vice-Moderator both be filled by pastors or laymen." 2. ARTICLE VII, SECTION 14 READS NOW-"The Budget Committee shall be responsible for studying the financial situation of the Association, planning the annual budget, and conducting educational promotion of the budget in the local churches. The Treasurer will be ex-officio member of the Budget Committee." PROPOSED CHANGE-INSERT AFTER THE PHRASE "planning the annual budget"- "recommendations for approval by the Executive Committee at the July Executive Committee meeting," 

RECOMMENDATION The Constitution Committee recommends that the Committee on Committees work out a plan for the rotation of the membership of standing committees to be presented for approval at the 1975 Annual Associational Meeting.

Rev. Tom Louey Mr. Carl Blake Mr. Phil Grady REPORT OF WOMAN'S MISSIONARY UNION Report No. 9 "Missions is women's work"— how often have you heard this statement? It is not true because we are to share our missions knowledge with the men and youth in our churches, and hopefully to get all church people involved in our mission enterprise at home and abroad.
Missions is the work of everybody.

How grateful we are for the Woman's Missionary Union of the Flat River Baptist Association, Last year was a very good year but there is always time for improvement. We attained the honor again of being a Distinguished Associational WMU.

The associational WMU Council met each quarter for planning and evaluation of our work.

A tea for the Baptist Women presidents and assistants was held in February at the Tabbs Creek Baptist Church. At this meeting, there was discussion among the ladies about their work in their churches. This was planned and directed by Mrs. Frances Morton, associational Baptist Women's Director.

The annual Spring Meeting was held in March and was well attended at the Sharon Baptist Church. Our theme was "How Will Anyone Go: Unless Someone Sends?" Our speaker for the afternoon session v/as Mrs. Rudolph Woods, missionary to Belgium and the evening session, speaker was Miss Nancy Curtis, State Baptist Young Women's Director.

In March at the Providence Baptist Church, a highlight in the Baptist Young Women's calendar was observed with a banquet. Special music by the Amis Chapel Youth Quartet and Mr. Grady Wicker of Oxford as guest speaker. Mrs. William Wilson is Director of BYW work in our Association.

The associational Acteens Rally was held in April at the Peace's Chapel Baptist Church.
This was planned by the associational Acteens Council and Mrs. James E. Frazier, associational Acteens Director. The theme was "All Of My Tomorrows." Mr. Bennett Straughn, chalk artist from Raleigh, was guest speaker.

Mrs. Lucy Lyles, associational Girls In Action Director, was well pleased with the Day Camp held in April at the Island Creek Baptist Church. Miss Marjorie Spencer, former missionary to Chile, presented the missions emphasis with other ladies and Acteens from our Association assisting with other emphases.

No associational meeting was planned especially for the Mission Friends, leaders and teachers; however, Mrs. Sanford Daniel, Mission Friends Director, encourages continued growth among our younger children.

Training leadership is an on-going concern of Woman's Missionary Union. Twenty-two churches were represented at the Leadership Conference held at the Mt. Zion Baptist Church in September, The Woman's Missionary Union helped this year to sponsor an associational Girls Softball league. Five teams participated representing the following churches: Island Creek, Pleasant Grove, Oxford, West Oxford, and Enon. The champion was Oxford Baptist with Pleasant Grove as runner-up. We hope more churches will become involved in the coming year in this softball league.

Each year WMU chooses one or more aspects of its work to receive special emphasis during that particular year. Missions Support was the emphasis this past year because it is a vital part of the work of Woman's Missionary Union. WMU has the responsibility for keeping before the church and its families the need to provide an atmosphere in which persons can hear and heed God's call as missionaries.

It is possible that the most significant work of Woman's Missionary Union has not found its way into this report for the most significant contribution has been made by you the individual member as you have prayed, given and witnessed to the lost in your community.

May we as the Woman's Missionary Union Council serve you better in the coming year, remembering that God is the source of all blessings, temporal, and spiritual, all that we have and all we owe to Him.

Respectfully submitted, -^[^ ' '' Mrs. Lawrence Davis , ' ' Assn. WMU Director BROTHERHOOD REPORT Report No. 10 As we come to the end of another year, we are very appreciative of the work being done in the twenty churches of the association that have Brotherhood work. This is an increase of four churches from last year. We are real glad to see this work of the men and boys increasing in the association. There are three other churches working on plans to start either Baptist Men or Royal Ambassadors and this is very encouraging.

Some of the highlights of activities for last year, we had 25 people in the leadership conference last October. For our annual Baptist Men-Ladies Night we had 200 present with 16 churches represented, and a real good program. Attendance at the R.A. Conclave was down but we still had a good conclave with 67 boys and 20 adults from eight churches. We would like to double this number next year. The associational Baseball League was a success with ten teams and about 200 boys participating. We had five men to go to a regional leadership conference in Durham. These men will lead Brotherhood leadership conferences here in our association on October 29th and will also serve as our associational Brotherhood Planning Committee.

We are very grateful of the work done last year, and are looking forward to the activities for the new year. We have some new programs for next year and we hope you will take part in these and make them a success.

Then, of course, the R. A. Conclave in May.

We thank the Lord for the men and boys of our churches and what they mean to the mission endeavors of the association. We ask for your prayers and help as we try to enlarge the Brotherhood work in our association.


In addition, $41,000.00 has been budgeted for a new classroom at Fruitland Bible Institute and another $35,000.00 for an Administration Building. Fruitland is a direct program of state missions. In fact, one of the largest single allocations of the State Missions offering goes to Fruitland. Fruitland is a mission which strengthens many local congregations and associations by providing well trained pastors and superintendents of missions. There are at present about one thousand graduates of which most are serving churches and associations in the state.

Special mission projects will require $9,000.00. The remaining $140,000.00 or all money above that already mentioned will be used by the Caraway Conference Center at Camp Caraway near Asheboro, N.C. This proposed conference center will be a multi-story building with forty- eight bedrooms with private baths. It will have six conference rooms, lounge areas, an auditorium seating 450 people. The Caraway Conference Center will be used primarily for adult leadership conferences, special seminars, in-depth Bible study and a continuing theological education center.

Last year twenty-seven of the thirty-three churches in Flat River contributed a total of $3,610. to State Missions. This year $3,878. were contributed by twenty-seven churches. Of course our goal should be for an increase in the State Missions Offering but our goal should also be for 100% participating of our churches. Won't each of you encourage your church to participate if it is not doing so?

Respectfully submitted, Johnny Brummitt HOME MISSIONS REPORT Report No. 13 Missionaries for the Home Mission Board are active in all fifty states, Panama and Puerto Rico. The board's missionary force totaled 2,170 at the end of the year and was comprised of students and volunteers. The board pays full support of about one third of the workers and shares two thirds of the support with the state convention. Record Cooperative Program and Annie Armstrong Easter Offerings will enable enlargement of missionary force and strengthening all programs.

Annie Armstrong Easter Offering increased from $6,137,612.00 to $6,884,355.61.

A total of 1,024 student summer missionaries argumented and supported the work of the missionaries.

The Division of Evangelism got a new director during the year. The division was reorganized to provide emphasis to lifestyle concerns, lay witnessing, mass evangelism and materials and services and make them more available where they are needed. Lay witness training involved some 300,000 people during the year. No doubt a vital factor in Youth programs such as the first youth evangelism leadership conference and the second Super Summer placed emphasis on youth role and responsibility in crossing barriers to win persons to Christ.

There are now some 850 full time chaplains with the mainstream of denominational life.
About one third minister to individuals in civilian hospitals, institutions, and industries. The rest serve in military services.

"Spring Street USA" is back again this year. This is the board's evangelistic television series. Viewers were numbered in hundreds of thousands in the past year. The program has now backlogged thirty-four tapes and is being seen on more than fifty stations across the nation. Mail response has increased showing an increased acceptance and ever widening audience. More shows, however, will depend on increased funding.

More Southern Baptists become more aware of the program of interfaith witness in the mission study series "No Man Stands Alone." Interest was shown everywhere in learning more about other faiths.

More than 1,050 missionaries are supported by the Home Mission Board working in language efforts in thirty-three state conventions, Panama and Puerto Rico. Work among several Indian groups extended the Boards outreach to some thirty language and culture groups.

The increasing participating of lay persons in missions was ungraded by the naming of the first director of Christian Service Corps. Mr. Wilson, a volunteer himself, became an unsalaried staff member in the. Department of Special Mission Ministries. He is giving half his time to enlist lay people on short term and long term assignments.

Much gratitude goes out to all Southern Baptists for their support because without it to reach people about Christ would be impossible.

Respectfully submitted, Mrs. Curtis Wilson FOREIGN MISSIONS REPORT Report No, 14 Sending out and maintaining missionaries is primary interest in our Southern Baptist Mission of the Foreign Mission Board. At the close of 1973, the Board had an overseas missionary staff of 2538 including 172 missionary associates, three special project medical personnel, 127 missionary journeymen, and 13 re-appointments.

Many more workers are needed in our efforts. Evangelism and church developments are important. Leading people to Christ and building more and better churches are our aims. Many of our national churches are self supported. They still need our prayers and support. Television and radios are increasing our witnessing mission.

We are doing great work of witnessing in our schools and student missions, publication and hospital medical care. All of these are getting support from our Foreign Mission Board, Total contributions towards the Southern Baptist Foreign Mission Board program was $39,435,694. The Cooperative Program total was $17,135,169.; Lottie Moon Christmas Offering was $19,794,025,; and other contributions were $2,506,500.

All of this was made possible by our effort at home.

William Lawrence REPORT OF THE ANNUITY BOARD Report No. 17 . ■ , What will your church do when your pastor can no longer serve you? Age, accident, sickness, and death can catch a church as well as its pastor "unprepared." This need not be! The Annuity Board of the Southern Baptist Convention offers churches the opportunity to protect those who serve them. Adequate retirement plans are an absolute necessity in the world in 34 which we live. Twenty -six of our churches are providing at least the basic retirement protection for their pastors. However, some of these are still based on a 14,000. yearly salary and should be increased to reflect at least ten percent of the present pastor's salary.

Churches should also consider health insurance as a part of the benefits it provides to a pastor. Two years ago I was faced with more than $17,000. in hospital bills at Duke. To put it mildly ... I would have been ruined had not the Hester Church provided adequate insurance for the pastor. But on the other side of that— the folks at the church can have a good feeling about "being prepared" for an unforseen emergency. Knowing the folks of this area as I do, they would have felt some responsibility in helping in this crisis situation. By providing assistance for an adequate insurance program, they were in a sense "protecting themselves" and their pastor.


Help in planning these programs was provided this year at a dinner for pastors and church lay leadership by Rev. Sam O'Neal, N.C. Baptist Convention Representative for the Annuity Board. Brother O'Neal stands ready to assist any church in providing adequate plans for retire- ment, disability, health insurance, and life insurance. You can contact him at the Baptist Building in Raleigh, P.O. Box 26508, Phone 833-1605.

Let's move toward 100% of adequate coverage in the 1974-75 church year.

Prayerfully submitted, Tom Louey NORTH CAROLINA HOMES FOR THE AGING Report No. 18 We, as Baptists support many important ministries in North Carolina, none of which is more important than the North Carolina Baptist Homes for the Aging. We've had a part in making their lives happier and more secure for over 23 years. This has been done through two sources— (1) on the third Sunday in February, there is a special offering for the Homes and (2) through the Cooperative Program. Over 30% of the needed budget is received in this way each year. The 1974 offerings, we hope, will exceed $350,000. needed for operating these five Homes plus the Nursing Care Unit.

Through this, God has blessed the ministry superbly by allowing us to establish Resthaven and the Hayes Home in Winston-Salem, the third, fourth, and fifth at Albemarle, Hamilton, and Yanceyville. Four out of every five residents in these Homes could not be cared for without our help.

The Trustees of the Homes have decided to establish a sixth Home on the Pearson Estate in the city of Asheville, which is in the western part of North Carolina. This beautiful, twenty-one* acre site overlooks the city of Asheville and has the Blue Ridge Mountains for a background. To get this initial phase started, a dedicated group of people in this Western area organized the beginning Fund for this Home. It is hoped this will inspire the rest of us to donate enough money to build thi^ Home after the General Board and the Baptist State Convention have approved. We, in the churches of North Carolina, are encouraged to visit the site for this proposed Home. If we make an appointment in advance of visiting, we wul be served lunch.

It is most urgent that we Baptists be more generous with our offerings on the third Sunday in February, the Cooperative Program, and extra gifts. With increased needs to take care of these older people, let us give until this great cause will not be neglected.

Remember, this ministry to the older people is ministering to Jesus Christ for when we give to one of the least of these, we do so for Him.


If you are interested in getting this motion picture for showing at your church, get in touch with Rev. Bob Wainwright or me, and we will be happy to assist you.

You may not know it, but the North Carolina Baptist Children's Homes always welcome visitors. Homes are located in Thomasville, Pembroke, Kinston, Waynesvule, Wallburgh and Asheville; and now the staff is in the process of reactivating the Greer Home in Chapel Hill. The Greer Home will be aimed at helping children with emotional problems.

It is the aim of all the Baptist Children's Homes to give the children homes as nearly as possible like real homes, and to provide trained personnel for any needs the children may have.
Mr. Roger Williams, Superintendent of the Baptist Children's Homes, reports that all campuses are operating at capacity, and that our foster homes are full. He also reports that our children are making good use of opportunities. A new cottage, for twelve children and two staff residences will be completed by the end of the year. I might remind you that buildings like these would not be possible without wills and special gifts from people like you. All the money from the churches and Cooperative Program go for child care— none for buildings! You may want to make plans for a donation for this purpose if you have realized some extra profit this year, or you may want to remember these children's homes in your will. It is worth thinking about. Further, Mr. Williams reports that because more and more troubled teenagers are being referred to us for care, we have started a new program on the Kennedy Home Campus. Limited to only eight boys, it is designed to help them work through some of their deepest troubles by using the principles of group therapy.

The Farm-Food program continues to be of vital importance. Although feed and fertilizer costs have risen tremendously, the Lord has blessed us with a good season, and all of our cottages and staff people have preserved as much food as possible to fight the rising cost of inflation. The children assist in this endeavor too.

A special campaign is in progress to increase the services available at the Odum Home in Pembroke. A year ago our first campus integration occurred, with the placement of a Black child at Odum Home. Our success in involving and ministering to Black children has continued at Kennedy Home in 1974. The program of therapy at the Mcneil Cottage, located at Indian Beach, continues to be one of the meaningful benefits which all of our children in group care homes enjoy. We desperately need your prayers and loving support as we face the pressures of an uncertain economy, rapidly spiraling costs, and the need to serve children whose personal problems are most intense. Remember, these children are "our North Carolina children"!!
Don't forget them!

When the special Thanksgiving Offering is taken in your church, remember that one third of the Children's Homes' support is from that offering. Also, don't forget them in your wills and individual giving!

"'' ^ Respectfully submitted, ij, Jean C. Tilley NORTH CAROLINA BAPTIST HOSPITAL REPORT Report No. 20 In spite of the growth in size and complexity at your Baptist Hospital, the emphasis is still upon "ministering with skill and understanding." Evidence of this is a typical patient's remark recently: "From the head surgeon to the aide who handed me a glass of water, with all the doctors, nurses, people in the business offices and chaplains in between, I was cheerfully given every personal consideration." Changes and additions continue to be made for the purpose of providing better hospital care. Renovation of the Progressive Care Unit has been completed, and the South and West Wings of the hospital building are now being renovated. Construction of the Ambulatory Care Unit is well under way and is scheduled for completion in the spring, or early summer, of 1975.

New discoveries continue to make possible better medical care. For example, a new EMU- Scanner is to be installed and is expected to be in use before the end of the year. This device, which combines the use of x-rays and computers, will make it possible to detect brain tumors, blood clots, and strokes more accurately and more quickly. Scans can be completed in about 30 minutes and the results can be available in another five minutes. There is no risk or discomfort involved, and the patient does not have to be admitted to the hospital.

New approaches are also being made in the hospital's ministry to the emotional and spiritual needs of people. The Division of Human Enrichment and Development, in the Depart- ment of Pastoral Care, is conducting retreats, seminars, and workshops designed to help appro- priate the resources of the Christian faith to the various areas and experiences of life. The Pastoral Care Department has also opened its first satellite center, in Fayetteville, to offer counseling and training to people in that area. Your Cooperative Program gifts help to support these ministries.

The School of Nursing graduated its last class in May. The school has been in the process of phasing out for the past three years, as the result of a trustee decision made in 1971.

In January the hospital had its only room rate increase in the past three and one half years.
Nevertheless, the hospital continues to operate "in the black." The 1973 Mother's Day Offer- ings amounted to $375,000. At the time of this writing, our 1974 Mother's Day gifts are running ahead of the same time last year. These gifts are used to assist patients with the most urgent financial needs. A special committee of five Baptists from the Winston-Salem area assists personnel from the hospital Business Office and the Department of Denominational Relations in administering these funds.

Your hospital staff, and the people they serve, are grateful for your interest, your prayers, and your financial support.

In our Association of 33 churches, 26 churches gave $3,059.72. This is $408.62 less than 28 churches gave last year. This means that 7 of our churches gave nothing to the work of the hospital and many who contributed gave less than they did in 1973. Let us prayerfully recon- sider our responsiby^y in this area of ministry.

i , ' Respectfully submitted.

Rev. Harvey L. Beeler Baptist Hospital Representative " \^ ADDRESS BY SUPERINTENDENT OF MISSIONS Report No. 21 This has been a great year for me personally and for our Association. I want to thank all of you who have been so much a part of the work of our Association. It is a real joy to me to be able to work with the pastors and lay people of this Association. I appreciate the privilege which is mine to serve here with you.

1 want to thank you also for the fine way I have been received as I have visited in the churches. I am still trying to learn the names of many of you. I hope you will continue to be patient and help me with this. I will continue to schedule visits to each church as I can get around but if I can help you with something, please call me anytime.

As we have heard the reports from the organizations and institutions, we have actually heard and will hear in the reports to come how we are "Sharing His Love Now." These reports are not just dry statistics but they represent real people with whom we are "Sharing His Love Now." We are sharing His love as we carry out the work of our association through its committees and organizations. All of the institutions, agencies, and organizations of our Baptist State Convention and Southern Baptist Convention came into being and are able to function and carry on their work because some churches got together a long time ago to carry on some needed work which they could not do by themselves. Not just busy work but ministry to the needs of people— the sick, the aging, homeless children, and missionary work.

So we are sharing His Love through the Cooperative Program as we give to our Stat^ Missions work, our Home and Foreign Mission work, our colleges and seminaries, our Annuity Board, our Children's Homes, our Baptist Hospital, and Homes for the Aging.

As we look at our digest of church letters it is good for us to look back and see what we have done to "Share His Love." (Note gains and losses.) Our biggest and most important project this year was the People Search or religious survey.
Ten or eleven churches began this project and I am not sure how many completed it. Some were still working on it when I last checked with them about it. This is unfinished business that needs to be completed. Every church needs to know who the people are that live around the church. If you haven't done this project, we urge you to set a date and do it. We have the necessary cards and maps in the office and we will help you with it if you will call on us. We must know who the people are before we can reach them.

Even more important than looking back is the need to look forward and plan what we will do by God's grace.

Your associational Executive Committee has approved and is recommending to the churches three major projects through 1976 to help you Share His Love Now: 1. November 10-13, 1974, Lay Evangelism School, Director— Thurman Fox. The spiritual objectives of a Lay Evangelism School are as follows: (1) to help Christians experience a full and meaningful life in Jesus Christ; (2) to help Christians share their testimony for Jesus Christ in simple and effective ways; (3) to help Christians know how to train others in sharing their testimony for Jesus Christ; (4) to help the church see the nature of its priority task in training and equipping Christians for a life of witness involvement for Jesus Christ; and (5) to help the church to establish continuing witness training and involvement for its members. These objec- tives are certainly worthy of our putting forth the necessary effort to obtain them. Each pastor has been sent a pre-registration sheet and we have more if you need them. We need them returned to the office by October 28th, if possible to help us in preparing the materials.


During 1975 there will also be special emphasis on the Cooperative Program 50th Anniver- sary. The Cooperative Program is the very life-line of our work as Southern Baptists. We need to continue to increase this support and widen its base. Our special offerings for some causes are needed and necessary, but our money goes only to one cause. When we give through the Cooperative Program, it goes to au causes. We ui:ge every church to make a special effort during 1975 to help the members to be better informed about the Cooperative Program and to increase their support of the Cooperative Program.

3. October 10-15, 1976— World Missions Conference. This is an opportunity for every church in the association to have first hand contact with some of our missionaries and to learn about au areas of our mission work. You will be receiving a letter about this before too long and we will have to have a definite commitment from each church as to whether they will participate. We have to know over a year ahead in order to schedule missionaries to come for this conference.

There are many opportunities to Share His Love Now in our own community. We shared a partial list of needs with the ladies at the WMU Conference recently. They are: ministry to the lonely; to youth in trouble; to slow readers and non-readers; to neglected children; to the needy; to nursing homes; to the retired people at Pinewood Housing Project; to the Oxford Orphanage children; to St. Cyprians Child Development Center. There are just a few— you could name others.

Mr. Earl Howard will share with you in a few minutes the work and some of the needs in the Butner area.

In all of the things that we have talked about, there ought to be one or more places where each of us here and every member of every church can find a handle and grab on to a place where he or she will be involved in Sharing His Love Now.

What we will do during the next year should not be determined by what we did last year.
What we will do should be determined by what our resources and abilities are and what the needs are.

Our resources and abilities are great. Certainly the needs are great. Christ calls us to Share His Love Now!

Robert H. Wainwright REPORT OF THE BUTNER CHRISTIAN SOCIAL MINISTRY IN THE FLAT RIVER BAPTIST ASSOCIATION Report No. 22 I am happy to report that the Butner Christian Social Ministry, sponsored jointly by the Flat River Baptist Association and the City and Metropolitan Missions Division of the Baptist State Convention of North Carolina has continued to be a growing and exciting ministry to the many people of the institutions located in the Butner Complex.

Will you be reminded that when we talk about Butner Christian Social Ministry we are talking about approximately 1,200 daily patients at the John Umstead Hospital, 75 patients at the Rehabilitation Center for the Blind, 125 young men at the Umstead Youth Center, 85 patients at N.C. Alcoholic Rehabilitation Center, 118 students (c-ed) at the C. A. Dillon School, and a total of 1,500 residents in the Murdoch Center for multi-mental retardees, plus personnel and staff of the several institutions. We are talking about approximately 4,000 people on any given day.

Your fine spirit of cooperation and generous contributions during the past twelve months have made the work exciting and assisted in making an excellent advance in our ministry to these children of God. Thank you and may God bless you as you reach into this new year with an even greater service.


More than one thousand hours of volunteer help have been given by you to the people at Butner. Also, approximately $800.00 for Murdoch Camperships were contributed by churches and individuals of Flat River Baptist Association plus other contributions of clothing, books, personal items and home type items to add beauty and warmth to the halls and rooms. Time and space do not permit me to list the various ways you have been involved and assisted in ministering in Christ's name through Butner Christian Social Ministries. Please continue to hold us up in your prayers and other support as we labor together in Chrjst's name to people who are hungry for our love and responsive to our ministry.

Respectfully submitted.

Earl W. Howard, Director BCSM SUNDAY SCHOOL REPORT " Report No. 23 Two Vacation Bible School clinics were held in the Association this year, one at Pleasant Grove and the other at Hester. Thirty churches were represented with a total attendance of 177. Thirty churches had schools with a total attendance of 2,690.

A two-night Sunday School Teaching Improvement Clinic was held at Oxford Baptist with 103 attending from twenty churches.

The "Reach Out to People" program was promoted through the Sunday School organiza- tion. Forty-five people from eighteen churches attended a Reach Out Rally to promote the program and to stress the "People Search." Ten churches participated in "People Search" discovering a great number of prospects. As a part of "Reach Out" we are promoting H^h Attendance Sunday in November 17. Each church is asked to set an attendance goal and to report attendance on that day. We need to know now the goal your church has set.

The emphasis of the Sunday School Board is on Adult work for the coming year. It is our plan to help the churches to train their adult leadership. Workshops are planned early in the calendar year.

The Sunday School Enlargement Campaign has proved a great success for churches in the past. On November 26, 7:30 P.M. at the Oxford Baptist Church, a meeting is planned for Pastors and Sunday School Directors to discuss the possibilities for a campaign within our association. It is hoped that every church wiu be represented to explore the opportunity for our churches.

Respectfully submitted, David G. Peach CHURCH TRAINING REPORT Report No. 24 ' The Southern Baptist Church looks to the Sunday School as the "right arm" of leading people to Christ. Church Training however is left with the responsibility of leading the new christian in his growth, and training au christians to be better servants of our Lord. This is the primary goal of the Flat River Association Church Training Department.




This was a good year for Church Training in our Association, and I appreciate deeply your response and help.

May we dedicate ourselves anew this year to the continuing leadership and training needed as we strive to serve our Lord more fully.

Respectfully submitted, Danny W. Collins, Church Training Director ASSOCIATIONAL PASTOR'S CONFERENCE REPORT Report No. 25 The Flat River Associational Pastor's Conference meets the first Monday after the first Sunday in each month.

These meetings involve a period of devotion given by the host pastor, by which we seek to grow closer to our Lord. There is a period of personal sharing by which we seek to grow closer to each other. A program is planned and presented by guests coming from a^variety of different walks of life. This is to help the pastor grow in his awareness of the world in which he lives.
