INFORMATION FOR PREPARING CHRISTIAN HIGHER EDUCATION REPORT TO ASSOCIATIONS Report No. 30 _^ Churches and Colleges are Partners While churches and colleges are not identical in their purposes, Baptist churches and Baptist colleges in North Carolina do their work in an atmosphere of mutual appreciation. This active partnership includes Baptist Youth Corps, student-led worship and revival services, visits to the campuses by pastors and other leaders, participation of faculty and staff in associational and local church programs, and special projects of promotion on behalf of the Cooperative Program.

.44 Cooperative Program Your seven Baptist colleges are grateful for the continuing financial support which they receive through the Cooperative Program. During 1974, approximately $2,350,000 v^ill under- gird the work of North Carolina Baptist colleges. At a very crucial and difficult time, these funds make a tremendous difference in the quality of programs offered by our schools.

Baptist Students The colleges make a special effort to recruit North Carolina Baptist students through pastors and superintendents of missions. No qualified student will be turned away simply because of inability to pay. Baptist colleges are anxious to enroll the young people from our churches.

Change in Wingate College Leadership After 20 years of effective leadership. Dr. Budd E. Smith resigned the presidency of Wingate College. On June 1, Dr. Thomas E. Corts of Georgetown, Kentucky assumed the presidency of the seventy-eight year old institution. Dr. Corts, the eleventh president of Wingate College, came to this position with excellent preparation and experience.


Submitted by: Charlie Jones REPORT TO BAPTIST ASSOCIATION: From— The Christian Action League of North Carolina Report No. 31 The officers, trustees, and the Executive Director ot the Christian Action League take this opportunity to express to the members of this Association, convened in annual session, their appreciation for the wonderful support which you gave in the liquor campaign last fall. We are still rejoicing in the wonderful victory that God gave to us. To receive over 70% of the vote in our favor and to carry 97 counties in the state is remarkable, indeed. It is a tribute to the wonderful spirit of cooperation on the part of people like you across the state.

Recently one of the organizations that conducts surveys across the state of North Carolina included a question about the visibility which the League enjoyed in North Carolina. It was a three part question, but we include here only the first part since the latter two are not relevant to present circumstances. The question was included with ten other questions and read as follows: DO YOU KNOW WHAT THE CHRISTIAN ACTION LEAGUE OF NORTH CAROLINA IS?

The results were: Yes, 80.6% No, 18.6% No Answer, 8% Thus it is revealed that four out of five persons across North Carolina know what the Christian Action League is. The polling company had the following comment: . . . "Pretty good recognition for an organization funded mainly by . . . religious groups." We are looking to the future. At the annual meeting in April, seven Task Forces were appointed, as follows: , .

TASK FORCE ON PRISON REFORM - TASK FORCE ON HIGHWAY SAFETY TASK FORCE ON ALCOHOL DRUG CONTROL LAWS TASK FORCE ON OBSCENITY AND PORNOGRAPHY TASK FORCE ON TAX REFORM TASK FORCE ON CAPITAL PUNISHMENT TASK FORCE ON BANNING ADVERTISING OF ALCOHOL Some of these groups have already met and others are meeting in the n' ar future. Work is going on in these areas. We need your continued prayerful and financial support.


Even so, the RECORDER has been in business for 141 years and expects to continue. A few changes have to be made, however, to keep the paper strong. As staff members have resigned or retired, they have not been replaced. Some weeks the number of pages has to be reduced to save money, but readers have been very understanding.

Despite some problems, the RECORDER is enjoying its highest circulation in history- more than 111,000— thanks to the continuing support of the vast majority of Baptist churches in the state. These churches and their members remember the part the RECORDER played in the overwhelming defeat of liquor by the drink last November. This is probably the most significant story that the RECORDER has reported since the associations met last year. You wiu remember that approximately 70 per cent of the voters in the state voted against liquor by the drink and it was defeated in 97 of 100 counties. This should take care of this issue for awhile.

The RECORDER will continue its emphasis on local church coverage, seeking to deal with problems that every church has. Naturally, we are prejudiced, but we do not believe any publication can take the place of the BIBLICAL RECORDER. If your church does not send it to every family, may we ask a simple question: Why? At a cost of only a nickel a week for each family, this is the best investment a Baptist church can make. Put the RECORDER in the budget before it has to increase the rate because of inflation.

HISTORICAL EVENTS COMPILED FROM CHURCH LETTERS Amis Chapel: Renovated sanctuary and basement. Installed new heating system in sanctuary and basement. Wall to wall carpet in sanctuary.
Bullock: Installed pipe organ in sanctuary.
Butner First: Licensed Mr. Lee Draughn to Ministry, Oct. 2, 1973. Called Rev. Ed Lycett as Associate Pastor, July 7, 1974.
Dexter: Dedication of a new Conn Organ which was a gift of the B. W. Hicks Family and friends in his honor.
Enon: Church pew cushions given in memory of the Rev. W. I. Johnson Florence Avenue: Ground-breaking service for new Church Building on November 4, 1973.

Began building Educational Building on April 9, 1974.

Central air conditioning installed in church and parsonage.
Hester: Employed Miss Gloria Kimball as Minister of Youth and Education, Sept. 1, 1974.
Knott's Grove: Planted shrubbery around church and parsonage. Built picnic shelter.
Mount Olivet: Church porch built. Church repainted and recarpeted. Shrubbery planted around church.
Mount Zion: Church property and parsonage appraised and insurance updated.
Olive Grove: Installed church steeple; installed chimes in steeple; purchased new piano for church; purchased new choir robes; purchased washer, dryer refrigerator for pastorium.
West Oxford: Purchased Activity Bus.
Pleasant Grove: New choir robes purchased; Bus Ministry approved by church; re-roofed the auditorium and painted trim works and painted the parsonage; began Evening Service.
Stovall: Called Rev. Lyn Anderson as pastor Aug. 1, 1974; brick vaneered and re-decorated parsonage and added a den, bathroom and carpet.
Tally Ho First: Called Rev. Bob Mcnew as pastor.
Union: Constructed a new church building and dedicated the building on July 18, 1974.

David Peach HISTORY OF CORINTH BAPTIST CHURCH Sometimes prior to February, 1832, Mr. Archie Cawthorn gave the land which Corinth Church now stands on. The first house of worship was built during the year of 1832, by J. E.
Allen. Preaching was held only once a month. In August, 1835, the church was constituted by Elder James Weathers and Zachariah Auen. Eleven white members and three colored members were appointed to write the Constitution and rules of the church. The church was received into the Flat River Association during the same year.

In March of 1857 the church voted to organize a Sunday School.

The church granted John A. Reams permission to build a school house on the church land in December of 1862.

Jeff Burwell, a colored member who had been licensed to preach, was ordained in July, ij 1868. A committee was appointed to act with him to establish an arm of Corinth Church composed of colored members, at Taborn School House.

1875 was the first time on record that State Missions were mentioned. $10.00 was given.

The church voted in May of 1923 to discontinue their Saturday service and have two services per month, meeting on Sunday.

1926, the old church building was sold to R. M. Tunstall for $200. In February, 1927, the first service was held in the new building which cost $4,760.

A cement base was put around the church in 1932. This was done instead of installing gutters.

The church purchased a communion set in 1940 that had individual glasses. Prior to this time the church used two goblets.

Electric lights were connected into the church in 1945. A few years prior to this, the church had used a Delco lighting system.

Two members. Rev. Nat T. Brummitt and Mrs. Hannah Brummitt Hills, went from the church into fuu-time Christian work.

In 1956 Corinth withdrew from the field with Peace's Chapel. Until this time the church had always been in a field with another church.

The church voted in 1958 to have communion quarterly, instead of semi-annually. The church began its procedure to rotate deacons, their term of service being three years.

In 1958 the pulpit area was converted into a choir loft, the back portion of the church was buut, which included seven Sunday School rooms, two baths, and choir room. Many hours of dedicated service was rendered by the men of the church, as they did the majority of the carpentry work.

The auditorium was redecorated in 1959. The benches and floors were refinished. In 1960 the Woman's Missionary Union gave the pulpit furniture that is in use at the present time, prior to this the W.M.U. gave the present light fixtures.

1961, a vestibule was built onto the front of the church, and carpet was installed in the aisle and front portion of the sanctuary.

1962, Mrs. Dulcie Tunstall Yeargin deeded to the trustees of the church a parcel of land adjoining the church property on the north side. A ground breaking service was held for the new parsonage in 1963. In 1963 Corinth hosted the Flat River Baptist Association's Annual Meeting.

The parsonage was completed in 1964. The men of the church built the parsonage with the only hired help being a brick mason. Our first pastor to live in the parsonage was Rev. James Perry.

In 1968 the church enjoyed the use of its first regular bulletin. Also 1968, the church installed its present heating system for $1,970.

In 1970 the woodwork on the church and the parsonage was repainted.

125 song books. The Baptist Hymnal, were purchased in 1972. These were all given by members and former members as a memorial or love gift, 1973, 16 stained glass windows were installed in the sanctuary and adjoining Sunday School rooms.

1973, the front entrance of the church was redecorated and lights were added. The entire inside of the church was painted, and carpet was installed in the back portion of the church.

In September, 1973, the church had its first Homecoming. A large crowd, which included six former pastors, were present for this event. One of the six, Mr. H. T. Stevens, who served the church from 1905 to 1908 was present.

Through the years, many gifts have been given to the church. Among these are our piano and our chimes. In January of 1974, the church was deeded some land directly behind the Parsonage. April, 1974, the church purchased an organ. This purchase was inspired by a $1000 contribution toward the purchase of an organ.

A total of forty-five ministers have served and led. the church through the years.


Compiled by Mrs. C. W. Thompson HISTORY OF TABBS CREEK BAPTIST CHUP.CH Tabbs Creek Baptist Church was formed in 1792. It was organized by Mr. George Robbards. Tabbs Creek was one of the churches to be dismissed from the Roanoke Association to organize the Flat River Baptist Association in 1794.

For two years the church met in the homes of members. The first building was built in 1794 and it was donated by Mr. James Parham, Sr. During this period, a group of the member- ship was dismissed to form Hester Baptist Church. This was in 1823. In 1883, Tabbs Creek Church closed its doors.

Tabbs Creek Baptist Church was re-organized in 1906 with fifteen charter members. At its new beginning the church meetings were held in the school building at Dickerson Station. In 1910 dedication services were held for a new structure. It was a frame building, 40' by 50' with plain glass windows. The land for this church was donated by Mr. James Parham, Jr. This building served the church until 1950, A new brick structure was completed that year. It was a sanctuary and four classrooms. In 1955 an educational building was added to this structure which contained four classrooms, two baths and a divided assembly room.

Tabbs Creek and Sharon Baptist Churches, sharing the church field of Rev, Oren Elms, built a parsonage in 1962, During these years the church made steady progress. In 1955 the church went from quarter time to half time and in 1965 went to full time pastorate.

In 1970 an additional educational building was constructed. This was a separate structure connected by a covered walk. It contained a kitchen, two baths, two classrooms and a divided assembly room.

At the present time Tabbs Creek Church is without a pastor. The Rev. Donald Morris is serving as interim pastor.

The membership has varied from year to year as follows: 1806 60 1830 60 1840 29 1850 90 1860 123 1870 35 ^ - 1907 17 1917 130 1938 138 1970 243 1972 242 1973 238 1974 (presently) 220 ' ' Pastors who have served the Tabbs Creek Baptist Church are: 1867 J. C. Carroll 1873 T. J. Horner 1878 W. H. Jordan 1907-1910 A. B. Dunaway 1913 J. B. Weatherspoon 1914-1916 R. H. Marsh 1917-1920 G. T. Tunstall 1921-1922 J. U. Teague 1923-1925 L. L. Hudson 1926- no pastor 1929-1938 J. U. Teague 1939-1951 C. A. Upchurch 1952-1957 W. J. Edwards 1958-1966 Oren J. Elms 1967-1969 Douglas Roebuck 1970-1974 Frank Ellis, Jr.

Compiled by Mrs. James H. Critcher BIOGRAPHICAL SKETCHES OF LAYMEN WHO WERE MEMBERS OF THE EXECUTIVE COMMITTEE OF FLAT RIVER WHO WERE DECEASED DURING THE PAST YEAR Mr. Henry Oliver Davis Mr. Henry Oliver Davis was born November 13, 1909 to Harry Ward and Ha^sie Thomas Davis of Bullock, North Carolina. He attended Norfolk College, Norfolk, Virginia; and Massey, Smithdeal Business College, Richmond, Virginia.

His farming career was interrupted by three years spent in the Marine Corps in the service of his country. He was a member and past president of the Stovall Ruritan Club of Ruritan National.

He was a life-long member of the Bullock Baptist Church, and at the time of his death, he was Chairman of the Board of Deacons, teacher of the Adult Men's Bible Class and Chairman of the Cemetery Committee. Mr. Davis was also associational representative from his church to the Flat River Baptist Association's Executive Committee.

He was married April 4, 1959 to Mertie M. Booker of Amity, Georgia.

His death was on February 11, 1974.

Mr. Calvin Vaughan Calvin Vaughan was born October 23, 1931 in Granville County, son of Mrs. Mollie Walker Vaughan and the late Johnnie Vaughan. At the age of twelve Calvin Vaughan professed his belief in Christ and joined the Grassy Creek Baptist Church. He received his education at Stovall High School and North Carolina State College in Raleigh, North Carolina.

In 1950 Mr. Vaughan was married to Betty Blackwell and thereafter were added to their family one son, Calvin Barry Vaughan; and two daughters, Darlene, now Mrs. David Weary; and Sue of the home.

While an active participant and leader in the community, Calvin Vaughan served his church faithfully as a Sunday School teacher, choir member and also served as a deacon of his church.
Mr. Vaughan served as a trustee of the Flat River Baptist Association from 1972 until his death on April 12, 1974. At the time of his death, he was Vice-President of Tar Heel Production Credit Association in Oxford, North Carolina.

His wife, Betty and daughter. Sue, now reside in Grassy Creek Community and are active members of the Grassy Creek Baptist Church.

After special music by Miss Jackie Newton of the Stovall Baptist Church, Miss Curtiss brought a most interesting message telling of special activities she had been involved in since she began serving in this position.

Constitution Flat River Baptist Association PREAMBLE For the declaration of its fundamental purposes and the permanent guidance and ;ontrol of its activities, this body does adopt the following as its constitution, to wit: ARTICLE l-NAME The name of this body is the Flat River Baptist Association of North Carolina.


ARTICLE 3 -MEMBERSHIP SECTION 1 . The Association shall be composed of all the pastors of the churches of the Association and messengers elected by the churches. Each church is entitled to three messengers for the first one hundred members or fraction thereof and one additional messenger for each additional fifty members or fraction thereof; providing no church shall have more than ten messengers.

SECTION 2. Any Missionary Baptist Church, which has been a member in good standing for a period of twelve months or more with another Missionary Baptist Association, and any new Missionary Baptist Church seeking admission to this body must present its application to the Executive Committee not less than six months prior to the annual meeting, and shall be received into the Association upon a majority vote of the members present.

SECTION 3. The membership of the Association shall meet annually on Tuesday and Wednesday after the third Sunday in October.

ARTICLE 4--0RGANIZATI0N SECTION 1. The officers of the Association shall be a Moderator, Vice-Moderator, Clerk, Treasurer, Assistant Treasurer, Auditor, Church Training Director, Sunday School Director, Woman's Missionary Union Director, Brotherhood Director, Director of Church Music, Director of Visual Aids, Historian (or Historians), Three Trustees, and Superintendent of Missions, and Parliamentarian. These officers shall be elected at each annual session and shall hold office for one year, or until their successors in office shall be filled to respective offices, except the Superintendent of Missions, whose term of office is indefinite, and the Trustees who will serve three-year terms on a rotation basis with one being elected each year for a three-year term. The Moderator and Vice-Moderator cannot succeed themselves more than one term. All terms of office shall begin immediately before adjournment of the annual meeting of the Association.

SECTION 2. The Moderator and Vice-Moderator shall be elected from the floor at the beginning of the second session of the first day. The office of Moderator shall rotate between a pastor and layman. The other officers (except the Superintendent of ' Missions) as named in Article 4, Section 1, shall be elected by the Association after receiving nominations from the Nominating Committee, and after giving opportunity for nominations from the floor of the Association.

In the event of the resignation or death of the Moderator, the Vice-Moderator shall complete the unexpired term of the Moderator. In such an event or in the event of the resignation or death of the Vice- Moderator, the Moderator or Clerk shall call the Executive Committee into session to elect someone to fill the vacancy for the unexpired term of the Vice-Moderator.

At no time shall the office Moderator and Vice-Moderator both be filled by pastors or laymen. A Committee of Committees, composed of five from differer churches, appointed by the incoming Moderator before the close of the Annui Session, shall nominate persons to fill all vacancies on the following committeei Nominating; Time, Place and Preacher; Resolutions; Obituary; Missions Advisorj Evangelism; Budget; Ordination; Christian Life; Camp; and Constitution. The tenur of elected committee members shall be arranged such that in so far as possible, ai equal portion of the elected committee membership shall be replaced each year. N; committee members shall serve longer than 3 years, and cannot be reelected to th same committee for one year after having completed an elected tenure c membership; provided however, this shall not apply if one is a committee member b virtue of holding some other position in the association. The members of th Calendar and Program Committees are selected by virtue of the office they hold These committees shall be composed of pastors and lay persons from the differer Churches. A chair-person shall be designated annually for each committee.

SECTION 2. The Nominating Committee shall be composed of five members. Thi Committee shall nominate all officers during the year, including the nominees to b elected at the following annual mmeting, except the Moderator and Vice-Moderate whose election is described in Article 4, Section 2. The Sunday School Directoi Church Training Director, Woman's Missionary Union Director and the Brotherhooi Director shall be nominated for election by the Executive Committee at is Jul meeting. Other officers to be elected at the annual association are Clerk, Treasurei Assistant Treasurer, Auditor, Director of Church Music, Director of Audio- Visua Aids, Historians, three Trustees, and other officer the Association may decide i needs, and the following representatives who will report to the annual Association State Missions, Home Missions, Foreign Missions, Baptist Colleges, Relief am Annuity, N. C. Baptist Hospital, N. C. Baptist Homes, and other objects or agencie of denominational interest.

SECTION 3. The Time, Place and Preacher Committee shall be composed of thre members. Duties of this Committee shall be to announce the time and place of th( next annual meeting of the Association and to recommend the time and place of th( annual meeting of the Association five years in advance and other meetings of thi Association. They shall recommend the preacher and the alternate for the Annua Sermon for the next year and report to the Association.

SECTION 4. The Resolutions Committee shall be composed of three members Duties of this Committee shall be to study all resolutions presented to the Associatioi on the first day and report to the Association on the second day, and form al expressions of courtesy.

SECTION 5. The Obituary Committee shall be composed of three members. Duties of this Committee shall be to form all expressions of memorials.

SECTION 6. The Missions Advisory Committee shall be composed of thirteen members, the Moderator, Vice- Moderator, Sunday School Director, Church Training Director, Woman's Missionary Union Director, Brotherhood Director, and seven members elected according to Article 7, Section 1. The duties of this Committee shall be to study the missionary needs in the Association, counsel with the Superintendent of Missions in promoting the adopted program of work, recommend projects with which the churches in the Association may cooperate. The Superintendent of Missions shall be responsible to this Committee.

SECTION 7. The Evangelism Committee shall be composed of seven members.
Duties of this Committee shall be to work with the pastors. Superintendent of Missions, Executive Committee and churches of the Association to promote simultaneous revivals, warehouse revivals, institutional evangelism, and evangelistic conferences and seek to stimulate a consciousness of evangelism needs within the Association.

SECTION 8. The Program Committee shall be responsible for planning the meetings of the Association. This Committee shall be composed of the Moderator, Vice-Moderator, Music Director, immediate past Moderator, and pastors of the host churches.

SECTION 9. The Calendar Committee shall be responsible for planning and recommending to the Association the Calendar of Events for the Association. This Committee shall be composed of the Moderator, Vice-Moderator, the heads of the School, Training Union, WM.U., Brotherhood, and Music Department, and the chairman of Evangelism.

SECTION 10. The Ordination Committee, composed of five pastors and five laymen, shall, when requested by any church in the Association, examine a candidate for ordination to he Christian ministry and assist with the service of ordination if such assistance is desired. Any church requesting the services of this committee will place the name of the candidate before the committee at least thirty (30) days prior to the date set for ordination for examination on the following principles: A. Christian experience and conversion B. call to the ministry C. moral and spiritual fitness D. doctrinal beliefs E. knowledge of the Bible F. attitude toward denominational work and objects G. support of denominational causes Every candidate for the Christian ministry is to be urged to prepare for such ministry in Baptist institutions of higher learning. This committee shall be responsible for recommending or not recommending that the candidate be ordained by the church making the request.

(a) Motion was made by Mr. Clay that the Budget be adopted as presented. Motion seconded.

(b) Amendment to this motion was made by Mr. Claude W. Allen, Sr. of the Brassfield Church, that the Radio Program Item (Flat River Baptist Hour, $936.00) be eliminated from the Budget. A second to this amendment was heard. This fifteen minute program is heard each Sunday morning and includes announcements, hymn and short devotional by a different pastor each Sunday.

(c) Moderator Adcock recognized Mr. Allen who enumerated several reasons for wanting to eliminate this item, i. e.: main reason is to reduce the Budget; listening audience small because of not able to receive the program; there are enough other religious programs; announcements made on the program are given in the well-prepared associational Newsletter; program not all that good for evangelism purpose.

(d) Mr. Carl Ragland of the Enon Church and Rev. Joe Leonard of the Olive Grove Church were among several who voiced opposition to the amendment by stating they felt the program was worthwhile if only reaching a few people.

(e) Moderator Adcock gave time for anyone wanting to speak in favor of the amendment. There were not any remarks made.

(f) Question was called by Rev. L. W. Burnette for vote to be taken.

(g) A vote on the amendment was taken at this time. The amendment was defeated by majority vote.

Report No. 2 EVANGELISM COMMITTEE REPORT Since Evangelism is at the very heart of the commission of Christ to His Church, I am very happy to report on the ministry of evangelism. During the past year in the Flat River Baptist Association we had a Lay Evangelism School on Nov. 10-13, 1974, with 120 enrolled and 9 churches represented. An Evangelism Rally was held on May 24-25, 1975 with Rev. Orvil Reid as evangelist. One of the most exciting evangelistic efforts in our Association was the Oxford Orphanage Revival Aug. 24-26,1975, with 30 making decisions for Christ.


It is our prayer that we plan now-share our revival dates and pray for each other as we join together in this great evangelistic effort for Christ.

Respectfully submitted.

Tom Lolley Report No. 4 REPORT OF THE EXECUTIVE COMMITTEE The Executive Committee of the Flat River Baptist Association is composed of all active pastors of the Association, all officers of the Association, all chairmen of standing committees of the Association, and a representative who will report at the annual associational meeting.

During the past Associational year the committee met once each quarter to perform its duties of carrying on the work of the Association between sessions, work out the budget and present recommendations and reports to the Association.
Attendance was good at each meeting with the exception of October when attendance was somewhat less than usual.

Actions taken by the Executive Committee other than hearing the reports of various committees, directors and the Superintendent of Missions consisted of the following: 1. Approved recommendations for commemoration of the 50th year of the Cooperative Program consisting of a. Purchase the film "Rope of Sand" b. That Cooperative Program brochures and pamphlets be used as bulletin inserts during the year c. That each church observe Cooperative Program Day on April 20 d. That each church seek to increase its Cooperative Program contributions 2. Named Mrs. Marie Pettit to the position of secretary due to the resignation of Mrs.
Jean C. Tilley 3. Approved sending minutes of each executive committee meeting to members for sharing with their respective congregations 4. Approved the proposed 1975-76 budget as recommended by the budget committee subject to action at the Annual Associational Meeting. All churches were encouraged to join the 4% plan under which each church would give 4% of its undesignated offering to support the Association. The plan would provide sound financial support for the associational programs yet fluctuate with church receipts.

5. Approved the appointment of a Bicentennial Committee consisting of the Reverend Clarence Godwin, Chairman; Mrs. Pou Coats, Mrs. Carl Ragland, Mrs.
Mac Satterwhite, Mr. Mark Stevens, and the Reverend Robert Wainwright.

6. Approved change in site of 1975 Associational Meeting from Peace's Chapel to Olive Grove due to construction at Peace's Chapel. The 1977 meeting will be returned to Peace's Chapel.

The range of programs and services provided through and by the Association has continued throughout the year at a very high level. Your continued prayers, participation, and financial support are needed in order that adequate services and programs be available to the 33 churches and their members.

Respectfully submitted, ! L. C. Adcock, Moderator Report No. 5 REPORT OF BICENTENNIAL COMMITTEE "Between now and July 4, 1976, millions of Americans will join together in celebrating the Bicentennial of our nation. The churches of America can play a major role in making the Bicentennial what it should be.

In November the Baptist Women's Day of Prayer was observed with study of program materials, individual praying, and a special offering made to help support the Baptist World Alliance Women's Department.

WMU Focus Week was observed in February with the theme: "Love Thy Neighbor".


Mrs. Clarence Godwin, associational Baptist Women's Director, reports that all but two of our thirty-three churches have Baptist Women's organizations.

The Baptist Young Women had their annual banquet on March 21 at the Amis Chapel Baptist Church. Miss Nancy Curtis, Baptist Young Women director for N.C.
WMU, was guest was guest speaker. Mrs. William Wilson, associational BYW Director, reports fourteen BYW organizations in our association.

On April 21 at the Providence Baptist Church, the Acteens held their annual meetmg using the theme: "Something's Beautiful". Mr. Scott Wilkerson from Rocky Mount was the speaker and soloist - bringing inspiring and challenging messages in each. Associational Acteens director is Mrs. Reynolds Frazier.


If Mission Friends is important for one child, it is important for all children. Mrs.
Sanford Daniel, associational Mission Friends director reports twenty-one organizations in our Association.


Several groups from WMU's in the churches assisted in the Vacation Bible School held this summer at Oxford Orphanage either with teaching or helping with refreshments. Several decisions were made during this week.

The associational Woman's Missionary Executive Council continues to meet quarterly to plan, coordinate, and evaluate the work.

Some of the associational Directors along with other WMU members from other j churches attended the State WMU meeting held in March in Winston-Salem. At this meeting our associational Woman's Missionary Union received recognition as being a Distinguished Associational Woman's Missionary Union for 1973-74. Other churches receiving recognition was Mountain Creek, Amis Chapel, Delrayno, Mount Zion, Island Creek, Oxford, and Providence.

Several of our associational WMU directors attended the State Workshop at Mundo Vista in September to enable us to better serve in our various places of leadership.

Training has no ending - it is ongoing as was proved at our WMU Leadership Conference on September 22, 1975 at First Baptist Church, Butner. There were 166 in attendance even with such terrible weather.

Much of the success of carrying out the concepts of the tasks of WMU, to teach missions, be involved in mission action, and to support missions through praying giving, and going depends upon inner allegiance to these purposes -- spirit in the work, desire to go forward and enthusiasm.

We are thankful for the past accomplishments and look to the days ahead with love, concern, and dedication to serve our Lord better as women involved in WMU organizations.

As an associational WMU Council of this Association, we stretch out with outreached arms to be off service to you as sharing Christ's love is what Woman's Missionary Union is all about.

How appropriate was the 1974-75 WMU Watchword: "God was in Christ reconciling the world unto himself... and hath committed unto us the word of reconcilation". 2 Cor. 5:19. This watchword should send WMU members forth with the determination of telling all men everywhere that through Christ, God has reconciled the world to himself by forgiving men of their sins. So many do not know this.

Respectively submitted, Mrs. Lawrence Davis - - Report No. 8 REPORT OF BROTHERHOOD We are happy to report that the Brotherhood of our Association is making steady progress each year. We have had 1 church to start Baptist Men's work, and 1 to start Royal Ambassadors in the past year-not as many as we would like, but we are proud of these. Our goal is to have Brotherhood organizations in every church in the Association. Won't you help us?

Some of the highlights of the past year and some of the plans for the coming year The Brotherhood Leadership Conference last November at Providence Baptist, Church was well attended with about 50 men participating. This was the largest number of Brotherhood leaders we have had at a training conference. Our next Brotherhood leaders we have had at a training conference. Our next Brotherhood Leadership Conference will be at Stovall Church November 4th. I hope more of our Brotherhood leaders will attend this conference to learn better how to lead our men and boys in missions.




The R. A. Conclave each May contmues to be a highlight for the boys. This year we had 7 churches represented with 75 boys and 22 men in attendance. Surely we should have more than 7 churches out of 33 to bring boys to Conclave. Won't you help us?

Another new endeavor this year was to send some boys to Camp Caraway for R. A.
Week. We would like to express our appreciation to Knotts Grove Baptist Church for providing the bus to carry 33 boys to Camp Caraway. We thank each of you who had a part in this and we hope more boys will go next year to learn more about the R. A.
work.

The Baseball League sponsored by the Brotherhood Department was a success with 10 teams and about 175 boys participating. Pleasant Grove's team was the champion.

The Brotherhood and W.M.U. jointly sponsored the Mission Action Workshop with good attendance.

The Associational Brotherhood Committee stands ready to help you anytime. Feel free to call us.

Thank you for your prayers and support the past year, and please continue to pray for us as we try to lead another year.

Respectfully submitted, George S. Gresnam Associational Brotherhood Director Report No. 9 THE COOPERATIVE PROGRAM REPORT Southern Baptists have frequently been called a people on mission for God. This mission is best carried out through doing together those ministries which it would be impossible to perform individually. Cooperation of this magnitude is a unique feature of Southern Baptists. The Cooperative Program.

Strengthening ministries help develop local church organizations such as Sunday School, WMU, Church Training, Brotherhood, etc. Lengthening ministries are those ministries which require the resources of more than one church or association Examples of these are resort missions, church loans, migrant missions, etc.

The 1975 State Missions Offering goal is $400,000, an amount representing aboul 40 cents per North Carolina Baptist. The 1975 offering will be divided as follows $175,000 for general state missions budget; $34,000 for payment on the administration building at Fruitland Bible Institute; $26,000 for special state missior projects; and $25,000 for the classroom building at Fruitland. All money ovei $260,000 will go toward long range planning and improvements at North Caroling Baptist Assembly, Southport.

Southern Baptist should have compassion for world missions, but while looking ai the needs of world missions we should not become blind to the real needs of our owi countries and state. Without neglecting distant lands we must continue to build i strong state missions program throughout North Carolina.

, Respectfully submitted, Johnny Brummitt Report No. 11 HOME MISSIONS REPORT There have been many serious national problems in our country in the past year. It has also been a year of spiritual renewal in churches, association and state conventions.

There was a great response to Annie Armstrong Easter offering. The $7.2 million dollar goal was reached and went over to $8,130,142.

There were 2,171 persons under missionary appointment by the Board at the end of the year.

We have so much to be thankful for the growing involvement of lay persons in volunteer missions work. There are more than 30,000 people currently involved in volunteer home missions service. Some work for a few hours a day; some work a few weeks or months out of the year. Some are associates, or assistants to missionaries in community centers, in pioneer areas and among ethnic groups. There are 1,100 student summer missionaries, some 50 US-2workers. Also there are several hundred youth choirs which go on summer mission tours. This also is a way to greatly spread the cause of Christ.

Baptists are providing a ministry to Vietnam Refugees as they begin new life in the United States. It's possible that Southern Baptists will come in contact with 110,000 refugees. Many of the refugees are making professions of faith through the Work carried out by missionaries and fellow Baptist workers.

So many things have come to an end this past year but the spreading of the Gospel has continued by the support of our fellov,^ Christians.

Respectfully submitted, Mrs. Curtis Wilson Report No. 12 Foreign Missions Report to the October 1975 Meeting of the Flat River Baptist Association At the end of 1974, Southern Baptists were represented overseas by a missionary staff of 2606. This staff included 191 missionary associates, 148 missionary journeymen and 3 special project medical workers. The Foreign Mission Board, as a means of easing the work of these missionaries, divided the world into eight administrative areas. Also, South America was divided into two areas by the beginning of 1975. By January 1975, Southern Baptist missionaries were assigned to 82 geographical entities, an increase of five areas added in 1974. Those five areas were: Panama, Grenada, St. Lucia, Dominica, and Madagascar.

The Foreign Mission Board's programs of work aroung the world are described in terms of evangelism and church development, schools and student work, publications work, hospitals and medical care, and benevolent missions.

The first of those programs is the central objective of missions. Overseas churches are stimulated by the Foreign Mission Board and its missionaries. Accomplishments are made largely by national churches, leades, and conventions but with the assistance of Southern Baptist missionaries and resources. Some of the activities representing evangelism and church development are in: radio and TV, religious education promotion, lay leadership training, stewardship, camps and conferences, youth work, and music.

The second program of schools and student work progressed well in 1974. The idea of the program is to train leaders for tomorrow among their people creating strong indigenous churches. This became possible through Baptist institutions ranging from kindergarten to theological schools and Bible Institutes for men and women.
Theological study-by-extension, begun in East Africa in 1974, reported 10 countries some with night classes added, some with additional master's degree programs, Around the world the student is receiving attention from Southern Baptist missionaries and national conventions.

The third program lies in the area of publications. As literacy rates rise and churches develop, the supply of Christian literature becomes more important.
Examples of literature provided includes new Bible correspondence courses, courses on the family, Sunday School literature for the Carribean, new hymnal edition in Portugal and dialect hymnals in Angola, just to name a few.

Another program is directed toward hospitals and medical care. While meeting physical needs for thousands who might otherwise by neglected, the medical field also opens strategic doors for evangelism. In 1974, significant developments included: new surgical ward and intensive care unit in Korea, pharmacy and staff quarters at the Ghana hospital, new out patient clinics in several countries, and government approval for a nursing school in Nigeria. Mobile units for rural areas and day clinics in local churches coninue to open doors where the gospel can be heard.

The last program of benevolent missions include agricultural programs, good will centers and community centers, orphan homes and direct relief. Southern Baptists should be aware that our Foreign Mission Board serves as a channel for ministries in overseas relief. In 1974 if we supported Foreign Missions we helped in: drought and famine relief efforts in Upper Volta, Niger Republic and Mozambique, reconstruction after the hurricane in Honduras and flooding in Bangladesh.

At the end of 1974, Southern Baptist's contributions to foreign missions totalled over $44 million. Clarence Bishop; Mrs. Evelyn Rogers; Mrs.

William Lawrence; Mrs. H.O. Davis Report No. 17 REPORT OF THE ANNUITY BOARD OF THE SOUTHERN BAPTIST CONVENTION The Southern Baptist Convention Annuity Board serves as a lifeline of support for 10,054 retired pastors and other church-related workers or their widows.


The Annuity Board also holds in trust and investments in retirement contributions from 38,000 pastors, other church related staff and denominational employees -- which will be converted into annuity benefits when they retire.

These funds held in trust amount to a record $353,445,743. While this seems a large amount of money, it needs to be noted that this must be divided by the number of future annuitants it will serve for the remainder of their lifetime after retirement.

The Annuity Board would have enjoyed its best year ever in 1974, but the stock market's downturn intervened. Realized and unrealized losses in income from bonds, short-term investments, and common stocks amounted to $21 million.

Gains in other investments reduced the net loss to $11.5 million, or three per cent based on average investments. All investment funds, whether church-related or having other connections, suffered from the stock market decline.


The majority of the Churches in the Flat River Baptist Association 25 of 33 participates in the Annuity of their Pastor and employed staff. It would be great to report next year that all were in the plan one way or another.

Respectfully submitted, Clarence Bishop Report No. 18 SUPERINTENDENT OF MISSIONS ADDRESS Thank you for the priviledge which is mine to serve as your Superintendent of Missions.

The report on the work of the churches is before you. You have heard some of the reports of the work of the association and you will hear others this morning. The digest of church letters indicates that the churches have made progress this year.
There have been increases in every column with the exception of Church and W.M.U. membership. There have been over a hundred more baptisms this year than last, total receipts, total mission gifts, gifts to the Cooperative Program and Associational Missions have shown a healthy increase.

The annual meeting of the association is a time of looking at what has happened in the past year and looking ahead to what we will do.

In Matthew 9:37, Jesus said, "The harvest is so great, and the workers are so few." Jesus was trying to give his followers a vision of what the needs were and what they should do. There is a passage in Proverbs 29:18 which says, "Where there is no vision the people perish." When God's people have no vision they cannot see what God has for them to do, they cannot see the great harvest that is before them and they perish.

An editorial by C.R. Daley in the Kentucky Baptist State Paper points out what happened to Baptist Churches in the early history of our country. (Associational Administration, Vol. 9, Sept. 1975, p. 6, "Anti-Missionism Means Death.") "History is an infallible teacher and pity the Baptist churches that disregard its lessons. In the beginnings of Baptist work in Kentucky and other frontier areas 200 years ago, one of the greatest controversies was between missionary and anti-missionary Baptists.

"The missionary group was considered heretical by strict Calvinists who believed God predestined from eternity some to be saved and others to be lost. Everyone would be saved or condemned by God without regard for any decision man might make.

"Fortunately, the majority of these two groups of Baptists got together early in Kentucky and from this union came the present-day Kentucky Baptist Convention numbering over 650,000. But there were those who refused to unite and continued as anti-missionary Baptists. They have persisted but have gradually declined and are almost unknown today except in some remote areas.

"Anti-missionism is a deadly disease for churches. Though Baptist churches admitting to be anti- missionary are rare today, the anti-missionary spirit and attitude are far from dead. All churches with name of missionary are not true to this name.
Self-centered churches directing their devotion from world missions to local causes are practicing anti-missionism. The excuse that a church must build a strong local base including all needed buildings and staff before doing much about world missions is just that-an excuse.

"The church which withdraws from world missions is dying evangelically, though the death may be slow. Evangelism and missions are two words for the same commitment. Concern for the lost at home cannot be genuine unless it is accompanied by concern for the lost of all the earth." There are many examples of the lack of vision on the part of God's people. All of us can recall some time in our own lives when v/e have lacked vision and people and opportunities for service have perished because of it. Some of our churches are still locked in on the past and are looking backward. They have no vision as to what God has out there before them where "The harvest is so great and the workers are so few." There are examples too of those who have been able to see that the harvest is ready and have come to know the thrill of being a worker in that harvest. Through our Lay Evangelism School last November there were some who shared for the first time what Christ means to them and have gone on to share at other times.

There are churches that have shown they have a greater vision by increasing their gifts to missions through the Cooperative Program and the Association. They have said that we cannot wait until we get our building debt paid off and everything we want for our church before we give more to missions. Their vision reaches beyond the fields aroung them to the whole world. God honors this kind vision and he will bless a church that has such vision.

God has blessed Southern Baptists through the vision some of our leaders had 50 years ago in establishing the Cooperative Program. Despite the economic uncertainty this year, the Cooperative Program and designated receipts from churches across the nation amount to more than $76.7 million dollars, an 8.44% increase. And this is only about one-tenth the amoung collected in offerings in 34,734 Southern Baptists churches.

God has blessed Southern Baptists by giving us pastors and leaders who have visions, who are concerned about winning the lost and about meeting the needs of people. God has given the churches in the Flat River Baptist Association such men as pastors to lead you, to help you have a greater concern for His work and His people.

This year I have seen churches build needed buildings because of the vision of pastors. I have seen programs provided to involve youth and minister to their needs.
I have seen work begun to share with those who are lonely and shut-in.

I have seen people become concerned about ministering to orphans and retarded people. I have seen programs begun to train teachers and to bring families together. Wainwright Report No. 19 REPORT OF THE BUTNER CHRISTIAN SOCIAL MINISTRY IN THE FLAT RIVER BAPTIST ASSOCIATION It is really exciting to be able to say to you, "Thank you for all that you are doing in the name of Jesus Christ." In addition to the faithful work being carried on in your local churches, you have reached out through the Cooperative Program and special mission opportunities and rendered a commendable ministry to people.

Among those to whom you have ministered are the approximate 1,100 patients at John Umstead Hospital; the 75 residents of the N.C. Rehabilitation Center for the Blind; the 125 young men at the Umstead Youth Center; the 85 patients at N.C.
Alcoholic Rehabilitation Center; the 113 youthful students at C.A. Dillon School; and the approximately 1,300 residents at the Murdoch Center, all located in the Butner complex. Add to these the many people who work in these institutions at Butner and you begin to sense something of the challenging ministry awaiting your decision to become more involved.

How grateful we are for the ministry being carried on by several of our churches and more than 300 volunteers who have given of themselves the past year rendering more than 1,500 hours of personal work and many dollars worth of clothing, camperships, reading materials, socials, and personal items to make life more enjoyable for God's special people in Butner institutions.

May God bless you for the blessings you have been to others. There is yet unmet needs that you and your church group(s) can help with. Please let me know if I can assist you in becoming involved in this ministry of love.

Respectfully submitted.

Respectfully submitted, Mark Stevens Report No. 23 REPORT OF N. C. BAPTIST CHILDREN'S HOMES Christian child care provided by the Baptist Children's Homes of North Carolina is a cooperative mission. It was through the life, teaching and ministry of Christ and under the leadership of the Holy Spirit that North Carolina Baptists launched their child care ministry 90 years ago. Today it is recognized as one of the outstanding ministries of its kind in the nation.

New and exciting developments are in progress throughout the state. During the past year emergency shelters were opened in Charlotte and Forest City to meet the needs of children requiring immediate assistance. Both Broyhill Home and Mills Home will have a new cottage for children constructed during the next year. Progress is being made to expand facilities at Odum Home. Increasing numbers of families are opening hearts and homes for the care of foster children. All of these indicate a living, vital and growing ministry to people.

At Kennedy Home during the past year, an exciting development has occurred in a special group-therapy program for a small number of boys. The boys who have completed this program have shown encouraging progress in learning to cope with their problems, so much so that there is a strong request from members of our staff to begin a similar program for girls in the fall of this year. Young, the department's first director, who died December 31. Full re -accreditation was given the School of Pastoral Care in December by the Association of Clinical Pastoral Education.

During the past year, Mr. Reid T. Holmes retired as president of the hospital. He had served in hospital administration for twenty-nine years.

BAPTIST HOMES FOR THE AGING During this year there have been more people who needed the aid of our Baptist Homes than ever before but particularly our older sick people. ; The needs of these aging is so overwhelming that we must strive harder to meet jthe needs of the approximately 21,000,000 Americans 65 years old and older.
! We are ministering to 230 of these aging at one time but for twenty-four years we [have helped hundreds at Resthaven, the Hayes Home, and the Nursing Care Unit in j Winston- Salem; and in the Albemarle, Hamilton, and Yanceyville Homes. By the grace of God, with our increased concern and continued giving of our tithes, we can expand our ministry to reach out and aid as many of these older people as possible in the future.

From the way things look we'll need greater financial support for these Homes for the aging than ever before.

This would enable us to have room for 300 older people instead of 250 as of now.

Also this home will mean that hundreds more will be served in this area.

We solicit your prayers that we will give as liberally as possible for the needs of these older people. Remember to help one of these in need will be helping Christ Himself. Let's not forget Him!

Godwin, Chairman, Ordination Committee Report No. 30 REPORT OF CAMP COMMITTEE World Missions Week, July 8-12, 1975, was an exciting adventure for the thirty-five campers and five chaperones from the Flat River Association. The place was the North Carolina Baptist Assembly at Southport.

Transportation to camp was on the Hester Baptist Church bus driven by the Rev.
Tom Lolley. The total camp cost included bus travel, lodging, and food.

There were twenty girls and fifteen boys who attended the camp. There were from the following churches: Amis Chapel, Hester, Island Creek, Mount Zion, Mountain^ Creek, Oxford and Providence. Chaperones were: Rev. and Mrs, Tom Lolley of the Hester Church; Mrs. Charles Bridges, Minister of Education/ Youth at the Hester Church; Miss Carol Dorman, 1975 summer youth worker at the Island Creek Baptist Church; and Rev. Bob Wainwright, Superintendent of Missins for Flat River.

The group stayed in one of the houses at the Assembly and shared chores for the week. Activities during each day included classes in the morning, recreation in the afternoon, and worship service at night. The day's activities ended with group devotions in the cottage.

This week proved to be one of inspiration, information, fellowship and fun.

Respectfully submitted, Robert Price, Chairman Camp Committee Report No. 31 REPORT ON THE CHRISTIAN ACTION LEAGUE The Director of the Christian Action League, and our new President, Bill Wallace, wish to express deepest appreciation to the many friends and supporters of the work of the League in the Flat River Baptist Association. Our program depends upon you for its success. You provide the money, you furnish the prayer support, and you furnish the "clout" which the League has in its efforts. With trustees and friends and supporters in every Association in the State, we have a broad base of support. As we do the work of God in North Carolina, we are so very conscious of our dependency upon Him and for the wonderful way in which you have supplied our needs.

The General Assembly has been in session this year. A bill was introduced by Representatives Dan Lilley of Kinston to close every beer joint in North Carolina at midnight. Under the present law these places can stay open until 2:30 in the morning from the last Sunday in April through the last Sunday in October. The bill failed to receive enough support in the House ABC Committee to pass A bill was introduced by Representative Jimmy Love of Sanford to strengthen the state-wide statute regulating the flow of pornography. This proposed change would have given the District Solicitor discretionary power in the matter of the adversary hearing, and would have defined hard-core pornography in the Public Nuisance Section of the Law. This bill also failed to get the nod of the House Judiciary One Committee. We will be back in a later session with both of these concerns.

We continue to need your support. The most dependable thing to do is to place the Christian Action League in the budget of your church for a definite amount each month. As you do this, we will continue to be able to serve.

The League will be promoting Alcoholism Awareness Sunday in January and Highway Safety Sunday in August. Your support of these two efforts will help to save the lives of multitudes of innocent people in North Carolina. God created human life and thereby made it sacred. We are to recognize the sacredness of human life and seek to preserve it for God in every way that is possible.

Respectfully submitted, D.P. Mcfarland, Executive Director Bill Wallace, President The Christian Action League Box 2126 Raleigh, N.C. 27602 Report No. 32 CHRISTIAN HIGHER EDUCATION REPORT TO ASSOCIATIONS Colleges Active in Mission Support Baptist colleges in North Carolina see themselves as active participants in the Christian mission to share the Gospel of Jesus Christ with all people everywhere. In addition to the important role of preparing future lay leaders for local churches, the colleges continue a strong emphasis upon ministerial and church-related vocational training. i In April of 1975, our seven colleges reported a total of 715 persons who were !
preparing for careers in church leadership. Most of these persons will complete their preparation by attending a seminary. Southern Baptist seminaries listed an enrolment of 303 graduates of North Carolina Baptist colleges in the spring of 1975. j The Foreign Mission Board has under appointment 152 of our graduates at present, I more than 6% of the total number of current missionary personnel. In addition, a significant number of Baptist college trained persons serve in strategic posts with the ( Home Mission Board, Baptist state conventions and associational missions throughout the Southern Baptist Convention. Baptist col-lege students receive « excellent experience and training for church and missionary service through Baptist Youth Corps. Approximately 100 Baptist college students were assigned to churches for the summer of 1975. The colleges furnish one-half of the Youth Corps worker's salary, and thus enjoy a vital partnership with churches and students in summer mission work. Cooperative Program Vital to Colleges With the exception of what students pay, the Cooperative Program continues to be the largest single source of annual income for our colleges. In these days of financial uncertainty, the colleges are grateful for the level of support which North Carolina Baptists have maintained. During 1975, the anticipated Cooperative Program budget for the colleges will provide: Campbell College, $416,066; Chowan College, $263,333; Gardner- Webb College, $^329,166; Mars Hill College, $360,776; Meredith j College, $360,766; Wake Forest University, $639,900; and Wingate College, $263,333. !

It is accurate to say that the ministry of North Carolina Baptist colleges, as we i know it today, is the direct result of Cooperative Program support.

Baptist College Day Another way the churches help to relieve the colleges' financial burden is through the annual offering on Father's Day. Designated for student aid, this offering gives much-needed assistance to many students from Baptist churches, making an education on a Christian college campus possible.

The Need to Enroll Baptist Students Pastors and other church leaders can assist their young people and our colleges by encouraging attendance at a Baptist college. Information is available in brochure form, free of charge, from the Council on Christian Higher Education of the Baptist State Convention in Raleigh. Each of the colleges pledges to North Carolina Baptist young people that no qualified student will be turned away for purely financial reasons. Through grants, loans and work scholarships, an education is available for anyone who seriously seeks it.

A Pledge and a Plea N.C. Baptist colleges are committed to Christian values. Your Baptist colleges sincerely solicit the supportive prayers of N.C. Baptist churches, that we will be aware of and receiptive to Divine guidance.

Respectfully submitted, Ray B. Morgan Report No. 33 1975 BIBLICAL RECORDER REPORT TO THE ASSOCIATIONS Baptist State papers claimed more attention than usual at the 1975 meeting of the Southern Baptist Convention in Miami Beach this year. In the celebration of the 50th Anniversary of the Cooperative Program, it was revealed that they had played a tremendous part in the growth of our denomination. Louie D. Newton, denomination statesman and former editor of Georgia's Christian Index, introduced all the 33 editors of state papers and made a comparison of circulation of each in 1975 with that of 1925 when the Cooperative Program was born. Many were not in existence at that time, however, but this in itself reveals something of Southern Baptist growth.

In giving a special committee report on state papers, H. Franklin Paschall, pastor of First Church, Nashville, Tenn., reported that present circulation of IH million and recommended that the convention reaffirm its goal of getting a state paper into two million Baptist homes by 1980. The committee recommended a vigorous campaign in all states to help accomplish this, and also recommended that state papers be incorporated in all local church budgets.

The BIBLICAL RECORDER is one of those 33 state papers, and one of the oldest, and we hope, one of the best. In spite of continued problems for religious publications because of inflation, and skyrocketting postage costs, the circulation of the RECORDER remains above 112,000. Congregations including it in their budgets for all their families are the ones which have an informed membership. It is our hope that North Carolina Churches will take seriously the recommendation reaffirmed at the Southern Baptist Convention that state papers be incorporated in local church budgets for all their member families. Already more than 1,000 churches in North Carolina are doing this, and more congregations do so each year. Pastors and deacons should initiate such action, and it should be supported by all the organizations in the church.

The BIBLICAL RECORDER has covered many sensitive issues this past year.
Words of appreciation have come from Baptist people all across the state, for they are aware that "an informed people is the bulwark of Baptist democracy." The RECORDER continues to operate on a limited budget, and with an altogether too small staff. Local churches can help us by understanding these limitations, and by renewing their subscription plans promptly.

Ray K. Hodge, pastor of First Church, Kinston, says: "One of the easiest tasks I have had as a pastor is to lead churches to include and keep the denominational paper in the church budget. It is such a bargain that trying to save a few dollars (by not subscribing to it for our families) is too costly. It would be like saving the expense of regular visits to the dentist, only to pay more in various ways later." His advice is well taken.

Respectfully submitted, J. MARSE GRANT, Editor BIBLICAL RECORDER Report No. 34 THE NORTH CAROLINA BAPTIST ^ FOUNDATION, INC. REPORT I As revealed through a Sunday School lesson during past year, a good foundation of discipline and training in Spiritual matters is necessary for a young life to develop in a way to withstand the pressures of life during periods of stress and strain. It is almost trite to refer to the need of a good base or foundation for any activity or project contemplated in this life. Not in anyway is there a difference for a need of the Foundation, as thought of in terms of the Baptist Foundation, when we consider the future potentialities and needs to sustain and expand mission efforts and services to bring mankind into the influence of the saving grace of God's Son, Jesus Christ.

Chartered in 1919 by the Baptist State Convention of North Carolina, The North Carolina Baptist Foundation, Inc. has sought to assist individuals throughout the state in their estate planning, providing ways to support Baptist institutions and mission work on a permanent basis. Property and funds placed with the Baptist Foundation are invested by a convention elected board of directors with the income paid annually to the institution or mission cause designated by the donor.

Individual assistance is provided Baptists throughout the state in the establishment of trusts that not only benefit the donor but also support the Christian ministries of our denomination in a lasting, perpetual way.

During the past two years, $330,070.41 has been given to the N.C. Baptist Foundation, Inc., in the way of annunity trusts. In this type trust, the donors reserve the right to the income from the investments for their lifetime and then the income will be paid to the Baptist cause specified in the original trust agreement. The laws of our land provide to the donor certain tax advantages for the gift portion of these transactions.

Wills still account for a major portion of the funds held in trust by the Foundation.
One such gift to benefit mission work from this year forward, came in the form of a farm and other assets given by the deceased. It was determined by the tax records found in this estate, that this person will give annually three times as much for missions through the income from this trust fund, as she was ever able to earn in total wages during any year of her lifetime. So what she could not do for our Lord's work here on earth in her lifetime, she does after her life here among us has ended.

New gifts to be held in trust by the N.C. Baptist amounted to $635,079.57 during 1974-75 fiscal year. Added to the market value of assets held from previous years brings the total value to $2,331,559.67 as of June 30, 1975. Income from all assets held by the Foundation which have been or will be distributed to designated in the near future amounted to $76,257.82 for the past 12 months.

For services offered by the Foundation to individuals or churches, please contact Mr. Edwin S. Coates, Executive Secretary, N.C. Baptist Foundation, Inc., P.O. Box 26508, Raleigh, N.C. 27611 (Tel No. 919-833-1605). All inquiries and assistance are considered confidential and personal matters.

Report No. 35 PRESENTATION OF PUBLIC SERVICE AWARD The purpose of this award is to express appreciation to Station WCBQ and to call attention to the work of the Radio and Television Commission of the Southern Baptist Convention, which you help support through the Cooperative Program.

Dr. Paul M. Stevens, who is president of the Commission reports 37 different programs are produced and distributed each week to 3,721 radio and television stations in all 50 states and 40 foreign countries. The public service time given by these stations during the past year is worth over nine (9) million dollars.

The true ministry of the Radio and Television Commission of the Southern Baptist Convention is evangelism. And evangelism is reaching out with the Word of God to provide hope and salvation to the millions of people who exist without Jesus Christ.
God has provided a unique opportunity through radio and television for taking the Good News of our Lord to people of every race and nation.


It is broadcast at 12:05 each Sunday at noon.

Soulseachers is the newest program produced by the Commission to be aired locally. It is a half-hour program with a rhythmn and blues format aimed at the black audience. It is broadcast at 7:00 each Friday evening.


In addition to these program WCBQ makes spot announcements about Revival meetings and other special services for the churches and the association.

We are grateful for the cooperation we have received and in recognition of the service that has been rendered, I want to present this award to the Station Manager, Mr. Al Woodlief.
